Tirucchanda Viruttam Vyakhya

Original text: Tirucchanda Viruttam of Tirumazhisai Āḻvār
Commentary: Uttamūr T. Vīrarāghavācārya
Source language: Tamil–Sanskrit Maṇipravāḷa
Translation and digital edition: Govinda
Śrīvaiṣṇava sampradāya: Parakāla Matham

Editorial Notice

This English translation was prepared from a transcribed source text. Obvious transcription and OCR artifacts have been corrected where reasonably identifiable.

The translation aims to preserve the terminology, doctrinal distinctions, quotations, grammatical explanations, and alternative interpretations of the original commentary as faithfully as possible.

This is not a critical edition of the original text. Readers using the translation for academic or textual research should consult the published source editions where possible.

Prepared and maintained by Govinda, of the Parakāla Matham Śrīvaiṣṇava tradition.

Śrīḥ

Refuge at the sacred feet of the Āḻvārs, Emberumānār, and Deśika.

Tirucchanda Viruttam, graciously revealed by Tirumaḻisai Āḻvār in the Iyar-pā division.

The Prabandha Rakṣā composed by Śrī Uttamūr T. Vīrarāghavācārya, disciple at the sacred feet of Śrī Raṅgarāmānuja Mahādeśika (Śrī Kōḻiyāḷam Svāmi), the Paramahaṃsa-Parivrājaka and Ubhaya-Vedāntācārya.

Avatārikā - Introduction

Opening Sanskrit Verse

Original

प्रणत हयमुख श्रीरङ्गसौमित्रियोगि- प्रवरमुखगुरु श्रीपादपद्मः प्रभुः स्याम् ।

परमत परिमर्दि प्राज्य धी भक्तिसार प्रकटित शुभवृत्त प्रख्य सूक्त्यर्थवित्तौ ॥

IAST

praṇata-hayamukha-śrīraṅga-saumitriyogi-pravara-mukha-guru-śrīpāda-padmaḥ prabhuḥ syām |

paramata-parimardi-prājya-dhī-bhaktisāra-prakaṭita-śubhavṛtta-prakhya-sūkty-arthavittau ||

English Translation

May the Lord whose auspicious lotus feet belong to the lineage of the foremost teachers headed by Hayamukha, Śrīraṅga, and Saumitriyogi be my support in understanding the meaning of the celebrated sacred utterance - the auspicious Viruttam revealed by Bhaktisāra, the possessor of a vast intellect that refuted opposing doctrines.

English Translation of the Introduction

Tirucchanda Viruttam is the fifth Divya Prabandham in the Mudal Āyiram (First Thousand). The Āḻvār who graciously revealed both this work and, before it, the Nāṉmukan Tiruvantāti is Śrī Bhaktisāra. The latter prabandham has been included in the Third Thousand. Because he incarnated in the divya-deśa called Tirumaḻisai, he received the Tamil sacred name Tirumaḻisaippirān. The Sanskrit name of Tirumaḻisai is Mahīsāra; that this is an etymological or causal name will be explained below in connection with his second taniyan.

He appeared in the lineage of the brāhmaṇa known as Bhārgava, as an aṃśa of the Lord’s divine discus, and was subsequently brought up in the house of Tiruvāḷan, a man of the Vellāḷa community who worked with bamboo. He grew up taking only cow’s milk. A Vellāḷa who, with devotion, brought him fresh cow’s milk every day and thereby served him was one day given the remainder of the milk sanctified by the Āḻvār’s consumption. By partaking of it, that man and his wife obtained the blessing of a son. Tradition says that the child thus born was Kaṇikaṇṇan, who later rendered service to the Āḻvār.

Having grown in this way and reached a suitable age, the Āḻvār, for the sake of conferring grace upon the world (lokānugrahārtha), entered into and examined all the religious systems current in the world in order to ascertain the Supreme Reality. After having properly systematized Śaivism, he became clear regarding the supreme truth that the Supreme Person is none other than the Lord of Śrī (Śriyaḥpati). He then remained for a long time absorbed in yoga directed toward Him. Because of the extraordinary abundance of his incomparable devotion he acquired the name Bhaktisāra. These two prabandhams are the works he graciously revealed when such devotion, knowledge, and the renunciation appropriate to them manifested of themselves and his wondrous experience overflowed.

In the Nāṉmukan Tiruvantāti, he rejects other doctrines and establishes, as the final determination concerning the true Supreme Deity, that Nārāyaṇa is the cause even of Brahmā, Śaṅkara, and the rest. This is made clear by Śrī Deśika’s verse: “O exalted one of Maḻisai who came in the month of Tai! Having understood the many other religions, you declared that there is no deity other than Māyōn; the ninety-six songs of Nāṉmukan are the rich meaning of the Veda.” In the same place, the second work, Tirucchanda Viruttam, is described as “the songs of Tirucchanda Viruttam, overflowing with truth.”

The purport is this. The Āḻvār does not merely teach that Nārāyaṇa alone is the Supreme Reality. With delight and devotion he repeatedly praises the realities in the world as both objects and the words denoting them; he teaches that Nārāyaṇa is the inner controller (antaryāmin) of all of them; he describes the various forms of the Lord as para, vyūha, vibhava, hārda (the indwelling form in the heart), and arcā; and, in various places, His wondrous divine sports. He directly points out the truth with statements such as “this is the truth proclaimed by the Vedic scripture.” He speaks of mantras such as the Praṇava and the Aṣṭākṣara, of attaining liberation by devotion (bhakti) or surrender (prapatti) to Him, and of how the Lord, who in the beginning granted him unclouded knowledge, has now come forward to bestow endless supreme bliss upon him.

Because, near the end of Tirucchanda Viruttam, the Āḻvār speaks of the Lord’s grace that enabled him to attain the supreme goal and related matters, this becomes his second prabandham. That the work may especially concern Lord Raṅganātha can be examined from addresses such as “Araṅganē!” He sings of many divya-deśas and instructs others always to render service only to the Lord of Śrī. Moreover, in Nāṉmukan Tiruvantāti he has revealed that he himself has been in precisely that state from birth - indeed from the time he was in the womb: “Never have I known forgetfulness. Keeping the Tall Lord in my heart, standing and sitting, I have ever been the servant of Śrīdhara, on whose chest Śrī eternally dwells; this has protected me from the day I was in the womb.” The greatness of his divine utterance may be understood from his own verse: “Sowing good Tamil as seed, You caused my heart to grow; entering it, You made it into the language I had learned” (Nāṉmukan Tiruvantāti 81).

Tradition also says that, after a long time, this exalted Āḻvār became a disciple of Pēyāḻvār, who had incarnated before him. The Ācārya’s verse calling him “the radiance that came to Maḻisai” likewise establishes that he is the incarnation of the luminous divine discus.

Śubham.

Taniyan Commentary

Taniyan 1 - by Tirukkacci Nampi

This is a taniyan graciously composed by Tirukkacci Nampi concerning this Divya Prabandham.

Tamil Mūlam

தருச்சந்தப்பொழில் தழுவு தாரணியின் துயர் தீரத்
திருச்சந்தவிருத்தஞ்செய் திருமழிசைப்பரன் வருமூர்
கருச்சந்தும் காரகிலும் கமழ்கோங்கு மணநாறும்
திருச்சக்தத்துடன் மருவு திருமழிசை வளம்பதியே.

IAST Transliteration

taruccantappoḻil taḻuvu tāraṇiyiṉ tuyar tīrat
tiruccantaviruttañcey tirumaḻisaipparaṉ varumūr
karuccantum kārakilum kamaḻkōṅku maṇanāṟum
tiruccaktattuṭaṉ maruvu tirumaḻisai vaḷampatiyē.

English Translation of the Taniyan

To remove the sorrow of the earth embraced by beautiful groves of trees, the exalted Tirumaḻisai Āḻvār came to the prosperous sacred town of Tirumaḻisai and composed Tirucchanda Viruttam. There, great sandal trees, dark eaglewood, and fragrant kōṅku trees spread their perfume, and the place is lovingly united with auspicious Śrī.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. taru-candam - possessing the beauty of trees; poḻil taḻuvu - embraced by groves; tāraṇiyiṉ tuyar tīra - so that the sorrow of the earth may depart; Tirucchanda Viruttam sey - he who graciously composed the Divya Prabandham called Tirucchanda Viruttam; Tirumaḻisai paran - Tirumaḻisai Āḻvār; varum ūr - the place where he incarnated; karu-candum - great sandalwood trees; kār-akilum - dark eaglewood trees; kamaḻ-kōṅkum - fragrant kōṅku trees; maṇam nāṟum - a place where, through the richness of the earth, fragrance spreads; tiru-candattuṭaṉ maruvu - lovingly united with Periya Pirāṭṭiyār, Śrī; Tirumaḻisai - the place called Tirumaḻisai; vaḷam pati - the prosperous sacred town.

On “embraced by groves beautiful with trees”: as the scripture says, “As the fragrance of a tree in full blossom is carried from afar,” the earth had a great sorrow: although so many flower gardens surrounded it and their fragrance spread far, no rare work had arisen from it whose excellent fragrance of knowledge would similarly travel afar. That sorrow was removed by Tirucchanda Viruttam, graciously revealed by the Āḻvār who incarnated at Tirumaḻisai, a place that lends fragrance even to fragrant trees such as the great sandalwood.

Alternatively, taru can denote the cosmic aśvattha tree, the universe extending from the world of Brahmā down to the lower worlds, in accordance with the Bhagavad Gītā’s statement: “They speak of the imperishable aśvattha with roots above and branches below.” Poḻil may mean “world” (cf. Tiruvāymoḻi 1.4.5). Thus taru-canda-poḻil can mean the universe described in Gītā 15 in the form of a tree. All worlds have been constructed for the experience of the results of dharma and adharma performed on earth; hence the earth is, as it were, embraced by the worlds. Such an earth suffered because there was no treatise by which everyone, in accordance with Tirumāl’s intention, could know reality and attain liberation. That sorrow ended after the appearance of Tirucchanda Viruttam.

On “fragrant kōṅku”: some connect the words so as to mean “Tirumaḻisai in which sandalwood and eaglewood are fragrant.” Just as karu and kār qualify trees, kamaḻ can also qualify the tree. Fragrance is naturally associated with kōṅku. But since the word kōṅku is used for two kinds of trees, one fragrant and another without fragrance, the qualifier “fragrant” may have been added to exclude the scentless kind.

On “united with auspicious Śrī”: Tirumaḻisai was a place filled with qualified persons of the kind described by the saying, “I dwell always in one who is constant in dharma, greatly intelligent, devoted to Brahman, truthful, humble, and charitable.” Those great persons themselves, perhaps, are metaphorically represented as the sandalwood and other trees. Because such eminent men as Bhārgava dwelt there, the place received an especially abundant favor from Śrī.

The elders also relate that when Tirumaḻisai Āḻvār was born he had no form of an infant but appeared merely as a lump of flesh, and therefore was cast away in disgust in a bamboo thicket. In time it assumed the form of a child and cried. Then Pirāṭṭi, together with Lord Jagannātha who had taken His abode in a nearby temple, came there, fed the child with her breast milk, and protected him. This tradition too may be intended in the phrase “united with Śrī.”

Taniyan 2 - by Tirukkacci Nampi

Thinking that the greatness of the sacred place itself was the reason this Āḻvār came to be known by the name Tirumaḻisai Āḻvār, Tirukkacci Nampi graciously composed this second taniyan to proclaim the greatness of that place.

Tamil Mūlam

உலகும் மழிசையும் உள்ளுணர்ந்து தம்மில்
புலவர் புகழ்க்கோலால் தூக்க - உலகுதன்னை
வைத்தெடுத்த பக்கத்தும் மாநீர் மழிசையே
வைத்தெடுத்தபக்கம் வலிது.

IAST Transliteration

ulakum maḻisaiyum uḷḷuṇarntu tammil
pulavar pukaḻkkōlāl tūkka - ulakutaṉṉai
vaitteṭutta pakkattum mānīr maḻisaiyē
vaitteṭuttapakkam valitu.

English Translation of the Taniyan

The wise, inwardly discerning the relative worth of the world and Maḻisai, weighed them on the balance of fame. When the whole world was placed on one pan and great, water-rich Maḻisai on the other, the pan holding Maḻisai alone proved heavier.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. pulavar - the learned, beginning with Brahmā; Maḻisaiyum - the town called Tirumaḻisai; ulakum - the other worlds; tammil uḷ uṇarntu - examining and clearly discerning within themselves, in order to make the matter perfectly clear; pukaḻ-kōlāl - with a balance that displays comparative fame; tūkka - placing the world on one side and Maḻisai on the other and having Viśvakarmā raise the balance to weigh them; ulaku taṉṉai - all the worlds except Maḻisai; vaittu eṭutta pakkattum - compared with the pan in which those worlds were placed and lifted; mā-nīr Maḻisai - Tirumaḻisai endowed with abundant waters; vaittu eṭutta pakkamē - the very pan in which it was placed and lifted; valitu - was weightier and therefore sank lower.

The words “inwardly discerning” indicate that Brahmā, who created the world, knew that within the world Maḻisai possessed a special superiority. Just as a balance was said to have been used to determine whether the Vedas or the Mahābhārata was greater, whereupon the greatness of the Bhārata was demonstrated, tradition says that Brahmā placed Tirumaḻisai on a balance and thus showed its superiority to Vasiṣṭha and the other sages. Hence, they say, the place has the Sanskrit name Mahīsāra, “the essence of the earth.”

The name Tirucchanda Viruttam is an explanatory or causal name. It can mean “the beautiful viruttam endowed with excellence.” In Tamil, viruttam is classified into forms such as kaliviruttam and āciriyaviruttam. Since this viruttam is exceptionally sweet to the ear, it was called chanda-viruttam, a metrical or melodious viruttam. Since all the greatness of the Lord is taught in it, the honorific tiru was prefixed to indicate that excellence. Alternatively, tiru means “exalted,” chandam means the Lord’s saṅkalpa or divine will, and viruttam means divine sport. Since it speaks of the exalted divine will and sport of the Lord, it is called Tirucchanda Viruttam.

Śubham.

Tirucchanda Viruttam - Commentary

Pāsuram 1

Tamil Mūlam

பூநிலாயவைந்துமாய்ப் புனற்கண்நின்றநான்குமாய்த்
தீநிலாயமூன்றுமாய்ச் சிறந்தகாலிரண்டுமாய்
மீநிலாயதொன்றுமாகி வேறுவேறுதன்மையாய்
நீநிலாயவண்ணம்நின்னை யார்நினைக்கவல்லரே.

IAST Transliteration

pūnilāyavaintumāyp puṉaṟkaṇniṉṟa nāṉkumāyt
tīnilāyamūṉṟumāyc ciṟantakāliraṇṭumāy
mīnilāyatoṉṟumāki vēṟuvēṟutaṉmaiyāy
nīnilāyavaṇṇamniṉṉai yārniṉaikkavallarē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Becoming the five qualities that naturally abide in earth; becoming the four that abide in water; becoming the three that abide in fire; becoming the two belonging to the excellent wind; becoming the one that abides in the upper expanse; and yet existing with a nature distinct from each of these - who indeed is capable of conceiving You as You thus abide?

English Translation of the Commentary

The Supreme Person whose supremacy was determined in Nāṉmukan Tiruvantāti shows affection toward the Āḻvār and causes His essential nature, forms, and other modes to become manifest. Seeing them, the Āḻvār is overwhelmed, as though dancing in ecstasy, and graciously gives forth this prabandham. For one plunged into divine experience, however many times the Lord’s qualities and deeds are experienced they appear wondrous, as though something entirely new were being experienced. Thus one must sweetly repeat again and again even what has already been said. When the same object is experienced in association with different objects, it becomes the cause of many distinct flavors of experience. Such experience may be said to be especially abundant in this Āḻvār, even in comparison with the other Āḻvārs.

Marveling at the Lord’s sovereignty in the līlā-vibhūti, the greatness of the bhoga-vibhūti or transcendent realm of enjoyment, the varieties of His divine auspicious form, the excellences of His qualities, and the many kinds of His wondrous activities, the Āḻvār begins by experiencing the principal doctrine of the relation of body and self (śarīra-ātma-bhāva) between the universe and the Lord. He goes further: not only are other substances related to Him, but the qualities residing in those substances themselves possess existence only in dependence upon Him. Hence even the words that denote those qualities can, through their supports, denote Him. He teaches this clearly at the outset, both from direct realization and from authorities such as the Gītā statement, “I am the taste in the waters, O son of Kuntī, and the pure fragrance in the earth.” The very character of this opening reveals the firmness of his determination regarding reality.

Word-by-word gloss. “Abiding in earth” means naturally present in earth; “becoming the five” means becoming the five qualities beginning with smell. “In water, becoming the four” means the four qualities beginning with taste that abide there. “In fire, becoming the three” means the three qualities beginning with form or color. “The excellent wind” is wind distinguished by giving life to living creatures; “becoming the two” means touch and sound. “That which abides above” refers to ether; “becoming the one” means sound. “Being of different, different natures” means being distinct from them and remaining untouched by the defects of sentient and insentient entities. “The manner in which You abide” means Your actual mode of existence. “Who can think of You?” means: other than true Vedāntins, who has the capacity to establish You as You really are? No one.

On pū nilāya: bhū is a Sanskrit word meaning earth. Nilāya is “having pervaded” or “abiding.” In puṉal-kaṇ, kaṇ is the suffix of the seventh case, “in water.”

From prakṛti arises mahat, and from it ahaṅkāra. From the sāttvika aspect of ahaṅkāra arise the mind and the eleven senses, while from its tāmasa aspect arise the elements. The sequence is: tāmasa-ahaṅkāra, the subtle element of sound (śabda-tanmātra), ether (ākāśa), the subtle element of touch, air, the subtle element of form, fire or tejas, the subtle element of taste, water, the subtle element of smell, and earth. The subtle elements have no special function other than producing the succeeding gross elements. The five gross elements are the causes of the physical universe. Thus, without separately counting the intervening tanmātras, the Taittirīya Upaniṣad concisely describes creation as ether giving rise to air, air to fire, fire to water, and water to earth.

Because ether is produced from the subtle element of sound, sound alone is its quality. Each gross element possesses the quality corresponding to the tanmātra from which it arose and, successively, also the qualities of the preceding element. Air therefore has two qualities, touch and sound. Fire adds form and has three. Water adds taste and has four. Earth has those four together with a fifth, smell. Since all these qualities are in every way dependent upon the Lord, the verse speaks of Him as being these very qualities.

The elements are substances, and therefore they are bodies (śarīras) of the Lord. A word denoting a body naturally also extends to its indwelling self. On that basis one may say that He “is” the elements. The qualities just enumerated, however, are not substances and therefore cannot themselves be bodies of Him. Nevertheless, there is a rule that a word denoting something inseparably connected with another can also indicate the latter, its support. Thus the word “blue,” while directly denoting blueness, can denote the thing possessing that blue color; and words such as “cow” and “man,” though denoting their respective universal classes, are used of the bodies that are the loci of those classes. In the same manner words denoting sound, touch, form, taste, and smell can finally indicate the Supreme Self.

One should not therefore think that the Lord is the immediate substrate of those qualities. Their immediate substrates are the respective great elements. The Supreme Self is the substrate and supporter of those elements. Yet the elements themselves possess their capacity to support their qualities only because the Supreme Self supports the elements; without Him that capacity would not arise. Hence, though not their immediate substrate, the Supreme Self is their supporting ground through the mediation of the elements, and words denoting those qualities can accordingly extend to Him.

A further point requires attention. The rule that a word denoting an inseparable attribute can also denote the substance qualified by it does not operate in the same way for certain expressions. Such words are called niṣkarṣaka-śabdas, words that isolate and precisely specify their immediate referent. Terms such as sound, touch, form, taste, smell, body, and so on belong to this class. Thus, for example, the word “sound” cannot simply be used to mean ether. How then can these words denote the Supreme Self? There is nevertheless no word whose extension to the Lord is absolutely barred. A word such as “blue” can denote blueness, the body possessing it, the individual self supporting that body, and finally the Supreme Self supporting that individual self. The word “form” does not have power to denote all the intermediate supports in that manner, but it can denote the color or form itself and, by an ultimate relational extension, the Supreme Self who is the final support of it. The Gītā’s statement “I am the taste in the waters” and similar declarations teach precisely this. These matters become intelligible only to one with at least some familiarity with the Bhāṣya, the Tātparya-candrikā, and related works.

Thus the Āḻvār firmly establishes this precise Viśiṣṭādvaita conclusion. While declaring the Lord to be these qualities in the stated sense, he adds “who can think of You?” to show how difficult it is to acquire knowledge of such a subtle distinction.

On mī nilāyatu: the elders take mī to be a modified form of mītu, “above.” It may also simply be accepted as a word for ether.

On “being of different, different natures”: the insentient entities such as the elements are differentiated into distinct classes corresponding to the different qualities whose supports they are; sentient substances are in turn different from those insentient things. By beginning with earth, the last member in the order of creation, and then proceeding upward, the verse distinguishes the higher insentients and then the sentient beings above them.

Alternatively, the expression means that although He exists as all these qualities in the mediated sense already explained, He Himself is also one who does not possess these material qualities directly. The Lord supports them through the elements and thus may be spoken of as them, but He is not directly characterized by them. Hence the Kaṭha Upaniṣad says: “That which is soundless, touchless, formless, undecaying, likewise tasteless, eternal and without smell, beginningless and endless, beyond the great principle and steadfast - having realized That, one is freed from the mouth of death.” Prakṛti, although without sound and the other manifest qualities in its subtle condition, possesses them in the gross state. The Lord, by contrast, is eternally free from material sound, touch, and the rest. This is why the text teaches Him as “eternally soundless” and “eternally touchless.” The same principle is to be seen in the Akṣara-vidyā and similar passages.

Again, taking seriously the word “nature” in “different, different natures,” it is also appropriate to understand the phrase as including all the other properties of the elements besides the principal qualities just mentioned - hardness, fluidity, unctuousness or cohesion, the capacity to illuminate other things, motion, and the like. When the Lord is described as these qualities, it can be understood without separately stating that He is also the substances that support them.

Another explanation of the expression is: He is the Self of the mutually distinct entities such as gods and others, even though the individual selves, as selves, are of one common essential nature. Some parse vaṇṇa ninnai as “Your manner and You.” The sense is that, in a world where it is itself rare to find one who truly thinks upon You, it is still more difficult to find one who can contemplate the manner in which You exist in all these ways.

Buddhists accept only qualities such as sound but do not independently accept substances such as earth. Logicians admit sound as belonging only to ether. Many deny a Lord altogether. Some who admit Him make Him devoid of qualities and deny even His cosmic manifestation. Others accept those but do not accept the relation of apṛthak-siddhi, inseparable dependence, between Him and them. Even among those who admit that relation, many do not know the supreme secret that words denoting these entities can, through them, also denote Him. The Vedāntic method is able to say “Brahman is all” while clearly maintaining that Brahman does not literally transform into the world: Brahman is one reality; the elements and sentient beings are distinct realities; their material qualities do not directly inhere in Brahman. This ancient Vedāntic procedure becomes especially clear in Sanskrit through the divine works of Bhagavad Rāmānuja, who illuminated the old Vedānta. The Tamil Vedas that underlie those works are pāsurams such as this one.

Pāsuram 2

Tamil Mūlam

ஆறுமாறுமாறுமா யொரைந்துமைந்து மைந்துமாய்
ஏறுசீரிரண்டுமூன்று மேழுமாறுமெட்டுமாய்
வேறுவேறு ஞானமாகி மெய்யினோடுபொய்யுமாய்
ஊறொடோசையாயவைந்து மாயவாயமாயனே.

IAST Transliteration

āṟumāṟumāṟumāy oraintumaintu maintumāy
ēṟucīriraṇṭumūṉṟum ēḻumāṟumeṭṭumāy
vēṟuvēṟu ñāṉamāki meyyiṉōṭupoyyumāy
ūṟoṭōcaiyāyavaintu māyavāyamāyaṉē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Becoming one six, another six, and another six; becoming one five, another five, and another five; becoming the exalted two, the three, the seven, the six, and the eight; becoming many different forms of knowledge, both true and false; becoming the five beginning with touch and sound - O cowherd, O wondrous Lord!

English Translation of the Commentary

Among the three matters - reality (tattva), the means (hita), and the supreme goal (puruṣārtha) - the first pāsuram declared that it is difficult to know Bhagavān as the Reality. In the second, the Āḻvār says that it is difficult to know Him as the means. In the third he will reveal the difficulty of knowing Him as the supreme goal.

Word-by-word gloss. “One six, six, six” means several distinct sets of six. “One five, five, five” similarly means several distinct sets of five. “The exalted two” means two eminent means; likewise the three, seven, six, and eight are further sets connected with the means. “Becoming different kinds of knowledge” means other forms of knowledge besides those already stated. “Together with truth” means as realities; “becoming falsehood” means also as what is non-real or non-literal. “Becoming the five that are touch and sound” means the group of five objects that includes touch and sound. “O cowherd! O wondrous one!” addresses the amazing Person who became the cowherd Kṛṣṇa. The implied continuation is, “Who can truly think of You?”

The expression or aintu is to be divided as “one five.” The hitas may be disciplines to be performed, forms of knowledge, and the scriptures that are their sources. Since this Āḻvār had penetrated all the śāstras, it is extremely difficult to determine with certainty every set he intended here. According to the knowledge of each interpreter, many different groups of six, five, and the other numbers can present themselves. We shall report the explanations given by the elders and first offer another explanation of our own. Because the verse admits many possible senses, the initial word-by-word gloss has deliberately been kept general.

One first interpretation is as follows. The first six are the six duties assigned to brāhmaṇas: sacrificing for oneself (yajana), officiating for another (yājana), Vedic study (adhyayana), teaching the Veda (adhyāpana), giving gifts, and receiving gifts. The second six are the duties of kṣatriyas: sacrifice, Vedic study, giving, receiving revenue or tribute from subjects, governing the kingdom, and fighting righteous wars. The third six are those assigned to vaiśyas: sacrifice, Vedic study, giving, increasing wealth through trade and lending, agriculture, and tending cattle.

The first five are the regular rites such as those enumerated in the Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad: aupāsana, agnihotra, the new- and full-moon sacrifices (darśa-pūrṇamāsa), the seasonal cāturmāsya rites, and āgrayaṇa. The second five are rites performed with one’s food: vaiśvadeva, the sacrifice to the gods, the sacrifice to the ancestors, the sacrifice to beings, and the sacrifice to human guests. The third five are the five daily modes of worship: abhigamana, upādāna, ijyā, svādhyāya, and yoga.

“The exalted two” are karma-yoga and jñāna-yoga, the principal preliminaries to bhakti-yoga. The three are the firm determinations of exclusive servitude (ananya-śeṣatva), exclusive reliance upon the Lord as means (ananyopāyatva), and exclusive acceptance of Him as the goal (ananya-prayojanatva). The seven are the seven disciplines beginning with viveka and vimoka that are taught at the beginning of the Śrībhāṣya’s discussion: discrimination or purity of food and conduct, freedom from attachment, repeated practice, performance of prescribed works, cultivation of auspicious virtues, freedom from dejection, and freedom from excessive elation. The six are prapatti understood as six-limbed surrender, and the eight are bhakti understood through the eight limbs of yoga. The fact that prapatti is mentioned before bhakti may suggest that, like other auxiliaries, surrender too can function as an aid in relation to bhakti-yoga.

“Different kinds of knowledge” may then mean the upāsanās that are auxiliaries of bhakti-yoga, other meditations performed for desired results, meditations involving a superimposed vision of Brahman (brahma-dṛṣṭi), and other true contemplations and dṛṣṭi-vidyās. “True and false” can refer to the fact that some meditations contemplate a thing exactly as it is and are therefore literally true, whereas in certain dṛṣṭi-upāsanās one thing is deliberately contemplated as another and the identification is therefore non-literal. Alternatively, “true and false” can mean the means taught by authoritative śāstra on the one hand and the alleged means invented by non-Vedic thinkers and distorted interpreters on the other.

On “the five, touch together with sound”: touch can be divided into five kinds - hot, cold, neither hot nor cold, soft, and hard. Likewise sound, represented by the Sāman, has five parts: hiṅkāra, prastāva, udgītha, pratihāra, and nidhana. Another possibility is that the sounds that are the basis of all knowledge are called five because, in our system, sound is admitted not only in ether but also in the four tangible elements earth, water, fire, and air. Since there are thus five material causes in which sound occurs, the phrase “the five consisting of touch and sound” can be understood as indicating fivefold sound as well.

“O cowherd, O wondrous one!” The Lord is the ruler of all and the marvelous Being who brings everything about exactly as He wills, yet He assumed the human class and became a cowherd. That appearance is, as it were, “false” in the sense of not revealing His full divine status. Thus the juxtaposition “cowherd and wondrous one” can illustrate how some scripturally prescribed modes are literally true while other scriptural contemplations employ non-literal identification.

There is another broad interpretation. The words “becoming different knowledges, true and false” may indicate the many systems of doctrine, some true and some false, spoken of in the Mahābhārata: “Know these knowledges, O royal sage, to be the various doctrines.” On that understanding, the elders have distributed the numbered groups among the sources by which these systems are known.

The first six are the six Vedic philosophical systems: Sāṅkhya, Yoga, Nyāya, Vaiśeṣika, the Jaiminīya or Pūrva-Mīmāṃsā system, and the Vyāsa or Vedānta system. The second six are the non-Vedic systems: Cārvāka, Jaina, and the four Buddhist schools - Sautrāntika, Vaibhāṣika, Yogācāra, and Mādhyamika. The third six are the Vedāṅgas: phonetics (śikṣā), grammar, meter, etymology, astronomy/astrology, and ritual procedure (kalpa).

The first five are the four Vedas together with the Ekāyana branch. The second five are Dharmaśāstra Smṛtis, Gṛhya-sūtras dealing with conduct, Itihāsa, Purāṇa, and Pāñcarātra. The third five are vārttā or practical economics, statecraft and punishment (daṇḍanīti), Āyurveda, Dhanurveda, and Gandharvaveda.

The second line can then indicate various divisions of Śruti. “The exalted two” are mantra and brāhmaṇa. The three are the Ṛg, Yajus, and Sāman mantras. The seven are injunction (vidhi), explanatory praise or narrative (arthavāda), mantra, naming statement (nāmadheya), prohibition, Āraṇyaka, and Upaniṣad. If the Upaniṣad is included within the Āraṇyaka, or prohibition under injunction, or nāmadheya is not separately counted, these become six. They become eight if arthavāda in the sevenfold division is split into blame and praise corresponding to prohibition and injunction, or if it is divided into independent arthavāda and vākya-śeṣa.

In this interpretation “touch together with sound” can refer jointly to the realities mentioned in the preceding pāsuram and the words used in the present pāsuram as names of those realities. “Touch” is then an indicative term for the category of realities. Five such realities may be counted as prakṛti, the eternal transcendent realm (nitya-vibhūti), time, the sentient self, and attributive consciousness (dharma-bhūta-jñāna).

A further interpretation is possible from the many kinds of evidence discussed in Pūrva-Mīmāṃsā. The first six are the six proofs of distinct ritual acts taught in the second chapter: difference of wording (śabdāntara), repetition (abhyāsa), number (saṅkhyā), technical designation (saṃjñā), qualifying attribute (guṇa), and difference of context (prakaraṇāntara). The second six are the six instruments of application taught in the third chapter: direct statement (śruti), indicatory mark (liṅga), sentence unity (vākya), context (prakaraṇa), position (sthāna), and title or etymological designation (samākhyā). The third six are the ordering principles taught in the fifth chapter: direct text, purpose or meaning, recitation, position, principal status, and procedure or commencement.

One set of five can be the modes discussed in the later six chapters: transference based on name, transference based on an explicit statement, inferential transference, adaptation (ūha), and cancellation (bādha). Another five are the five organs of knowledge. A further five are mind, intellect, ego, citta, and the self-luminous entity. The exalted two are perception and inference. The three are perception, inference, and verbal testimony. Seven pramāṇas may be counted as perception, inference, comparison, verbal testimony, presumption, inclusion or possibility (sambhava), and non-cognition or absence (abhāva). When sambhava is not separately counted, the Mīmāṃsakas accept six; when traditional report (aitihya) is added to the seven, the count becomes eight.

Yet another arrangement is possible. The three sixes together are the eighteen Purāṇas. One five consists of the Mahābhārata, Rāmāyaṇa, Mīmāṃsā, Dharmaśāstra, and Vaikhānasa. Another five are astrology, legal procedure, expiation, medicine, and yoga. These are among the disciplines that, as the saying has it, immediately cause harm to people if one teaches them without mastering them properly: “astrology, legal procedure, expiation, and medicine…” A third five consists of the Vedāṅgas other than astrology. The exalted two are the Veda and Ekāyana. The three are the sciences of dance, song, and instrumental music. The seven are Sāṅkhya, Vaiśeṣika, Jaina, and the four Buddhist schools. The six are the Vedic divisions Ṛg, Yajus, Sāman, Brāhmaṇa, Āraṇyaka, and Upaniṣad. The eight are the four Paśupati-related systems - Kāpālika, Kālāmukha, Śaiva, and Pāśupata - together with the four Pāñcarātra classifications called Āgama-siddhānta, Mantra-siddhānta, Tantra-siddhānta, and Tantrāntara-siddhānta. Since authoritative Vedāntins do not accept as valid all the texts and divisions that can be counted in this way, the Āḻvār appropriately says “becoming different knowledges, true and false.”

We shall also record the explanation given by earlier commentators. Their first six are the duties beginning with Vedic study and sacrifice already mentioned above. Their second six are the six seasons beginning with spring, fit for the performance of rites. Their third six are the six sacrifices beginning with Āgneya that make up the Darśa-Pūrṇamāsa complex. Their first five are the five great sacrifices; the second five, the five oblations to the vital breaths; the third five, the five Vedic fires - Gārhapatya, Āhavanīya, Dakṣiṇāgni, Sabhya, and Āvasathya. The two are knowledge and detachment. The three are lordly prosperity, isolated self-realization, and mokṣa; alternatively, parabhakti, parajñāna, and paramabhakti. The seven are the disciplines beginning with viveka and vimoka already described. The six are the Lord’s six qualities of knowledge, power, strength, sovereignty, heroic energy, and splendor. The eight are the eight qualities associated with the supreme Self - freedom from sin, old age, death, sorrow, hunger, and thirst, together with true desire and true resolve.

On this elder interpretation, “different knowledge” can mean Smṛtis of outsiders and distorted schools. “True together with false” can mean that the Lord is true and present to those who take refuge in Him, but seems absent or “false” to others because He remains remote from them. “The five, touch together with sound” means touch and sound together with the other three objects - form, taste, and smell - making five in all.

It is true that in this older explanation some item falling under one count appears again under another more often than in our own explanation, and that in some places one must take a stated division as merely indicative and supply additional members not explicitly named. Even so, the interpretation is possible. We, however, have taken the pāsurams in sequence as concerning tattva, hita, and puruṣārtha and therefore explained this verse differently so as to fit that progression. This very plurality of possible interpretations also reveals the greatness of the verse. Other meanings too may be investigated.

If every subject alluded to here were explained in full, the treatment would become as extensive as the Mahābhārata itself; therefore we have stated matters only briefly. Those who desire complete detail should learn these subjects from teachers who have studied the śāstras. Greater elaboration can come elsewhere.

Pāsuram 3

Tamil Mūlam

ஐந்து மைந்து மைந்துமாகி யல்லவற்றுளாயுமாய்
ஐந்தும் மூன்றுமொன்றுமாகி நின்ற ஆதிதேவனே!
ஐந்துமைந்து மைந்துமாகி யந்தரத்தணைந்துநின்று
ஐந்தும் ஐந்து மைந்துமாய நின்னை யாவர் காணவல்லரே.

IAST Transliteration

aintu maintu maintumāki yallavaṟṟuḷāyumāy
aintum mūṉṟumoṉṟumāki niṉṟa ātitēvaṉē!
aintumaintu maintumāki yantarattaṇaintuniṉṟu
aintum aintu maintumāya niṉṉai yāvar kāṇavallarē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Becoming five, five, and five, and dwelling also in all that is other than these; becoming five, three, and one - O Primal Deity who thus abides! Becoming five, five, and five, drawing near and standing in the transcendent realm; becoming five and five - who is able to behold You?

English Translation of the Commentary

The first pāsuram stated the Reality that is the cause of the universe. The second spoke of the means. The third now teaches the supreme human goal: rejoicing in the direct vision of the Lord together with both His realms of manifestation.

Word-by-word gloss. The first “five” means the five great elements beginning with ether; the second, the five organs of knowledge; the third, the five organs of action. “Dwelling also in the others” means being present in all the remaining entities whose number is less or greater than five. The next five are the five subtle elements; the three are the qualities sattva, rajas, and tamas; the one is primordial prakṛti. “Standing” means abiding as one wholly opposed to every defect and full of auspicious qualities. “O Primal Deity” means the Supreme Being who, as the cause of the universe, enjoys the play of creation.

The next five are the bound souls, liberated souls, eternally free souls, attributive consciousness (dharma-bhūta-jñāna), and time. The next five are the five non-material elements corresponding to earth and the rest in the transcendent realm; the next five are the five qualities there beginning with sound. “In the interior/upper realm” means in Paramapada; “having approached and stood” means eternally seated there in inseparable union with Periya Pirāṭṭiyār. The next five are the five Upaniṣadic powers, and the final five are the five divine modes - para, vyūha, vibhava, hārda, and arcā. “You” are the Lord of both vibhūtis; “who can behold You?” means: who, without taking hold of the true means among those spoken of earlier, can attain the capacity to see You?

In the first pāsuram the words “who can think of You?” taught that He is one who must be known only through the scriptural tradition. The second explained at length that, because many systems of knowledge have arisen in the world, acquiring that correct knowledge is difficult. Now the Āḻvār teaches that unless one, through extraordinary good fortune, becomes the recipient of the Supreme Reality’s supreme compassion and learns through the authentic tradition, one cannot obtain the great attainment of seeing Him. Therefore he says here, “who can behold You?”

“Dwelling also in the others” includes all transformations of prakṛti that cannot neatly be grouped in fives: mahat, ahaṅkāra, mind, the ten vital airs, and the many physical products. The identity expressed by “becoming” is not a literal transformation of Brahman into inert matter. It is identity in the relation of controller and controlled (niyantṛ-niyāmya-bhāva). To make clear that Brahman does not Himself transform into insentient entities, the Āḻvār says “the Primal Deity who stands.” “Standing” means remaining distinct from those entities and untouched by any defect arising from association with them. It can also be explained as standing in the causal condition qualified by subtle sentient and insentient realities.

On allavaṟṟuḷ āyum āy: Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai divides the phrase as “within those other things,” so āyum āy appears to carry the sense “being their indwelling controller.” Āy can be taken as “mother” or as an entity that examines; alternatively the words can simply mean “being one who exists in the other entities.” Tamil uḷāy is the second-person form corresponding to “you are,” just as uḷāṉ/uḷaṉ means “he exists.” Thus the sense is simply “becoming one who is present in them.”

The five Upaniṣadic powers are those referred to in the formula beginning parameṣṭhī pumān viśvo nivṛttiḥ sarva eva hi… The Lord’s divine auspicious body is described as constituted of these five powers.

The elders also give an alternative syntactic arrangement of the verse: “becoming five, five, five; becoming five, five, three, and one; dwelling also in the others…” On their interpretation the first four groups of five are taken as previously indicated; the three are prakṛti, mahān, and ahaṅkāra; the one is mind. “Dwelling in the others” then means that He is also the indwelling controller of sentient beings, who differ from the insentient entities named before and after. The later three groups of five are the five non-material elements, the five powers, the five organs of knowledge, and the five organs of action; another five are the five objects beginning with sound; and the final five are the place of enjoyment, the instruments of enjoyment, the divine attendants in Vaikuṇṭha, the contemplative sages there, and the liberated souls. Among the eternal residents of Paramapada, those especially devoted to service are called amaras, while those whose state is characterized chiefly by enjoyment and contemplation are called munis. As before, antaram, literally the upper or intervening expanse, is understood as Paramapada.

Pāsuram 4

Tamil Mūlam

மூன்றுமுப்பத்தாறி னோடொரைந்து மைந்து மைந்துமாய்
மூன்றுமூர்த்தியாகி மூன்று மூன்று மூன்று மூன்றுமாய்
தோன்று சோதிமூன்றுமாய்த் துளக்கமில் விளக்கமாய்
ஏன்றெனாவியுள்புகுந்ததேன்கொலோ வெம்மீசனே!

IAST Transliteration

mūṉṟumuppattāṟi ṉōṭoraintu maintu maintumāy
mūṉṟumūrttiyāki mūṉṟu mūṉṟu mūṉṟu mūṉṟumāy
tōṉṟu cōtimūṉṟumāyt tuḷakkamil viḷakkamāy
ēṉṟeṉāviyuḷpukuntatēṉkolō vemmīcaṉē!

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O our Lord! Becoming the three together with the thirty and six, and becoming five, five, and five; becoming the three embodied forms, and four groups of three; becoming the three radiant manifestations that shine forth, and the unwavering Lamp - having taken all these forms, why have You entered into my very life? What a wonder!

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār now divides and describes the Lord’s forms in many ways.

Word-by-word gloss. “O our Lord!” - O Master of us all. “Three with thirty and six” means thirty-nine varieties of vibhava manifestation. “Five, five, five” means three principal groups of five among those manifestations. “Becoming three forms” means assuming forms in the Milk Ocean, the solar orb, and Śrī Viṣṇu-loka. “Three, three, three, three” means the twelve secondary vyūha forms beginning with Keśava. “The three radiant manifestations” are the luminous forms Saṅkarṣaṇa, Pradyumna, and Aniruddha, from which those others proceed. “The unwavering Lamp” is the imperishable para form. “Taking upon Yourself” means accepting all these forms. “Entering my life” means entering and shining in my heart as the hārda form. “Why?” can mean “for what reason?” or simply “what an astonishing wonder!”

In the Tattva-trayādhikāra of the Śrīmad Rahasya-traya-sāra, it is stated that the vibhavas are “some thirty and more forms beginning with Padmanābha.” That sacred statement appears to have this pāsuram in view. Commentaries such as the Sārāsvādinī, relying on Pāñcarātra passages from the Sātvata and other Saṃhitās, explain which forms these are. They also note that different Saṃhitās speak of six, eight, or nine beyond the thirty, and therefore Deśika used the general expression “thirty and some.” From this a count of thirty-nine vibhavas can be established, and we have accordingly used that count here.

The same work further says, to show the special prominence of Matsya, Kūrma, and similar descents, that “among these, the incarnations beginning with Matsya and Kūrma are specially mentioned because of particular purposes.” Following this, we explain the three fives in the verse. The first five are the forms named in the first half of the traditional list: Matsya, Kūrma, Varāha, Narasiṃha, and Vāmana. The next five are the forms that, unlike those, appeared by taking human parents: the three Rāmas - Paraśurāma, Rāma, and Balarāma - together with Kṛṣṇa and Kalki/Janārdana as indicated by the traditional enumeration. The third five are forms especially manifested for the promotion and preservation of sacred knowledge: Haṃsa, Hayagrīva, Nara, Nārāyaṇa, and Dattātreya. The greatness of Dattātreya is praised by the statement that when the Vedic dharma had been destroyed by the heterodox followers of Kaṇāda and the Śākya, Viṣṇu, previously appearing as the bearer of the triple staff, protected the threefold Veda.

“Becoming three forms” may alternatively mean that the Lord becomes the threefold divine manifestation by entering Brahmā and Rudra as their inner Self. The Keśava and other forms are grouped as four sets of three because the four principal vyūhas - Vāsudeva, Saṅkarṣaṇa, Pradyumna, and Aniruddha - each have three derivative forms connected with their respective functions.

On “becoming three”: although the primary vyūhas are four, Deśika explains that Pāñcarātra sometimes speaks of a threefold vyūha, not separately counting Vāsudeva, because there is no difference in the plenitude of qualities between this Vāsudeva and Para-Vāsudeva. Hence the Āḻvār can say “three” rather than “four.” In this way the verse teaches the para, vyūha, vibhava, and hārda modes. Since the para, vyūha, and vibhava forms also become manifest as arcā, the arcā mode too is implicitly included. If desired, either the phrase “three forms” or the “three” in the third line can also be taken as the three renowned arcā forms at Kōyil (Śrīraṅgam), Tirumalai (Tiruvēṅkaṭam), and Perumāḷ Kōyil (Kāñcī). Since the Āḻvār directly addresses the visible arcā Lord as “our Lord,” just as later he says “O Lord of Araṅgam,” one may also say that arcā is explicitly intended.

The word “taking upon Yourself” explains why He assumes all these forms. You dwell within sentient beings and govern everything merely by resolve; yet, in order to fulfill the desires of devotees, You consent to manifest in manifold forms. The scriptural sentiment is: “You have no material form, no fixed shape, no material weapons or abode; nevertheless, for the sake of Your devotees You reveal Yourself in such forms.”

“Within my life”: if Your forms existed only outside me, I could not see them at all times. Some are in higher worlds. Some appeared in former ages and have disappeared. Arcā forms too can generally be served only by going to the places where they are established, and many kinds of obstacles can intervene. Therefore, wishing always to remain with me and show Yourself whenever I desire, You also shine within me as the indwelling hārda form. Your parental tenderness (vātsalya) and loving infatuation with Your devotee cannot be adequately described. Since “within my life” explicitly speaks of the hārda manifestation, the rest of the verse may be understood as elaborating the external forms mentioned at the end of the preceding pāsuram; the terms “form” and “radiance” fit this interpretation especially well.

We shall also record the explanation of the elders. Since in the second, hita-oriented pāsuram we interpreted the numbers as many branches of knowledge, it is fitting here to take the letters that underlie those disciplines. “Three and thirty” then means the thirty-three consonants. One must supply an understood conjunction here. “With six, one five, and five” gives sixteen vowels. The next five are the special letters beginning with ḷa. “Three forms” are the three Vedas, Ṛg, Yajus, and Sāman. “Three, three, three, three” is the twelve-syllabled Vāsudeva mantra. If it is asked why the Āḻvār speaks of the Dvādaśākṣara rather than the Aṣṭākṣara, the answer is that he wishes to teach that this mantra too possesses greatness; elsewhere he speaks of it again. One cannot therefore arbitrarily make every number in the verse denote a mantra, for no such interpretations are traditionally well established.

“The threefold appearing radiance” is the radiance manifest in the three syllabic constituents of the Praṇava. The syntax can be rearranged to yield this sense. Although the simple word “three” might have sufficed, the expression emphasizes the special glory of the Praṇava. “The unwavering Lamp” then refers to the letter a. On this reading, the concluding sense is: You who are of such a nature have taken my affairs upon Yourself as Your own responsibility and entered my life - what an astonishing condescension!

The thirty-three consonants are the twenty-five mute consonants in the five classes beginning with ka, ca, ṭa, ta, and pa; the four ya, ra, la, va; and the four sibilant/aspirate letters śa, ṣa, sa, ha. If one asks why the latter eight are distributed in an unusual manner in some counts, it may be because śa, ṣa, and sa are regarded as approximately one class. The sixteen vowels are those from a through aṃ and aḥ, counting the additional vocalic forms according to the traditional alphabetic scheme.

The five special letters are counted in the traditional grammatical discussions as ḷa and certain compounded or special sounds such as kṣa, jihvāmūlīya, upadhmānīya, and jña. Though kṣa is formed from k and ṣ, it is separately counted because compounded vowels such as e are likewise formed from simpler vowels but are counted distinctly, and because in recitation it exhibits special behavior after visarga. The special signs before ka and pa are termed jihvāmūlīya and upadhmānīya in phonetic texts. Some elementary alphabets replace these with conjunct representations; proverbial joking questions about variant forms arise from such traditions. Jña too is historically a conjunct but is counted separately because its pronunciation is distinctive. Similar pronunciation changes occur in conjuncts such as hma and hya; why those are not likewise counted requires investigation, and the scheme should not be taken as universally obligatory.

If it is asked why the twelve-syllabled mantra is not stated according to its ordinary word divisions but as four groups of three, one may say that the Āḻvār intentionally veils the mantra. All contextually suitable interpretations should be carefully considered.

Pāsuram 5

Tamil Mūlam

நின்றியங்குமொன்றலா வுருக்கள்தோறுமாவியாய்
ஒன்றியுள்கலந்துநின்ற நின்னதன்மையின்னதென்று
என்றும்யார்க்குமெண்ணிறந்த வாதியாய், நின்னுந்திவாய்
அன்றுநான்முகன்பயந்த ஆதிதேவனல்லையே.

IAST Transliteration

niṉṟiyaṅkumoṉṟalā vurukkaḷtōṟumāviyāy
oṉṟiyuḷkalantuniṉṟa niṉṉataṉmaiyiṉṉateṉṟu
eṉṟumyārkkumeṇṇiṟanta vātiyāy, niṉṉuntivāy
aṉṟunāṉmukaṉpayanta ātitēvaṉallaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

As the inner Self in every one of the many forms that stand and move, You are joined with them and remain mingled within. Who, at any time, can fully think out and say, “Such precisely is Your nature”? O incomprehensible Primal Cause! Are You not that First Deity who, in the beginning, produced four-faced Brahmā from the mouth of Your sacred navel?

English Translation of the Commentary

An introductory dialogue may be imagined. The Lord asks: “Why are you so astonished, saying, ‘Why has He entered my life?’ Is it not four-faced Brahmā, the creator of the world, who creates the gods and others and causes Me to enter them? In the same fashion he has made Me enter your life. What have I done in this?” The Āḻvār replies: as taught in the adhikaraṇa concerning the assignment of names and forms, You are the principal agent of total creation, individual creation, and every other act of creation, and You are the creator even of that Brahmā. Therefore everything is in truth Your doing. Since Your nature cannot be exhaustively conceived by me or by anyone else, there is nothing surprising in my astonishment.

Another introductory explanation is also given. If the Vedas and their secrets exist, one might ask, how could the Āḻvār have said “who can behold You?” The answer is: no one sees, as I have been enabled by Your grace to see, the nature of You who are the Inner Controller of all.

Word-by-word gloss. “Standing” - when Your resolve is absent, nothing can act at all. “Moving” - only when You resolve does anything become active. “Not one” - not a single uniform kind but many diverse forms. “In every form” - in bodies extending from those of gods down to immobile plants. “Being the life and becoming one with them” - fitting Yourself to them as their inner Self. “Standing mingled within” - being inwardly associated also with the individual selves who are the selves of those bodies. “Your nature” - Your essential mode. “Such as this” - capable of being defined as exactly so. “At any time, to anyone” - at no time and for no person. “Beyond thought” - transcending the completion of conceptual determination. “O Primal One” - the cause even of individual or differentiated creation. “Then” - after creating the aggregate universe, performing the threefold combination of the elements, and reclining upon the waters as Nārāyaṇa. “From the mouth of Your navel” - from Your divine navel. “Producing four-faced Brahmā” - creating Brahmā. “Are You not the First Deity?” - You are the God of gods, the source of all.

Even when Brahmā, using the Veda given by You, creates the gods and other beings, he does nothing independently. Because he resolves in accordance with the resolve of You who have entered within him, You are the true agent. Moreover, since You are the Self both of those bodily forms and of the individual selves who animate them, You alone everywhere are also the material cause of all. Brahmā has no such capacity at all. This is established by the words “as the life in every form, becoming one, standing mingled within.”

Some explain “standing and moving” simply as “the immobile and mobile beings.” In that case one would grammatically expect forms equivalent to “what stands and what moves.” One might instead take “standing” adverbially - “You, being present as an immobile thing” - and connect it with “being the life.” But then the statement that He is the Self of that entity would not be as directly expressed. Therefore we have included both stationary and moving beings under the word “forms” and interpreted “standing and moving” in relation to their dependence upon His will. The expression may also mean “remaining manifest in accordance with the individual souls’ experience of the fruits of karma,” comparable to an expression such as “remaining and becoming weary.” Since “standing and moving” itself shows that these things have no independent power, we have not adopted for “not one” an alternative sense such as “not constituting even one means for their own deliverance,” but have followed the other explanation of the elders.

“Being the life, becoming one, standing mingled within” can distinguish two modes taught in the statement “He Himself is body and life”: the Lord’s indwelling in bodies and His interior pervasion of the individual soul. Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai glosses the passage in terms equivalent to “pervading completely, entering, and remaining so mingled that it may be spoken of as one substance.” There the notion of samāpti - complete presence - is taught with respect to every form.

The word samāpti has more than one sense: “termination or limitation” and “complete attainment or complete pervasion.” Complete pervasion means not merely being spread through a thing as ether is, but pervading it together with the power of governance. This can be seen in the explanation of the Gītā statement, “You completely pervade all; therefore You are all.” It can also mean being related to every part of a thing rather than being connected to one part and absent from another. Again, the pervading entity may completely attain one thing without depending upon another thing in order to attain it; this too can be called a kind of completion. Another kind of “completion” is the absence of partiality on the side of the pervader: it is not that only one portion of the pervader relates to the object while another portion remains unrelated. There are also ordinary forms of limitation, such as existing at one time and not another, or being in one place and not another. Just as samāpti may describe the pervader’s relation to every part of the pervaded object, it may also describe the pervader as present with its whole reality. The latter is connected with the spatial issue just mentioned.

When we ask what kind of samāpti belongs to the Lord in each entity, it cannot be a limitation by which the all-pervading Supreme Being, present always and everywhere, terminates in one place and is absent elsewhere. Śrī Deśika therefore teaches that the Lord’s complete presence must be of a different sort.

The term samāpya in Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai’s commentary is itself general, and thus can stand in the sense explained by Śrī Deśika. If one takes samāpti as “limitation,” what sort of limitation is intended? If it means “He is only here and not elsewhere,” can that apply to the all-pervading Lord? Can absence elsewhere be predicated of one who is present elsewhere too? Some reply, “What is impossible for the omnipotent Lord? Does He not possess the capacity to make the impossible possible (aghaṭita-ghaṭanā-śakti)?” But if that license were accepted without qualification, every mutually contradictory predicate could be attributed to Him. Our teachers could then, without any effort to explain the body-self relation, have casually affirmed both absolute identity and absolute difference between the individual soul and the Lord.

The same issue arises with Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai’s word “entering.” If one insists on a literal, ordinary meaning, “to enter” means to perform a motion and arrive at a place with which one had previously no connection, and then remain specifically inside it. When a non-contradictory sense of the more general term samāpya is available, can one reject it and, amid noisy sectarian controversy, insist on a contradictory literal meaning? If one says the literal sense of “enter” must be abandoned because an all-pervading Lord cannot previously have been unrelated, cannot newly acquire motion, and cannot be confined only within, then why not similarly abandon the impossible sense of spatially limited samāpti? One cannot simply invoke omnipotence and aghaṭita-ghaṭanā-śakti for one expression but refuse the same reasoning for the other.

Many scriptural passages do say “having entered within, He rules,” “He who moves within the earth,” and the like. If one took “He who moves within the self” literally, one would be forced to imagine the all-pervading Supreme Self physically walking about inside the atomic individual self. Rāmānuja did not take such a crude sense there. The same reason he gave there must be applied here. Thus both “entering” and samāpti require contextually appropriate non-material meanings.

One may object that Rāmānuja and others, in works such as the Vedāntasāra, use as an illustration the view of those who accept universals such as cowness. They seem to say: if you consider the claim that a universal is “completely present” in an individual to be reasonable, why can you not similarly accept that the Supreme Self is completely present? Does that not show that the elders admitted samāpti of the Supreme Self? Certainly they did. The issue is not whether the word may be used but what that samāpti means.

Do theorists of universals mean that the universal “cowness” exists in one cow and is therefore absent from another? No. A universal is not a property that has vyāsajya-vṛtti, whose presence in one locus depends upon simultaneous presence in another, as duality does. When we say “two pots,” the property “twoness” exists only by relation to both pots; the word “two” cannot denote just one. A universal is not like that. Cowness can be present in one cow without depending upon another cow for that presence. In calling this cow “a cow,” we do not have to include some other cow in the same usage. In the same way, the Supreme Self’s presence in one entity does not depend upon His presence in another entity. This is the kind of samāpti that Rāmānuja admits. Similarly, just as a partless universal does not admit the alternative “present wholly or present only in one part,” such a whole-part dilemma does not apply to partless Brahman either. This is Rāmānuja’s intention. Let the matter rest there.

Where, then, has Śrī Deśika rejected samāpti in this valid sense? Some people write that “Śrī Deśika rejected it in the Śrīmad Rahasya-traya-sāra, but he himself accepted it in the Nyāya-siddhāñjana,” and thereby mislead readers as though Deśika contradicted himself. Both works teach the same doctrine. Would Śrī Deśika contradict himself when a single consistent meaning readily fits both texts? Those who lack the capacity to investigate the passages misunderstand them and then impose their misunderstanding upon Śrī Deśika. What a reversal! What obstinacy!

In the Nyāya-siddhāñjana itself Deśika enumerates and rejects several improper alternatives concerning samāpti. In the Śrīmad Rahasya-traya-sāra he criticizes only a type of samāpti accepted by rival systems and requiring appeal to an unrestricted “power to accomplish contradictions”; he never says that the very word samāpti is impermissible. One should also consult the Tātparya-candrikā on “You pervade all.” The true sense of fullness (pūrṇatva) is not a spatially bounded samāpti; it is as Śrī Deśika explains. This may also be seen in Śrī Bhaṭṭar’s commentary on the Viṣṇu-sahasranāma and in the Śrutaprakāśikā.

We have stated this briefly so that everyone may understand. It is not ordinarily appropriate to give so extended a discussion in a Tamil commentary. We have done so here because some, seeking to consider themselves accomplished by criticizing Śrī Deśika, even drag the elders of their own line - who actually stood in agreement with him - onto a wrong path, and their polemical display, produced by sectarian fixation rather than necessity, continues to grow. This clarification is meant to expose that problem.

The phrase “standing mingled within” further teaches that the Lord remains beside the individual soul as its companion while the soul experiences the fruits of karma, and that He interiorly pervades the partless substance that is the self, being present throughout the locus in which that self exists. Further details may be learned from our works such as the Ācārya-bhāṣya-tātparya.

On “O incomprehensible Primal One”: grammatically this is the vocative of “the primal one.” Calling Him the beginning or source establishes that He, not Brahmā, is the principal cause even of differentiated creation. Because the Lord is the Inner Controller everywhere, as another pāsuram says, “all that stands is the Tall Lord.” For the wise, therefore, whatever entity is directly apprehended, the Lord stands as the principal reality in that very apprehension. There is also an interpretation that the word “Primal” suggests this semantic primacy: whatever word is spoken, the learned finally take Him to be its highest and principal referent.

Pāsuram 6

Tamil Mūlam

நாகமேந்து மேருவெற்பை நாகமேந்து மண்ணினை
நாகமேந்துமாக மாக மாகமேந்துவார்புனல்
மாகமேந்துமங்குல் தீயோர் வாயுவைந்தமைந்துகாத்து
ஏகமேந்திநின்றநீர்மை நின்கணே யியன்றதே.

IAST Transliteration

nākamēntu mēruveṟpai nākamēntu maṇṇiṉai
nākamēntumāka māka mākamēntuvārpuṉal
mākamēntumaṅkul tīyōr vāyuvaintamaintukāttu
ēkamēntiniṉṟanīrmai niṉkaṇē yiyaṉṟatē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

You alone bear the Meru mountain that reaches heaven, the earth borne by the serpent, heaven itself with its great delight, the vast mid-region, the streaming waters borne through the heavens, the cloud that bears abundant water, the peerless fire and peerless wind - harmoniously preserving these five. This power of standing alone as the bearer belongs naturally to You alone.

English Translation of the Commentary

The preceding verse described the Lord as the inner Self and as the cause of the creation and continuance of everything. This verse now says that the whole universe is supported by Him so that its constituents neither collide with one another nor fall from their appointed places.

Word-by-word gloss. “Meru that bears heaven” - Mount Meru, whose height reaches the heavenly region. “The earth borne by the serpent” - the earth sustained by Ādiśeṣa. “The serpent/heaven that bears great delight” - heaven, which affords abundant pleasure. “Great kham” - the vast intermediate region or atmosphere. “The flowing water borne by the heavens” - the great current of the Gaṅgā supported in heaven and the sky. “The cloud that bears the great water” - the cloud abiding in the sky and carrying excellent waters. “The one fire, the one wind” - the incomparable fire and wind. “The five” - the five great elemental realities thus indicated. “Having fitted together and protected them” - harmoniously preserving them. “Standing as the one bearer” - alone bearing the entire arrangement. “This nature” - the natural power called vīrya. “In You alone” - it belongs inherently to You.

As the traditional verse says, “Heaven with moon, sun, and stars; ether, the directions, the earth, and the great ocean - all are upheld by the power of the great Vāsudeva,” the entire universe remains in its proper place and performs its proper function without its parts colliding, solely because of His innate quality called vīrya. The Īśāvāsya tradition likewise states that “in Him Mātariśvan assigns or sustains the waters,” teaching that air moving through space and water and the rest remain supported because He is their indwelling ruler. In his commentary there Śrī Deśika explains that “waters” can indicate water, clouds, planets, stars, and related entities.

On “Meru that bears heaven”: the height of Mount Meru is said to be eighty-four thousand yojanas. The solar sphere is at a distance of one hundred thousand yojanas above the earth, and the region up to it is called the atmosphere. From the solar sphere up to Dhruva is called heaven; beyond that are the four worlds beginning with Maharloka and ending with Satyaloka. Since Meru rises eighty-four thousand yojanas toward the heavenly region, it is poetically said to reach heaven and even to support it. Alternatively, because the inhabitants of heaven come comfortably to dwell upon Meru’s summit, Meru may be said to bear heaven. Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai explains that Meru serves as a support to heaven through the brilliance of its summit. Since the radiance of that golden mountain, which lies so near, extends up to heaven, this explanation is appropriate.

The second occurrence of nāgam can carry the Sanskrit sense nāga, “serpent.” There are also the directional elephants, which are called dig-gajas.

On nāgam ēntum māgam: it can mean “the heaven possessed of the great delight that is borne,” or “heaven that bears great delight.” Māgam may be analyzed as mā, great, plus kam, happiness. This gives an etymological explanation of nāgam: kam means happiness and akam its absence; nāgam may be interpreted as that in which the absence of happiness is absent. Alternatively, agam can be taken as sorrow, giving “that which is without sorrow.” Since heaven is not literally free from all sorrow, the phrase “bearing great delight” indicates that, unlike the earthly world, it is predominantly pleasurable. Some understand “the heaven borne by nāga” as Paramapada, the supreme ether that bears nothing but bliss. Since āgam can mean body, others parse the words as a vocative: “O Lord whose divine body is borne by Ādiśeṣa!” As in several later pāsurams, however, the verse can also stand without an explicit vocative.

The later mā-kam is mahā-kham, the great intermediate region, namely the sky. The following occurrences likewise can denote sky, while the final word in the phrase denotes water. Since the verse thus alludes to the five great elements, “five” sums them up; it may also include all physical products composed of the five. Some take “fire” as the Vaiśvānara fire in the stomach and “five winds” as the five vital breaths, though the reading “five winds” is not the one preferred by the commentator.

Ekam can mean “the whole” or “alone.” Some of the entities just named appear to support others. In reality You alone are the support. By Your power those other things act like auxiliary props supporting a temple tower and therefore receive the conventional designation “supports.” “In You alone” excludes Brahmā, Rudra, and the others: they do not possess this independent sustaining power.

Pāsuram 7

Tamil Mūlam

ஒன்றிரண்டு மூர்த்தியாய் உறக்கமோடுணர்ச்சியாய்
ஒன்றிரண்டுகாலமாகி வேலைஞாலமாயினாய்
ஒன்றிரண்டுதீயுமாகி ஆயனாயமாயனே!
ஒன்றிரண்டுகண்ணினானு முன்னையேத்தவல்லனே.

IAST Transliteration

oṉṟiraṇṭu mūrttiyāy uṟakkamōṭuṇarcciyāy
oṉṟiraṇṭukālamāki vēlaiñālamāyiṉāy
oṉṟiraṇṭutīyumāki āyaṉāyamāyaṉē!
oṉṟiraṇṭukaṇṇiṉāṉu muṉṉaiyēttavallaṉē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Becoming the one and the two forms; becoming sleep together with waking; becoming the one and two divisions of time; becoming the ocean and the earth; becoming the one and two fires - O cowherd, O wondrous Lord! Is even the three-eyed one capable of praising You as You truly are?

English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord may be imagined saying: “Even if the capacity to harmonize, preserve, and bear the five elements belongs to Me, consider the superiority of Śiva, the eight-formed Lord, who regards these five elements, the moon, the sun, and the sacrificer as his own forms.” The Āḻvār replies with this verse. Another introduction is: if one asks how he can say “who is able to behold You?” when there are beings endowed with complete knowledge, he answers that even the omniscient Rudra cannot adequately know or praise Him.

Word-by-word gloss. “Becoming the one and two forms” - becoming Viṣṇu and, as their inner Self, the forms of Brahmā and Rudra. “Becoming sleep and waking” - governing both sleep and wakefulness. “Becoming one and two times” - being the Self of present, past, and future. “Becoming ocean and earth” - being the Self of sea and land. “Becoming one and two fires” - becoming the three Vedic fires. “Becoming a cowherd” - appearing as Kṛṣṇa. “O wondrous Lord!” - O astonishing Being. “Even he of one and two eyes” - even three-eyed Rudra. “Can he praise You?” - can he truly praise You according to Your real nature? He cannot.

You are not merely the form called Viṣṇu. Because You are the Inner Controller of Brahmā and Rudra, You are also the inner Self of those forms. Thus even the so-called eightfold manifestation of Rudra is in fact a manifestation dependent upon You, and whatever lordship he possesses has been obtained through Your grace. Sleep and waking are under You. Just as knowledge and ignorance are sometimes symbolically spoken of in relation to Your sword Nandaka and its sheath, sleep and waking - particular states associated with knowledge and non-knowledge - are dependent on You. Therefore night and day, their causes, and the moon and sun who preside over them are also subject to You.

The present and the other two times are You in the sense that You are their inner Self; what doubt then can there be that night and day are dependent upon You? The same is true of the five elements. The sacred fires through which the sacrificer receives ritual eligibility are also You. Thus the eight forms attributed to Rudra are themselves included within Your cosmic form. He is merely called “eight-formed”; You are the wondrous one, the truly universal form.

On “becoming one and two times”: the logicians hold that past and future are indicated by relation to the present; the details may be seen in the Nyāya system. Since there is no need to isolate one of the three times merely on that account, no forced alternative meaning is necessary. Some nevertheless interpret the three as times dominated respectively by sattva, rajas, and tamas. “Ocean-earth” may also be understood as “the earth surrounded by the ocean.” Some restrict it to Bhārata-varṣa, which is particularly designated for the performance of dharma.

The Viṣṇu form is mentioned separately at the beginning because it is a direct divine manifestation. Among the Vedic fires, the Gārhapatya is the source from which the others are produced; yet because the principal oblations are mostly offered in the Āhavanīya, that fire too is said to be chief. Rudra’s forehead-eye, unlike his other eyes, is fiery; hence it is counted separately to yield the expression “one and two eyes.”

“O cowherd, O wondrous one!” Let all the cosmic matters stand aside. Rudra is not even capable of adequately praising the single, apparently insignificant marvel of Your having become a cowherd. How then could he finish telling the wonder of Your true majesty? In the Kṛṣṇa incarnation, when You fought Bāṇāsura, what did the three eyes of the three-eyed Rudra and all his other equipment accomplish? It is enough merely to remember that defeat. Such is the intended force.

Pāsuram 8

Tamil Mūlam

ஆதியானவானவர்க்குமண்டமாயவப்புறத்து
ஆதியானவானவர்க்கு மாதியான வாதி நீ
ஆதியான வானவாணரந்தகாலநீயுரைத்தி
ஆதியானகால நின்னை யாவர் காண வல்லரே.

IAST Transliteration

ātiyāṉavāṉavarkkumaṇṭamāyavappuṟattu
ātiyāṉavāṉavarkku mātiyāṉa vāti nī
ātiyāṉa vāṉavāṇarantakālanīyuraitti
ātiyāṉakāla niṉṉai yāvar kāṇa vallarē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

You are the beginning of those celestial beings who are creators, and of the cosmic egg itself; You are the beginning also of the eternal dwellers beyond that sphere. You are the Beginning that is the cause of all and has no prior cause. O One who is the inner ruler even of Time, the beginning of all! You have ordained the end-time of the celestial beings who themselves are agents of dissolution. Who can fully behold You, the primordial source?

English Translation of the Commentary

The previous pāsuram taught the Lord’s causality with respect to preservation. The Āḻvār now says that He is also the cause of the very beings who function as agents of creation and destruction, and the Inner Controller of Time, which conditions all such processes. Thus He cannot be fully comprehended even by beings superior to Rudra.

Word-by-word gloss. “For those who are beginnings” - for Brahmā, the Prajāpatis, and the other creators. “The cosmic egg” - that which develops into the Brahmāṇḍa. “Beyond it” - beyond that sphere. “For the primordial celestials” - for the eternally existing residents of Paramapada. “The Beginning that is their beginning” - You are the cause of their essential existence, continuance, and activity. “You, the Beginning” - You are the cause who has no cause of Your own. “O Beginning of time” - O inner ruler even of Time, which is causal with respect to all conditioned processes. “The celestial lords who are beginnings” - Rudra and others who act as agents of dissolution. “Their ending time You have declared” - You have fixed the limit of their lifespan. “Who can behold You?” - who can completely comprehend You who are the root of all?

For the first line one may recall the passage beginning “Brahmā, Dakṣa, and the others…” and for the third the passage beginning “Rudra, Kāla, Antaka, and the others….”

On “beyond the cosmic egg”: some explain “the cosmic egg” as Paramapada itself, but the elders understand the phrase as “in Paramapada, which lies beyond the līlā-vibhūti denoted by the word ’egg’.” Some textual discussions invert the wording, but the printed reading can itself be understood clearly. The word “beyond” naturally demands a spatial point of reference; therefore “the cosmic egg” can denote the realm of prakṛti, beyond which lies Paramapada. One may also say that the Āḻvār intends the līlā-vibhūti itself by “becoming the egg.” Just as elsewhere a case-relation is understood in an expression like “becoming the fruit that gives,” “becoming the egg” can carry the sense “with reference to the cosmic egg, the līlā-vibhūti.” If “cosmic egg” were merely an adjective of Paramapada, it would be redundant; its natural function is to specify that beyond the domain of prakṛti lies the transcendent realm. The verb “becoming” is also significant: the reference need not be confined to one Brahmāṇḍa but may indicate the whole root prakṛti from which such worlds arise.

In the second line, if “being a beginning” is taken as “being a cause,” it may refer to certain eternal attendants who, according to the chapter on divine weapons and ornaments in the Viṣṇu Purāṇa that underlies Śrī Deśika’s teaching, participate causally in functions connected with the līlā-vibhūti. If all the Nityasūris are intended, “being primordial” can simply mean “existing from beginningless time.”
