வெஞ்சினத்த வேழவெண் மருப்பொசித்துருத்தமா
கஞ்சனைக் கடிந்துமண் ணளந்துகொண்ட காலனே!
வஞ்சனத்து வந்தபேய்ச்சி யாவிபாலுள் வாங்கினாய்
அஞ்சனத்த வண்ணனாய ஆதிதேவ னல்லையே.

IAST Transliteration

veñciṉatta vēḻaveṇ maruppocitturuttamā
kañcaṉaik kaṭintumaṇ ṇaḷantukoṇṭa kālaṉē!
vañcaṉattu vantapēycci yāvipāluḷ vāṅkiṉāy
añcaṉatta vaṇṇaṉāya ātitēva ṉallaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O One whose feet measured and took the earth! You drew out the life of the deceitfully approaching demoness together with her milk. Breaking the white tusk of the elephant raging with fierce anger, You then destroyed the mighty Kaṃsa who raged like that elephant. Are You not the Primal Deity who shines in the dark hue of collyrium?

English Translation of the Commentary

The preceding pāsuram was an appendix to the story of subduing the bulls. The Āḻvār now speaks of the killing of Kaṃsa.

Word-by-word gloss. “O One of the foot that measured and took the earth!” — O Lord whose divine feet took possession of the world in the Vāmana incarnation. “Coming in deceit,” the demoness Pūtanā; “her life,” “together with the milk, You drew in” — sucking away her very life through the breast-milk. “The elephant of fierce rage,” Kuvalayāpīḍa; “breaking its white tusk.” “The mighty Kaṃsa who raged” like that elephant; “destroying him.” “Becoming of the color of collyrium”; “are You not the Primal Deity?” — are You not the Supreme God Himself?

If the verb “destroyed” is connected syntactically with “O One who took the earth,” strict chronology becomes disordered, and one would have to say the poet disregards temporal sequence. It is more fitting to connect it with “becoming collyrium-colored”: after destroying Kaṃsa and gaining the splendor of victory, Kṛṣṇa’s dark divine body shone with the deep brilliance of collyrium.

“Are You not the Primal Deity?” His destruction of Pūtanā and similar enemies did not by itself make everyone recognize Him as the Supreme Deity. The killing of Kaṃsa did. Only then did all understand that He was the son of Devakī foretold by the disembodied heavenly voice, and that the prophecy had been fulfilled.

Pāsuram 44

Tamil Mūlam

பாலினீர்மை செம்பொனீர்மை பாசியின் பசும்புறம்
போலும்நீர்மை பொற்புடைத் தடத்துவண்டு விண்டுலா
நீலநீர்மை யென்றிவை நிறைந்தகால நான்குமாய்
மாலினீர்மை வையகம் மறைத்ததென்ன நீர்மையே.

IAST Transliteration

pāliṉīrmai cempoṉīrmai pāciyiṉ pacumpuṟam
pōlumnīrmai poṟpuṭait taṭattuvaṇṭu viṇṭulā
nīlanīrmai yeṉṟivai niṟaintakāla nāṉkumāy
māliṉīrmai vaiyakam maṟaittateṉṉa nīrmaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

In the four fulfilled ages You possess respectively the nature of milk, the nature of red gold, a nature like the fresh green surface of moss, and the nature of the dark blue water-lily in a beautiful pond where bees spread their wings and roam. Yet what strange nature has this world, that it can conceal the very nature of the Lord whose love for beings is so great?

English Translation of the Commentary

The description of the vibhava incarnations reaches a kind of conclusion here. Having contemplated the collyrium-dark radiance of Kṛṣṇa who slew Kaṃsa, the Āḻvār longs to see that color. Scripture teaches that the Lord’s manifested form has a distinct color in each age, and those colors come to mind: “In the Kṛta age He is white and four-armed; in Tretā He is red; in Dvāpara Bhagavān is dark; in Kali He is Kṛṣṇa, dark in luster,” and similar statements. Though He assumes different colors to mark the different ages, in Kali He keeps His natural dark hue unobscured, in keeping with the very name Kṛṣṇa, so that those who wish to see Him may recognize Him readily. Yet even then the world conceals Him; the Āḻvār grieves that the eye fails to see One who is near.

Word-by-word gloss. “The nature of milk”; “the nature of red gold”; “a nature like the green surface of moss”; “possessing beauty,” “in a pond,” “where bees open their wings and roam,” “the nature of the blue water-lily.” “The four times” — the four yugas — “becoming filled with these [colors].” “The nature of Māl” — the special gracious nature of the Lord who is passionately attached to living beings. “The world concealed [it]” — prevented people from seeing Him. “What sort of nature is that?” What a strange quality belongs to this world!

By naming not merely white, red, green, and black but milk, gold, moss, and the blue water-lily, the pāsuram indicates more than color. His divine body is delightful like milk, precious like gold, cooling like moss, and endowed like the blue lily with beauty, fragrance, softness, and other excellent qualities.

It is not enough merely to say “the nature of moss,” because one side may be soiled or discolored. Hence the verse specifies “like its fresh green surface.” “Possessing beauty” indicates visual loveliness that delights the mind. “In a pond” suggests coolness pleasing to the sense of touch. “Where bees spread their wings and roam” evokes nectar relished by the greatest devotees and thereby delights ear and tongue through sound and taste. The “blue lily” itself evokes color, fragrance, and softness, pleasing eye, nose, and other senses. Thus, in accordance with scriptural descriptions such as “all fragrance, all taste,” the verse unfolds the extraordinary nature of a form to be enjoyed by every sense.

The choice of the broader word “nature” (nīrmai) rather than simply “color” is intentional: as far as possible, the associated qualities are to be taken together with the hue.

“The world concealed it.” The Lord, out of infatuated love, assumes these many appropriate colors so that all may enjoy Him; yet the bad “nature” of this world proves stronger in practical effect. It makes people plunge into its own attractions so that they do not see Him, and even when they hear the truth and intellectually understand it, it prevents their thoughts from going toward Him. The nature of worldly māyā is famously described as “the curtain, Māyā, that deludes the world.”

Some explain “the world concealed Him” as: “By what evil habituation do the people of the world reject Him?” Then the Āḻvār is grieving over the condition of persons such as the asuras. “World” must in that case stand for such persons within it.

A variant reading has “the four are” rather than “becoming the four.” On that reading the syntax is: “the Lord who is the four fully established times,” that is, the regulator of the ages. Since the transmitted reading commonly recited and printed is “becoming the four,” it is preferable to take āy as an absolutive, “having become,” in accordance with the gloss above.

Pāsuram 45

Tamil Mūlam

மண்ணுளாய்கொல் விண்ணுளாய்கொல் மண்ணுளேமயங்கிநின்று
எண்ணுமெண் அகப்படாய்கொல் என்னமாயை நின்றமர்
கண்ணுளாய்கொல் சேயைகொல் அனந்தன் மேற்கிடந்தவெம்
புண்ணியா புனந்துழாயலங்கலம் புனிதனே!

IAST Transliteration

maṇṇuḷāykol viṇṇuḷāykol maṇṇuḷēmayaṅkiniṉṟu
eṇṇumeṇ akappaṭāykol eṉṉamāyai niṉṟamar
kaṇṇuḷāykol cēyaikol aṉantaṉ mēṟkiṭantavem
puṇṇiyā puṉantuḻāyalaṅkalam puṉitaṉē!

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O our blessed Treasure who reclines upon Ananta! O supremely pure One adorned with a garland of forest-grown tulasī! Are You in the earth—or in heaven? Do You refuse to enter the thought of those who, deluded within the world, stand absorbed in worldly objects, yet become visible to the eyes of Your devotees? Are You then also far away? What an immeasurable wonder is this!

English Translation of the Commentary

A possible objection arises. If one admits that the Lord possesses a form, He should be visible only where that form is located. Instead of saying “the world conceals Him from everyone,” why not simply say that He has a form of such-and-such color in one particular place and therefore is not seen elsewhere? The pāsuram answers by showing that ordinary spatial limitations do not apply to Him.

Word-by-word gloss. “Upon Ananta,” Ādiśeṣa, “reclining”; “O our blessed One!” — O embodiment of our accumulated good fortune. “Forest-grown tulasī garland”; “O beautiful supremely pure One!” “Are You in the earth?” — You are indeed in this world. “Are You in heaven?” — You are also in the higher world. “Being deluded within the earth,” those who stand absorbed in worldly objects and “think” only of them: “are You not caught within their thought?” — You do not dwell in such minds. “Your people,” Your devotees: “are You within their eyes?” — You become directly perceptible to them. “Are You far away?” — at the same time You remain remote from others. “What a māyā!” Your wondrous capacity cannot be described.

The interrogative particle kol highlights apparent contradiction. Ordinarily, if a person is in one place, he cannot at that very time be somewhere else. You, however, are simultaneously in Paramapada and in the realm of Prakṛti. Again, what cannot even be grasped by thought should not become an object of sight; yet You, while transcending conceptual capture, become visible to the eye.

The Lord’s essential nature as Supreme Self pervades everywhere and therefore is in both earth and heaven. Further, because He can assume many forms, there can be one divine manifestation here and another there at the same time. How can the One who cannot be grasped by thought nevertheless be seen? The wording itself resolves the matter by specifying two classes: “those who stand deluded in the world and think [only of worldly objects]” do not grasp Him even mentally, whereas “Your devotees” have Him before their eyes.

Recall the traditional verses: “For those turned away from Govinda, whose minds are attached to sense-objects, that Supreme Brahman stands farther than the farthest. But for those whose minds are placed in Govinda in complete identification and who have abandoned sense-objects, that Reality is to be known as near.” See also the benedictory verse of the Vedānta-dīpa.

After four mutually contrasting properties have been stated, what new idea is added by “are You far away?” It teaches not merely that He fails to enter the minds of those deluded by sense-objects, but that in places populated only by such persons He does not even choose to manifest in special incarnations, and that their attaining Him will not occur in any short span of time. It may also indicate degrees among devotees: to some He becomes exceedingly near and visible; to others He is accessible only through hearing and reflection and is not yet directly present.

“Reclining upon Ananta” can indicate His being directly visible; “forest-grown tulasī garland” can indicate that even when unseen He may be inferred or recognized through its fragrance. “O blessed One” marks Him as the cause of attainment of what is desired; “O pure One” marks Him as the cause of removal of what is undesired. Punam means forest or uncultivated land; wild tulasī is especially fragrant. Punam can also mean ornament.

Thus He can bear many forms, exist in many places, and become directly visible to many devotees even within this world. His colors therefore cannot be denied on the ground that a form must be local and inaccessible. The statement that “the world conceals Him” must be understood as applying in the predominant case of worldly people, not absolutely to every person.

Pāsuram 46

Tamil Mūlam

தோடுபெற்ற தண்டுழா யலங்கலாடு சென்னியாய்
கோடுபற்றி யாழியேந்தி யஞ்சிறைப்புள்ளூர்தியால்
நாடுபெற்ற நன்மைநண்ண மில்லையேனு நாயினேன்
வீடுபெற் றிறப்பொடும் பிறப்பறுக்கு மாசொலே.

IAST Transliteration

tōṭupeṟṟa taṇṭuḻā yalaṅkalāṭu ceṉṉiyāy
kōṭupaṟṟi yāḻiyēnti yañciṟaippuḷḷūrtiyāl
nāṭupeṟṟa naṉmainaṇṇa millaiyēṉu nāyiṉēṉ
vīṭupeṟ ṟiṟappoṭum piṟappaṟukku mācolē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord whose crown is stirred by the cool tulasī garland with its flower-petals! Holding the conch and bearing the discus, You ride the beautiful-winged King of Birds. Though I, low as a dog, did not attain the blessed fortune enjoyed by those who lived in the lands of those incarnations, tell me the way by which I may attain liberation and cut off both death and birth.

English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord is thus near and is even seen by some, yet the Āḻvār laments that he himself has not seen even one of those divine colors. He grieves that he did not have the fortune to be born in the times when the Matsya, Kūrma, and other vibhava manifestations were present, and asks in bewilderment how he can now attain the Lord.

Word-by-word gloss. “Possessing petals,” “the cool tulasī garland,” “dancing upon the crown” — O One whose divine head is adorned by the swaying garland. “Holding the horn/conch” — placing Pāñcajanya in one divine hand; “bearing the discus” in another. “The beautiful-winged bird,” the King of Birds, Garuḍa; “as vehicle,” whom You cause to move whenever needed. “I, dog-like and lowly,” although “without attaining” “the good fortune obtained by the people of those lands” — the blessings enjoyed by people alive at the times of Your incarnations — “tell [me] the way” by which, “obtaining liberation,” I may “cut off death together with birth.”

When You nodded Your crowned head in grace toward those who sought You, they saw the beauty of the swaying tulasī, the beauty of the conch and discus, and the grandeur of the great wings of Garuḍa, and thereby obtained immense benefit. Others in later times too may obtain it. But I was born in the interval—neither in those earlier incarnational times nor in some future direct manifestation—and seem destined simply to die after this middle birth. Therefore tell me what path remains by which my cycle of death and birth can cease.

Naṇṇam means approaching or attainment. “I, a dog” means “I, one lowly like a dog.” Under pāsuram 110 Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai explains the expression vividly: “I whose nature is like one who enters every open doorway, is despised at every doorway entered, and is of such a kind that even if Your Reverence lovingly touches me, disgrace seems to accrue to that very place.”

Pāsuram 47

Tamil Mūlam

காரொடொத்த மேனிகங்கள் கண்ண!விண்ணினாதனே!
நீரிடத்த ராவணைக் கிடத்தியென்ப ரன்றியும்
ஓரிடத்தை யல்லையெல்லை யில்லையென்ப ராதலால்
சேர்விட்த்தை நாயினேன் தெரிந்திறைஞ்சு மாசொலே.

IAST Transliteration

kāroṭotta mēṉikaṅkaḷ kaṇṇa!viṇṇiṉātaṉē!
nīriṭatta rāvaṇaik kiṭattiyeṉpa raṉṟiyum
ōriṭattai yallaiyellai yillaiyeṉpa rātalāl
cērviṭttai nāyiṉēṉ terintiṟaiñcu mācolē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O our Kṛṣṇa whose body is like a dark rain-cloud! O Lord of the supreme heaven! They say You recline upon the serpent-couch in the midst of the waters; and they also say You are not confined to any one place and that there is no limit to Your locations or manifestations. Therefore tell me how I, low as a dog, am to determine the place where I may reach You and worship.

English Translation of the Commentary

There are many divine forms, yet the Āḻvār feels that none presents itself as a readily attainable foothold for one as sinful as himself. He therefore asks: which form am I to choose as the object into which I can plunge?

Word-by-word gloss. “Body like the cloud” — O One whose divine body resembles the dark rain-cloud. “Our Kṛṣṇa” — our Kṛṣṇa who was born in this age. “O Lord of heaven” — O resident and Master of Paramapada. “In the water,” in the midst of the ocean, “on the serpent-couch,” Ādiśeṣa, “they say You lie.” “And not only [there].” “You are not [One] of one place” — You do not possess only a single location. “There is no limit” — there is no boundary such as “this many forms for this long in each place,” they say. “Therefore,” because Your manifestations are innumerable, “determine the place of approach” — decide which among them is the place I should reach. “Tell me how I, dog-like and lowly, may worship.”

Among the four colors recalled in pāsuram 44, the phrase “body like the cloud” shows that the Āḻvār’s attraction has become especially fixed upon the Lord’s natural dark hue appropriate to this age.

Some explain “there is no limit” through contemplative forms such as the Dahara, Śāṇḍilya, and Vaiśvānara upāsanās: the scriptural loci in which the Lord may be contemplated are themselves without simple numerical limit.

The emotional implication of this whole stretch of pāsurams is that the Āḻvār has found none of the para, vyūha, vibhava, or hārda manifestations sufficiently suited to his present need for immediate access. This prepares the turn to arcā.

Pāsuram 48

Tamil Mūlam

குன்றினின்று வானிருந்து நீள்கடற் கிடந்துமண்
ஒன்று சென்ற தொன்றையுண்ட தொன்றிடந்து பன்றியாய்
நன்றுசென்ற நாளவற்றுள் நல்லுயிர் படைத்தவர்க்கு
அன்றுதேவ மைத்தளித்த ஆதிதேவ னல்லையே.

IAST Transliteration

kuṉṟiṉiṉṟu vāṉiruntu nīḷkaṭaṟ kiṭantumaṇ
oṉṟu ceṉṟa toṉṟaiyuṇṭa toṉṟiṭantu paṉṟiyāy
naṉṟuceṉṟa nāḷavaṟṟuḷ nalluyir paṭaittavarkku
aṉṟutēva maittaḷitta ātitēva ṉallaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Standing upon the sacred hill, abiding in heaven, reclining in the vast ocean, entering one earth, swallowing that same one, and, becoming the Boar, raising that same one up—are You not the Primal God who, in the good days that thus pass, creates worthy souls and on each such day establishes and grants them the divine form suited to their capacity?

English Translation of the Commentary

When the Āḻvār concludes that none of the previously contemplated forms gives him a stable place of access, the Lord turns his mind toward the arcā forms. Fixing upon one such form, the Āḻvār recalls the forms already contemplated, praises the Lord’s supremacy, and lovingly reveals His supreme beneficence: in every age He grants many manifestations suited to the capacities of those who live then.

Word-by-word gloss. “Standing on the hill” — graciously standing in divine posture at Tiruvēṅkaṭam. “Abiding in heaven” — remaining in Paramapada. “Reclining in the long ocean” — lying in the vast Milk Ocean. “Going into one earth” — incarnating at a particular place on land just as He manifests in the waters. “Swallowing that same one” — keeping the world caught in dissolution within His divine stomach. “Becoming the Boar,” “raising that same one” — lifting the earth oppressed by Hiraṇyākṣa. “Among the days that pass well in this manner,” “creating good souls” — producing qualified persons who devote themselves to You. “Then,” on each respective occasion, “for them,” the persons so produced, “establishing a deity/form and granting it” — providing a manifestation suited to their qualification. “Are You not the Primal Deity?” — are You not the God of gods who does all this?

It is fitting to turn away from vibhavas, which disappear swiftly like floodwater under the open sky, and become established in the enduring arcā. You who govern the Līlā-vibhūti never abandon Your sporting hill, Tiruvēṅkaṭam. Even at cosmic dissolution, that non-material holy hill is placed elsewhere beyond destruction and You remain manifest there. Thus, while standing at Tirumalai itself, You perform all functions concerning both vibhūtis. This makes the arcā there supremely excellent.

You who manifest in each era also establish the mode by which persons of different capacities can resort to a divine form appropriate to them. The implied prayer is therefore: “For this present age and for me too, devise and grant some path. If Your intention is, ‘Worship Me at Tirumalai,’ that is well; if it is some other manifestation that issues from there, that too is well. Only grant me a form I can approach.”

Alternative explanations are also given. “Going into the earth” may mean measuring the earth in the Trivikrama incarnation. “Among the days that went well” may mean the exalted days of the Varāha-kalpa. And “deity” may, in a secondary reading, denote other deities established for persons pursuing particular desired fruits (kāmya ends), within the cosmic order arranged by the Supreme.

Pāsuram 49

Tamil Mūlam

கொண்டைகொண்ட கோதைமீது தேனுலாவுகூனிகூன்
உண்டைகொண்டரங்கவோட்டி யுள்மகிழ்ந்தநாதனூர்
நண்டையுண்டுநாரைபேர வாளை பாயநீலமே
அண்டைகொண்டுகெண்டைமேயு மந்தணீரரங்கமே.

IAST Transliteration

koṇṭaikoṇṭa kōtaimītu tēṉulāvukūṉikūṉ
uṇṭaikoṇṭaraṅkavōṭṭi yuḷmakiḻntanātaṉūr
naṇṭaiyuṇṭunāraipēra vāḷai pāyanīlamē
aṇṭaikoṇṭukeṇṭaimēyu mantaṇīraraṅkamē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

The Lord whose heart rejoiced after driving away the hunch of the hunchbacked woman—bees roaming for honey over the flower-garland set upon her coiffure—as effortlessly as a pellet flies from a pellet-bow, has His abode here: Śrīraṅgam, with beautiful cool waters where the heron, having eaten the shore-crabs, moves away; the vāḷai fish leaps in fear; and the keṇḍai fish grazes securely with the dark water-lily as its shelter.

English Translation of the Commentary

When Tiruvēṅkaṭam comes to the mind of the Āḻvār, who is seeking a form distinct from the transient vyūha and vibhava manifestations, his longing for arcā forms surges forth. Every arcā manifestation appears exalted to him. Among them he first becomes deeply absorbed, in several pāsurams, in Periya Perumāḷ, Lord Raṅganātha.

The fact that Tiruvēṅkaṭam was contemplated before great arcā forms such as Periya Perumāḷ calls to mind an explanation attributed to Piḷḷai Tirunaṟaiyūr Araiyar. Asked why Tiruppāṇāḻvār, while singing of “the Lord of lofty-walled Raṅgam” in Amalan Ātipirān, first praises “the Lord of fragrant-groved Veṅkaṭam,” he replied: “When composing poetry about a person, one should narrate his history. He came from Śrī Vaikuṇṭha, stayed at Tirumalai, and only then came to Kōyil, Śrīraṅgam.”

Word-by-word gloss. “The coiffure,” the tied hair; “upon the garland placed [there],” “bees roaming” for honey; “the hunchback,” Trivakrā; “her hunch,” the bodily crookedness. “Taking it as a pellet” from a pellet-bow, “driving it completely away,” and “rejoicing inwardly.” “The abode of that Lord,” Kṛṣṇa, is this place. “Having eaten the crabs” moving near the bank, “the heron moves away”; “the vāḷai fish leaps” in fear. “Taking only the dark water-lily as its nearby shelter,” “the keṇḍai fish grazes.” Possessing such “beautiful cool waters,” the place is Śrīraṅgam itself.

The Āḻvār had been absorbed in Kṛṣṇa—“our Kṛṣṇa whose body is like the dark cloud”—and now, searching for the place where that same Kṛṣṇa abides in another form after the vibhava period, declares that His dwelling is Śrīraṅgam.

Trivakrā was hurrying to carry sandal-paste to Kaṃsa, adorned with an excellent flower-cluster over which bees wandered for honey. Kṛṣṇa won her over, received the sandal-paste from her with delight, and immediately decided to straighten her hunch and make her body as beautiful as the flower-adorned hair above it. He pressed her feet with the toes of His divine foot, placed two fingers beneath her chin, and raised her body. The hunch vanished. The image is of a pellet released from a pellet-bow: once shot, one cannot see where it has gone, while the bow itself springs straight again; so her hunch disappeared and her body became erect.

The Lord thus takes the initiative in favoring one who has approached Him, regards even the slightest service as immense, and, as the emperor of gratitude (kṛtajña-sārvabhauma), bestows grace that makes the person flourish permanently. The divine place where such a Lord abides is Śrīraṅgam.

The natural scene can itself be read as an evocative allegory of the Kṛṣṇa episode. The heron, like Kaṃsa, eats the shore-crabs, comparable to the Yādavas, and then moves about in apparent kingship. The vāḷai fish move fearfully under his dominance. Trivakrā, like the keṇḍai fish, though driven away with the rebuke “Will you give Kaṃsa’s sandal to this thief?”, found the dark-blue Lord as the keṇḍai finds the dark-blue water-lily for shelter and grazed joyfully under His protection. It is as though the flowing Kāverī itself recalls and reenacts that history. Hence this truly is His city.

Some explain the first lines instead with reference to Rāma. The story is that in childhood, while playing with a pellet-bow, Rāma shot a pellet at Mantharā’s hunch, and she later retaliated by working to deprive him of the kingdom. That story is not in the Vālmīki Rāmāyaṇa but is found in traditions such as Kampaṉ’s. One might suppose that Nammāḻvār’s line “You shot from the pellet-bow so that the hunch was shattered, O Govinda!” and this pāsuram serve as sources for the tradition.

Yet, as teachers observe, the Tiruvāymoḻi context does not fit that Rāma story especially well. Its words “so that it was shattered,” “with the skill [of the pellet-bow],” and especially “O Govinda” accord better, in the manner of the traditional six-thousand-word commentary, with Trivakrā. The same preference applies here. It is not fitting to set aside the Lord’s loving favor toward a dependent and instead make canonical a story of conduct that seems less consonant with Rāma’s character, then treat that as the chief object of contemplation.

Moreover, though “with the pellet” would be grammatically possible, “driving the hunch thoroughly away” does not fit Mantharā: the story says he struck her hump, not that he removed it. One could, of course, invent a relish in which Rāma inwardly rejoiced because the hunch remained firm enough to perform its later role in sending him to the forest. But that is not the natural force of the line.

If the Trivakrā episode is retained, how should “taking the pellet” be connected? One possibility is to understand “receiving the ball/lump of sandal-paste from her.” Or uṇṭai can be the lump of the hunch itself, grasped with the left hand: while His right hand raised her chin, He struck or pressed the hunch inward with the left. Another possibility is that Nammāḻvār’s “with the skill of the pellet-bow” later made explicit the simile latent in the present “taking it like a pellet.”

If one nevertheless insists on the Rāma story, the verse can be read humorously: “The place of Rāma, whose behavior once frightened those nearby, is clearly Raṅgam; for here too one sees crabs eaten by herons, vāḷai fish trembling, and keṇḍai fish sheltering.” Perhaps for this reason Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai makes the inner allegorical meaning rather than the literal episode central to the first lines, saying that the hunch is egoism and the Lord effortlessly removes it.

One might further defend the Rāma story by saying that, intending ultimately to protect His dependents by going to the forest, He committed a small childish mischief that would provide a chain of causes, and also demonstrated that childhood play before the age of moral responsibility is not sinful. The neighboring sequence alternates Rāma and Kṛṣṇa themes, so the verse may intentionally admit a shared resonance.

Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai also gives an inner meaning for the latter half to answer the question, “Is it fitting for the Āḻvār to spend attention describing herons eating crabs?” The heron is egoism, which consumes saṃsārins. The vāḷai fish are striving devotees who fear egoism. The keṇḍai are those who have surrendered and live by taking refuge in the Lord in a way that gives no room either to egoism or to anxious self-effort.

That inner meaning may stand. There is no inherent impropriety in describing crabs and herons while depicting a holy place: to describe a river naturally includes its living ecology. Some say even the animals in divine places are to be regarded, according to the elders, as Nityasūris who have come from the Nitya-vibhūti. Others reply that if one fully assimilates them to Nityasūris, one would expect only singing, dancing, and delightful divine acts to be mentioned, not herons eating crabs and fish trembling. The simplest answer is that a river-scene is being described as it naturally appears. Still, when one asks why the Āḻvār lovingly selected precisely these details rather than more conventionally exalted ones, a deeper symbolic interpretation is both legitimate and beautiful. We have therefore also offered our own interpretive connection above.

Pāsuram 50

Tamil Mūlam

வெண்டிரைக் கருங்கடல் சிவந்துவேவ முன்னொர்நாள்
திண்டிறற் சிலைக்கைவாளி விட்டவீரர் சேருமூர்
எண்டிசைக் கணங்களு மிறைஞ்சியாடு தீர்த்தநீர்
வண்டிரைத்த சோலைவேலி மன்னுசீரரங்கமே.

IAST Transliteration

veṇṭiraik karuṅkaṭal civantuvēva muṉṉornāḷ
tiṇṭiṟaṟ cilaikkaivāḷi viṭṭavīrar cērumūr
eṇṭicaik kaṇaṅkaḷu miṟaiñciyāṭu tīrttanīr
vaṇṭiraitta cōlaivēli maṉṉucīraraṅkamē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

The abode to which that great Hero comes—the One who, on a former day, discharged an arrow from His mighty bow-hand so that the dark ocean with its white waves reddened and boiled—is Śrīraṅgam of enduring glory, encircled by groves humming with bees and possessing the pure waters of the sacred Kāverī in which hosts from all eight directions bow and bathe.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. “The dark ocean,” deep and dark in appearance, “with white waves”; “so that it reddened and boiled.” “On one former day,” with “the bow-hand of firm strength,” “releasing an arrow.” “The Hero,” the great warrior Śrī Rāma; “the city He joins,” the place where He now graciously abides. “The groups in all eight directions,” every assembly from the eight quarters, “bowing” and “bathing in the sacred water” — the pure Kāverī. “With grove-walls where bees hum”; “of abiding excellence”; that place is Śrīraṅgam itself.

A very deep sea looks dark. When waves rise upon it, the water appears white both because water is naturally pale and because the rays of the blazing sun strike the crests. When the Ocean refused to yield, the arrow released from the bow in the hand of our Hero made even those two colors disappear and the sea become red. Rāma, intending to dry the ocean, employed the fiery āgneya-astra, so that the wave-beaten sea appeared to catch fire and burn.

Pāsuram 51

Tamil Mūlam

சரங்களைத் துரந்துவில் வளைத்திலங்கை மன்னவன்
சிரங்கள்பத் தறுத்துதிர்த்த செல்வர்மன்னு பொன்னிடம்
பறந்துபொன்னிறந்துநுந்தி வந்தலைக்கும் வார்புனல்
அரங்கமென்பர் நான்முகத்தயன்பணிந்த கோயிலே.

IAST Transliteration

caraṅkaḷait turantuvil vaḷaittilaṅkai maṉṉavaṉ
ciraṅkaḷpat taṟuttutirtta celvarmaṉṉu poṉṉiṭam
paṟantupoṉṉiṟantununti vantalaikkum vārpuṉal
araṅkameṉpar nāṉmukattayaṉpaṇinta kōyilē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

The golden place where the wealthy Lord, the Husband of Sītā, eternally abides—the One who bent His bow, discharged His arrows, cut off, and made fall all ten heads of the king of Laṅkā—is the temple once worshiped by four-faced Brahmā: Śrīraṅgam, where the broad flood of the Kāverī spreads everywhere, flows full, pushes along gold, and surges into every side.

English Translation of the Commentary

Having described in the preceding pāsuram the burning anger directed against the ocean, the Āḻvār now recounts the later event in which that anger reached its full fruit.

Word-by-word gloss. “Bending the bow,” setting the string and drawing it; “discharging arrows,” many kinds of shafts; “the king of Laṅkā,” Rāvaṇa; “his ten heads,” “cutting and making them fall.” “The wealthy Lord” — Śrīmān, the Lord accompanied by the wealth of victory and by Sītā. “The golden place where He permanently abides.” “Four-faced Brahmā,” “the temple he worshiped.” “Spreading,” “coming full and abundant,” “pushing gold along,” “surging/striking” everywhere; “the broad flood.” That place, they say, is Śrīraṅgam.

The word “wealthy” (celvar) indicates both the Goddess of victory and Sītā united with the victorious Lord. “Golden place”: it is as though the gold driven and deposited by the waves of the Ponni, the Kāverī, accumulated here and became Śrīraṅgam itself. Moreover, since Tiruvāraṅgam has gained the pre-eminent title simply “Kōyil,” “the Temple,” it is itself a golden and supremely precious place.

“The Ponni pushes gold.” Driving gold before itself is a fitting ceremonial gift offered by the river to the divine Bridegroom. When the flood becomes great, earth and sand are stirred up and carried in the Kāverī; that soil is mixed with particles having a golden luster. Hence the river gained names such as “Suvarṇa-nadī,” the Golden River, and “Ponni.”

“The temple worshiped by Brahmā.” The Lord who was worshiped by four-faced Brahmā, together with the divine vimāna called Śrīraṅga, later became the object of worship of the Ikṣvāku kings in Ayodhyā. Through Vibhīṣaṇa, Śrī Rāma caused that same Lord to come and abide upon the bank of the Kāverī. Therefore Śrīraṅgam is rightly called the temple worshiped by Brahmā.

Pāsuram 52

Tamil Mūlam

பொற்றையுற்றமுற்றல்யானை போரெதிர்ந்துவந்ததைப்
பற்றியுற்று மற்றதன் மருப்பொசித்த பாகனூர்
சிற்றெயிற்றுமுற்றல் மூங்கில் மூன்றுதண்டரொன்றினர்
அற்றபற்றர்சுற்றி வாழுமந்தணீரரங்கமே.

IAST Transliteration

poṟṟaiyuṟṟamuṟṟalyāṉai pōretirntuvantataip
paṟṟiyuṟṟu maṟṟataṉ maruppocitta pākaṉūr
ciṟṟeyiṟṟumuṟṟal mūṅkil mūṉṟutaṇṭaroṉṟiṉar
aṟṟapaṟṟarcuṟṟi vāḻumantaṇīraraṅkamē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

The abode of the young Kṛṣṇa who seized the fully grown, mountain-like elephant that came against Him for battle, engaged it at close quarters, and then broke its tusk, is Śrīraṅgam of beautiful cool waters, around which live detached ascetics bearing three staffs of mature bamboo with close-set joints, together with those who have joined themselves to such Bhagavatas.

English Translation of the Commentary

The previous verse praised the sacred river in which assemblies from all eight directions bow and bathe for desired fruits. Now the Āḻvār points to an even greater source of sanctity: those foremost, single-minded Bhagavatas who, carrying the Lord within, make sacred places themselves sacred, in accordance with the saying, “They make the tīrthas into tīrthas through the Club-bearing Lord dwelling in their hearts.” The place where such devotees abide is the abode of the Supreme who transformed persons such as Akrūra into supreme Bhagavatas.

Word-by-word gloss. “Having the character of a mountain,” or “belonging to the elephant-forest,” “the fully matured, exceedingly strong elephant,” Kuvalayāpīḍa; “coming against [Him] for battle.” “Seizing it,” taking hold of its trunk; “engaging closely” — entering and moving repeatedly between its legs and exhausting it; “further,” when it became still more enraged, “its tusk,” “breaking it.” “The abode of the boy,” the young cowherd Kṛṣṇa. “Small joints,” bamboos with close-set nodes, and “mature” canes; “three-staff bearers” — ascetics who carry a tridaṇḍa of such bamboo. “Those who have joined [them]” — those attached to such Bhagavatas. “Those whose attachments are cut off,” the detached. “Living around” [the place]. “Beautiful cool water”; the place is Śrīraṅgam itself.

Poṟṟai can mean a mountain. Some say it denotes an elephant-forest, a forest in which herds of elephants gather. “A mature elephant of the poṟṟai” therefore indicates one foremost among all elephants. Or “joined to the mountain” can mean mountain-like in bulk.

Pākan is sometimes interpreted “one who knows everything like a mahout.” More naturally, a lexicon gives pota/pāka and related forms in the sense of a young child, and that suits the dramatic contrast. When Kuvalayāpīḍa came to battle, Devakī, Vasudeva, and the others trembled on seeing a ferocious adult elephant rush upon what looked like a little boy. The verse deliberately calls the elephant “mature” and Kṛṣṇa “the boy” to evoke precisely that fear and His effortless victory.

“Small joints” means that the bamboo nodes stand close together. “Three-staff bearers” shows that these are renunciants who practice saṃnyāsa-dharma according to scripture. Since such renunciants themselves are already paramaikāntins, it is not satisfactory to make “those who are one” simply mean another class of paramaikāntins; “ekāntin” too would be almost the same. We therefore take the word to mean persons who have attached themselves to these Bhagavatas. Alternatively, because saṃnyāsins normally wander, “those who have become one [with the place]” may denote renunciants who, for the sake of permanent residence in the sacred field, remain settled there.

“The detached” explains why they live around the outskirts rather than seeking residence amid the bustle of the central city. In accordance with the discipline “resorting to solitary places,” they abandon crowds so that yoga and contemplation may flourish and live along the banks of Kāverī.

The verb “live” can grammatically be joined not only with those devotees but also with the water: the beautiful cool water “lives” or flourishes through association with them. On that reading, their presence makes the river itself a sacred tīrtha, which is why the adjective “beautiful” is especially apt.

Pāsuram 53

Tamil Mūlam

மோடியோடிலச்சையாய சாபமெய்திமுக்கணான்
கூடுசேனை மக்களோடு கொண்டுமண்டிவெஞ்சமத்து
ஓடவாணனாயிரம் கரம்கழித்த ஆதிமால்
பீடுகோயில் கூடுநீரரங்கமென்ற பேரதே.

IAST Transliteration

mōṭiyōṭilaccaiyāya cāpameytimukkaṇāṉ
kūṭucēṉai makkaḷōṭu koṇṭumaṇṭiveñcamattu
ōṭavāṇaṉāyiram karamkaḻitta ātimāl
pīṭukōyil kūṭunīraraṅkameṉṟa pēratē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Śrīraṅgam is the glorious temple of the Primal Māl, Kṛṣṇa—the source even of Brahmā and Rudra—who made three-eyed Rudra, carrying his bow and accompanied by the bashful, cursed Moḍi, his sons, and the assembled army, rush away from the fierce battle, and who then cut down Bāṇa’s thousand arms. Its sacred waters are filled with gathered multitudes and supreme devotees.

English Translation of the Commentary

After praising Śrīraṅgam through the greatness of its sacred water and through the Bhagavatas who sanctify that water, the Āḻvār now declares that even the Bhagavatas derive their greatness from their relation to Bhagavān. He identifies that Bhagavān as Kṛṣṇa, who made clear the subordinate status of other deities and their followers, and who then graciously arranged that they should live with devotion toward Him and toward those connected with Him.

Word-by-word gloss. “Having obtained a cāpa” — bearing a bow; “becoming ashamed”; “the three-eyed one,” Rudra. “Together with Moḍi,” who is either a fierce goddess or a personified power, “having been cursed,” “embodiment of shame”; “together with his sons,” Gaṇapati, Subrahmaṇya, and others; “the gathered army” that came with them; “taking [them] along,” “rushing,” and “fleeing from the fierce battle.” “Bāṇa,” Bāṇāsura; “his thousand arms,” “cutting them off” with the discus. “The Primal Māl” — Kṛṣṇa-Viṣṇu, cause even of Brahmā and Rudra. “The temple of greatness.” “With waters in which crowds assemble” — sacred waters where multitudes and paramaikāntins gather; bearing the name “Śrīraṅgam.”

The narrative is as follows. Uṣā, daughter of Bāṇāsura, received through Pārvatī’s grace the knowledge that the man who appeared to her in a dream would be her husband. Her friend Citralekhā, skilled in painting, identified the dream-man from portraits as Aniruddha, the divine grandson of Kṛṣṇa. By yogic power Citralekhā secretly brought Aniruddha into Uṣā’s inner apartments, where the two lived together in delight.

The guards eventually discovered this and informed Bāṇa. He fought Aniruddha and finally bound him with serpent-missiles. Nārada reported everything to Kṛṣṇa, who came with a massed army to subdue Bāṇāsura.

Rudra had granted Bāṇa boons, a thousand arms, and immense strength; had stationed his companion Agni as though he were a fortification around Bāṇa’s city; and continuously guarded him together with his retinue. Thus Rudra entered the battle with his sons, Moḍi, and a great host. Kṛṣṇa’s forces humiliated them in many ways. Rudra became ashamed and fled swiftly with those who had accompanied him. Kṛṣṇa then used the divine discus to cut off nearly all the arms that Rudra’s boon had bestowed on Bāṇa, thereby stripping away the pride of Śiva and Śaiva followers. The temple of such Viṣṇu is Śrīraṅgam, filled with waters in which the foremost Vaiṣṇavas gather and bathe.

“Moḍi” can mean Durgā, who is certainly capable in battle and may therefore be pictured as coming with Rudra. Strictly, however, moḍi can also denote a hatchet-like fierce female power, Koṭarī. “Embodiment of shame” can qualify both Moḍi and the three-eyed Rudra.

Some explain “having obtained a curse” literally: Durgā is “cursed” in the sense that supreme single-minded devotees do not worship her, while Śiva was cursed by Brahmā to become a skull-bearer. But those curses have no immediate connection with this battle. Therefore in the principal gloss we take cāpam in its other sense, “bow”: Paśupati fled with the bow he had brought grasped in one hand, and Durgā in the other, along with his sons and troops.

When “cursed” is connected specifically with Moḍi, however, the elders’ interpretation gains strong textual support. In Śrī Viṣṇucitta’s commentary on Viṣṇu Purāṇa, while explaining the episode of Bāṇa’s war, a passage from the Gomanta section of the Harivaṃśa is cited concerning the naked Koṭarī who stood before Kṛṣṇa. Bhagavān curses her in substance: “The great fruit obtained by one who worships Me with devotion for twelve years shall be destroyed by bowing once to you. May you always remain naked; stand or go before Me so.” The text thus explicitly describes Koṭarī as cursed so that bowing to her destroys accumulated merit, and as doomed always to nakedness. The same commentary identifies this Koṭarī as a power of Gaurī. Hence Koṭarī can well be the “Moḍi” of the pāsuram.

“Three-eyed” also hints that the fire residing in Rudra’s third eye served him, forming a wall around Bāṇa’s city and participating in the battle. “Fierce battle” is veñ-camam; camam/samaram can itself mean war.

“Primal Māl.” The name Māl not only identifies Viṣṇu/Kṛṣṇa but evokes His overflowing affection. After Kṛṣṇa had crushed Rudra’s forces and cut away most of Bāṇa’s arms, the three-eyed god bowed at His feet and begged Him to forgive the offenses and show grace to his devotee. Immediately Kṛṣṇa again displayed the same old affectionate concern toward Rudra and, for Rudra’s sake, toward Bāṇa, allowing Bāṇa to remain alive with four arms. The narrative thereby also suggests that Rudra and his followers afterward conducted themselves devotedly toward Kṛṣṇa and those connected with Him, such as Aniruddha; they honored Kṛṣṇa and His retinue and gave Uṣā in marriage. Pīḍu means greatness or dignity.

Pāsuram 54

Tamil Mūlam

இலைத்தலைச் சரந்துரந்திலங்கை கட்டழித்தவன்
மலைத்தலைப் பிறந்திழிந்து வந்துநுந்து சந்தனம்
குலைத்தலைத்திறுத்தெறிந்த குங்குமக்குழம்பினோடு
அலைத்தொழுகுகாவிரி யரங்கமேய வண்ணலே.

IAST Transliteration

ilaittalaic caranturantilaṅkai kaṭṭaḻittavaṉ
malaittalaip piṟantiḻintu vantununtu cantaṉam
kulaittalaittiṟutteṟinta kuṅkumakkuḻampiṉōṭu
alaittoḻukukāviri yaraṅkamēya vaṇṇalē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord who fittingly abides in Śrīraṅgam, beside the Kāverī that is born high upon Sahya mountain, descends and flows a long distance, pushes along sandal trees, batters, breaks, and casts them into its waters, and surges onward mingled with the paste of saffron creepers—You are that Rāma who discharged broad leaf-headed arrows and destroyed the defenses of Laṅkā.

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār has declared, chiefly through Rāma and Kṛṣṇa, that Śrīraṅgam is the place where the Lord is available for present experience. Āṇḍāḷ seems to follow this very mode when she says, “The city cherished by the Lord of the Lady is great Raṅgam.” Having first enjoyed “His place is Raṅgam,” the Āḻvār now reverses the emphasis for two pāsurams: “The One who abides in Raṅgam is that very Lord.” The first of the two contemplates Him as Rāma.

Word-by-word gloss. “Leaf-headed arrows” — shafts with broad tips shaped like leaves; “discharging [them].” “Laṅkā,” “its defensive arrangement,” the military fortification; “the One who destroyed it,” Śrī Rāma. “At the head of the mountain,” Sahya; “being born and descending,” the Kāverī arising there and flowing downward; “coming” over a long distance. “Pushing sandalwood”; “battering,” “agitating,” “breaking,” and “casting” into its current both the sandal trees and the saffron growths; “together with the paste of saffron,” and the sandal paste produced by their grinding; “surging and flowing.” O Master who fittingly abides in Śrīraṅgam upon that Kāverī!

The sequence “battering, agitating, breaking, and casting” can be connected with both sandalwood and saffron. The Kāverī thus appears to serve the emperor’s son Rāma with sandal paste, in the manner of the Rāmāyaṇa description of the Lord anointed with the most precious sandal. Having destroyed the king of Laṅkā and thereby gained Sītā, who is an incarnation of the daughter of the Ocean-king, He is ceremonially honored by Kāverī with these fragrant substances. “Mēya” means fitting, appropriate, or graciously dwelling.

Pāsuram 55

Tamil Mūlam

மன்னுமாமலர்க்கிழத்தி வையமங்கை மைந்தனாய்ப்
பின்னுமாயர் பின்னை தோள் மணம் புணர்ந்ததன்றியும்
உன்ன பாத மென்ன சிந்தை மன்னவைத்து நல்கினாய்
பொன்னி சூழரங்கமேய புண்டரீகனல்லையே.

IAST Transliteration

maṉṉumāmalarkkiḻatti vaiyamaṅkai maintaṉāyp
piṉṉumāyar piṉṉai tōḷ maṇam puṇarntataṉṟiyum
uṉṉa pāta meṉṉa cintai maṉṉavaittu nalkiṉāy
poṉṉi cūḻaraṅkamēya puṇṭarīkaṉallaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

You are the ever-youthful beloved of the great lotus-Lady who abides eternally upon the thousand-petaled flower and of the earth-maiden; and beyond that, You joyfully united with the shoulders of Nappinnai of the cowherd clan. Not stopping even there, You graciously placed Your own feet permanently within my mind. Are You not the supreme Puṇḍarīka who fittingly dwells in Śrīraṅgam, encircled on both sides by the Ponni?

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. “The great flower,” the thousand-petaled lotus; “the Lady who abides” there eternally, the rightful Mistress, Śrī Mahālakṣmī; “the earth-maiden,” Bhūdevī; “being the young beloved” of both. “And further,” “Nappinnai of the cowherds”; “uniting in marriage with her shoulders” in intense delight. “And not only that”: “Your feet,” “within my thought,” “causing them to remain permanently,” “You graciously bestowed [them].” Why? Because You are “the Puṇḍarīka,” the supremely excellent Lord “who fittingly dwells in Raṅgam,” surrounded on both sides by the Ponni/Kāverī.

In the transcendent form You are the object of enjoyment of both the Lotus-Lady and the Earth-Lady. When You incarnated as Kṛṣṇa, You additionally united with Nappinnai, the cowherd maiden. Had You stopped with only these divine Consorts, it would not have been possible for me even to contemplate You. But now You have shown affection toward me without drawing a distinction between me and those Goddesses and have established Your divine feet in my mind. This immense gain has come because You chose to abide in Śrīraṅgam.

“Granted” (nalkināy) is the finite verb, and the fourth line states the reason for that grant. Some instead construe: “O You who granted them! Not stopping even with this, You also became the Puṇḍarīka dwelling in Raṅgam.”

The feminine kiḻatti corresponds to masculine kiḻavaṉ. The latter can mean an elderly man, but it can also mean one who possesses or has rightful claim; here the feminine means “the rightful Lady.” The deeper sense is the Mistress who never abandons the Lord and who is also sovereign Lady of the whole world.

Here You are the maintan, the ever-young beloved, of the Lady and the Earth-maiden. Yet Your affection seems continually to move toward someone still “younger”: first Nappinnai, and now, the Āḻvār playfully suggests, the maiden that is my own mind. That maiden-mind has become a fully suitable recipient of the love of You, the Puṇḍarīka.

Pāsuram 56

Tamil Mūlam

இலங்கைமன்னன் ஐந்தொடைந்து பைந்தலை நிலத்துகக்
கலங்கவன்று சென்றுகொன்று வென்றிகொண்ட வீரனே!
விலங்குநூலர் வேதநாவர் நீதியான கேள்வியார்
வலங்கொளக் குடந்தையுட் கிடந்தாலு மல்லையே.

IAST Transliteration

ilaṅkaimaṉṉaṉ aintoṭaintu paintalai nilattukak
kalaṅkavaṉṟu ceṉṟukoṉṟu veṉṟikoṇṭa vīraṉē!
vilaṅkunūlar vētanāvar nītiyāṉa kēḷviyār
valaṅkoḷak kuṭantaiyuṭ kiṭantālu mallaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Hero who, going forth on that day, destroyed Your enemies and won victory so that the ten youthful, powerful heads—five and five—of the king of Laṅkā fell to the ground and his mind was thrown into confusion! Are You not also the loving Lord who reclines at Tirukkuḍandai so that wearers of the sacred thread, whose tongues bear the Veda and whose hearing of scripture proceeds rightly with reasoning, may gain strength and benefit?

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār, who had not been able to experience the incarnations extending through the vibhava forms, became absorbed in arcā, first taking hold of Periya Perumāḷ and praising seven-walled Raṅgam through seven pāsurams. He now reflects, “Is this the only arcā? You have manifested in many arcā forms.” In the next six pāsurams he delights in the thought that the same Māl has appeared as Ārāvamudan at Tirukkuḍandai.

Word-by-word gloss. “The king of Laṅkā,” Rāvaṇa; “five and five,” his ten; “fresh/young heads” — heads full of youth and strength; “so that they fell to earth,” and “so that he became confused.” “On that day going forth” — during the forest exile, going to Janasthāna; “slaying” enemies there and in Laṅkā according to Your resolve; “winning victory,” obtaining Victory-Śrī. “O Hero!” “Wearers of the crosswise thread” — those who wear the sacred thread upon the body; “those whose tongue bears the Veda”; “those whose hearing is proper and orderly” — persons who study śāstra in due method, including reasoning. “So that they obtain strength/benefit”; “reclining in Kuḍandai”; “are You not also that Māl,” the Lord passionately attached to devotees?

Because the Lord reclining at Tirukkuḍandai is Śārṅgapāṇi, the Bearer of Śārṅga, the Āḻvār begins by contemplating Him as Rāma, the divine wielder of that bow.

“Fresh heads.” Calling them “green” or fresh means heads possessing youth and vigor. Paittal can also indicate raging anger. “Going so that they fell and he was confused”: Rāma’s going to Janasthāna threw Rāvaṇa’s mind into confusion, and that confusion continued until his ten heads finally fell.

Why say “five and five” rather than simply “ten”? Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai explains that the wording hints at the spiritual interpretation of the Rāmāyaṇa in which Rāvaṇa’s ten heads symbolize the five organs of knowledge and the five organs of action. One can also understand the count in an ordinary visual way: taking two central heads as reference, five heads on the right and five on the left. The former explanation is more significant.

“Obtain valam.” Eminent Vedic persons directly experience Ārāvamudan and thereby gain strength and efficacy in their observances; they obtain many favorable results. The usage is comparable to “obtaining a boon.” Another explanation is reflexive from the Lord’s side: He abides there “in order to accept their valam,” their favorable conduct and growth in devotion.

The conjunctive particle in “Māl also” includes the arcā at Śrīraṅgam already contemplated: the same affectionate Lord who is Māl at Raṅgam is also the One who lies at Kuḍandai.

Pāsuram 57

Tamil Mūlam

சங்குதங்கு முன்கை நங்கை கொங்கைதங்க லுற்றவன்
அங்கமங்க வன்றுசென் றடர்த்தெறிந்தவாழியான்
கொங்குதங்கு வார்குழல் மடந்தைமார் குடைந்தநீர்
பொங்குதண் குடந்தையுட் கிடந்தபுண்டரீகனே.

IAST Transliteration

caṅkutaṅku muṉkai naṅkai koṅkaitaṅka luṟṟavaṉ
aṅkamaṅka vaṉṟuceṉ ṟaṭartteṟintavāḻiyāṉ
koṅkutaṅku vārkuḻal maṭantaimār kuṭaintanīr
poṅkutaṇ kuṭantaiyuṭ kiṭantapuṇṭarīkaṉē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

The supreme Puṇḍarīka who reclines in cool Tirukkuḍandai—where the sacred water swells as young women with long, fragrant hair plunge and bathe—is that wielder of the discus who, on a former day, went forth, pressed hard, and destroyed the body of the one whose desire was obstructed in relation to the breast of the noble Lady whose forearms bear bangles.

English Translation of the Commentary

The preceding verse prepares the thought that just as Kṛṣṇa favored the wives of the sages even more directly than the sages themselves, the Lord shows special grace toward women who approach Him in devotion.

Word-by-word gloss. “The noble Lady whose forearms bear conch-like bangles”; “in relation to her breasts,” “the one who encountered obstruction,” or the one whose mind became fixed there; “so that his limbs/body perished”; “on that day going,” “pressing hard and casting him down”; “the Lord of the discus who hurled [it].” “With fragrance abiding,” “long hair”; “young women,” “the water in which they plunge and bathe,” “swelling.” “In cool Kuḍandai,” “reclining”; “the Puṇḍarīka,” the supremely excellent Lord.

Earlier teachers take the first line as referring to Rāvaṇa’s destruction by Rāma. Then the preceding pāsuram emphasized Rāvaṇa’s imperial power, while this one stresses the crime that merited his death. But how is “the wielder who hurled the discus” to be reconciled with Rāma, who did not kill Rāvaṇa with the discus? One may say that all weapons are portions or manifestations of Sudarśana, so an arrow may be referred to through the lordship of the discus. Or, following an older gloss, take “Āḻiyān” as “the One whose body is like the ocean,” and connect “hurled” with an arrow rather than the discus: the ocean-bodied Lord cut and cast [Rāvaṇa] down with His arrows.

A more natural alternative is Śiśupāla. Prevented from marrying Rukmiṇī, he remained mentally agitated and later, at the Rājasūya sacrifice, poured forth abusive words. Kṛṣṇa, no longer tolerating this, caused figures such as Sahadeva to confront him and finally destroyed his body with the divine discus. On this reading, “whose desire was obstructed regarding the Lady’s breast” evokes the maiden Rukmiṇī and her marriage. The previous pāsuram concerned Rāma; this one can shift to the Kṛṣṇa narrative for the sake of the divine Lady.

The latter half paints even young women as coming in groups before the service of Śārṅgapāṇi, plunging into the Kāverī without concern that the fragrance of their long hair might be disturbed by the water. Commentators say that maṭantai, “young women,” may even signify divine women from Paramapada.

Pāsuram 58

Tamil Mūlam

மரங்கெட நடந்தடர்த்து மத்தயானை மத்தகத்
துரங்கெடப் புடைத்தோர்கொம்பொசித்துகந்த வுத்தமா
துரங்கம்வாய் பிளந்துமண் ணளந்தபாத வேதியர்
வரங்கொளக் குடந்தையுட் கிடந்தமாலு மல்லையே.

IAST Transliteration

maraṅkeṭa naṭantaṭarttu mattayāṉai mattakat
turaṅkeṭap puṭaittōrkompocittukanta vuttamā
turaṅkamvāy piḷantumaṇ ṇaḷantapāta vētiyar
varaṅkoḷak kuṭantaiyuṭ kiṭantamālu mallaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Supreme Person who crawled so that the twin maruta trees were destroyed, fought the rut-maddened elephant and struck its forehead until its strength failed, then broke off its peerless tusk and rejoiced! O Lord whose feet measured the earth after You split the mouth of the horse-demon! Are You not also that Māl who reclines at Tirukkuḍandai so that knowers of the Veda may obtain the boons they seek?

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār now wishes to experience Śārṅgapāṇi more intensely as Kṛṣṇa than as Rāma.

Word-by-word gloss. “The trees,” the twin maruta trees; “crawling so that they perished,” breaking them while moving as a child. “Fighting,” “the rut-maddened elephant,” Kuvalayāpīḍa; “upon its frontal globes,” “striking” until “its strength was destroyed”; “breaking one incomparable tusk,” and “rejoicing.” “O Uttama,” O Supreme Person! “Splitting the horse’s mouth,” Keśin’s; “O One whose foot measured the earth” in the Vāmana incarnation. “Those who know/study the Veda,” “so that they obtain desired boons”; “reclining in Kuḍandai”; “are You not also that Māl,” the Lord of overflowing love for devotees?

The pāsuram gathers Kṛṣṇa’s childhood feats, His destruction of Keśin, and the ancient Trivikrama stride and sees them all in the same Lord who now lies accessible at Tirukkuḍandai. The title “Uttama” is especially apt: even while acting like a crawling child or a cowherd youth in combat with animals, He remains the Supreme Person. His lying in Kuḍandai is for the benefit of Vedic persons who approach Him and obtain the boons appropriate to them.

Pāsuram 59

Tamil Mūlam

சாலிவேலி தண்வயல் தடங்கிடங்கு பூம்பொழில்
கோலமாட நீடுதண் குடந்தையே கோவலா!
காலனேமி வக்கரன் கான்முரன் சிரமவை
காலனோடு கூடவிற் குனித்தவிற்கை வீரனே!

IAST Transliteration

cālivēli taṇvayal taṭaṅkiṭaṅku pūmpoḻil
kōlamāṭa nīṭutaṇ kuṭantaiyē kōvalā!
kālaṉēmi vakkaraṉ kāṉmuraṉ ciramavai
kālaṉōṭu kūṭaviṟ kuṉittaviṟkai vīraṉē!

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O cowherd Lord who lovingly dwells in cool Tirukkuḍandai, long and beautiful with fields hedged by ripened rice, cool paddies, broad tanks, betel gardens, flower-groves, and splendid lofty mansions! O heroic master of the bow whose hand bent it so that the heads of Kālanemi, Dantavakra, and Mura went to join the Lord of Death!

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. “Long-extended,” “rice” — rich sāḷi/sambā crops; “cool fields,” “broad ponds,” “betel plots,” “flowering groves,” and “beautiful mansions” — all these characterize “cool Kuḍandai.” “O cowherd who lovingly dwells there!” “Kālanemi,” the asura associated with the Tārakāsura battle; “Vakkaran,” understood as Dantavakra; and “Mura,” the asura connected with Narakāsura. “Their heads,” “so that they joined Kāla,” the realm of Yama; “bending the bow.” “O heroic wielder whose hand bears the bow!” — O unsurpassed master of archery.

The Āḻvār usually states the Lord’s deeds in the earlier lines and the glory of the divine place in the later ones. Here he reverses the order, perhaps because he wished first to spread out the praise of Kuḍandai as fully as needed and then, with the space remaining, gather as many defeated enemies as possible into the concluding line.

The adjective “long/extended” may grammatically qualify all the features: long rice-fields, extensive cool paddies, broad ponds, lengthy betel gardens, flower-groves, and tall houses. The transcript’s vakkaran is understood as a form of vakran/vaktran and is traditionally identified here with Dantavakra.

Pāsuram 60

Tamil Mūlam

செழுங்கொழும் பெரும்பனி பொழிந்திட வுயர்ந்தவேய்
விழுந்துலர்ந்தெழுந்து விண் புடைக்கும்வேங்கடத்துணின்று
எழுந்திருந்து தேன்பொருந்து பூம்பொழிற் றழைக்கொழும்
செழுந்தடங் குடந்தையுட் கிடந்தமாலு மல்லையே.

IAST Transliteration

ceḻuṅkoḻum perumpaṉi poḻintiṭa vuyarntavēy
viḻuntularnteḻuntu viṇ puṭaikkumvēṅkaṭattuṇiṉṟu
eḻuntiruntu tēṉporuntu pūmpoḻiṟ ṟaḻaikkoḻum
ceḻuntaṭaṅ kuṭantaiyuṭ kiṭantamālu mallaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Are You not also the loving Māl who, after standing at Veṅkaṭam where the tall, luxuriant, thick bamboos are cast down by the heavy, dense mist and then, when dried by the sun, rise again to strike the sky, reclines in fertile Tirukkuḍandai of great tanks and flower-groves with luxuriant leaves, where bees emerge from closing flowers and settle in those that open, dwelling there every day?

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār now gathers standing, moving, and reclining forms together and praises the Māl who lies at Kuḍandai.

Word-by-word gloss. “Luxuriant and stout,” “exceedingly tall,” “bamboos”; “the dense, luxuriant great mist” pouring down repeatedly; because of it the bamboos “fall” to the ground in the night; then “dry,” as the sun’s rays rise and the mist clears; “rise” again to their former height and “strike the sky.” “Within Veṅkaṭam,” “standing” in the standing posture. “Honey/bees”: “emerging” from flowers that close, “remaining” in flowers as they open, and “joining” them day after day; “flowering groves” with “thick luxuriant foliage”; “great tanks”; “within fertile Kuḍandai,” “reclining.” “Are You not also that Māl,” the Lord infatuated with His devotees?

The cowherd Lord who stands on Tiruvēṅkaṭa hill in order to bless gods and humans is the very One who lies in Tirukkuḍandai.

Through the fertility of Tiruvēṅkaṭam’s earth and the divine glance of Lakṣmī’s Lord, the bamboos grow upward to the sky as though calling the immortals to come. When thick fog presses them down upon the mountain, they seem to say, “You too perform full prostration!” At dawn, when the sun dries them, they rise again.

Upon that mountain, where bamboos as high as mountains repeatedly fall and stand, the Lord remains standing in accordance with the scriptural image, “He stands in heaven, firm like a tree, the One.” Because there are beings who have not yet bowed, He seems to remain standing until all have been made to bow; and meanwhile, as though bowing in gracious condescension to all, Lakṣmī’s Lord shines there in the standing posture. This is the sense of the first half.

In the latter half, the word “honey” also suggests the Nityasūris who come and sport in the groves. Because the text inserts the description of the mist between “luxuriant/thick” and the tall bamboos, earlier teachers naturally attached those adjectives first to the mist itself: ceḻumai indicates its long duration, koḻumai its dense, heavy fall. A fog strong enough to bend tall bamboo implicitly establishes the luxuriance and thickness of the bamboo even without separately adjectivizing it.

If “rising and remaining” is connected with “reclining,” the phrase recalls in another way the familiar paradox “rising from the manner in which He lay,” which appears later in the work. Taḻai here is a noun, leaf or foliage; if it were a verb meaning “to flourish,” the consonantal form in the compound would differ. Taṭam means pond or tank.

Pāsuram 61

Tamil Mūlam

கடந்தகால்கள் கொந்தவோ நடுங்கஞால மேனமாய்
இடந்தமெய் குலுங்கவோ விலங்குமால் வரைச்சுரம்
கடந்தகால் பரந்தகாவிரிக்கரைக் குடந்தையுள்
கிடந்தவா றெழுந்திருந்து பேசுவாழி கேசனே.

IAST Transliteration

kaṭantakālkaḷ kontavō naṭuṅkañāla mēṉamāy
iṭantamey kuluṅkavō vilaṅkumāl varaiccuram
kaṭantakāl parantakāvirikkaraik kuṭantaiyuḷ
kiṭantavā ṟeḻuntiruntu pēcuvāḻi kēcaṉē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Keśa, O Lord of beautiful hair! In Tirukkuḍandai on the bank of the Kāverī—which crosses obstructing great mountains and forests and deserts and spreads into many broad channels—why do You lie in this manner? Did the feet that walked so far in incarnations such as Rāma become sore? Or did Your body grow weary when, as the Boar, You raised up the trembling earth? Rise and sit up and tell me! Yet—may You flourish just as You lie!

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār beautifully suggests that one can infer from the very way Ārāvamudan lies at Kuḍandai that He is the same Lord who performed the immense labors of the other incarnations.

Word-by-word gloss. “O Keśa!” — O One distinguished by beautiful hair. “Crossing” the “great mountains” that stand as obstacles, and “forests/deserts”; “with channels spread wide,” the Kāverī possessing many broad distributaries; “on the bank,” “within Kuḍandai.” “The manner in which You lie”: was it because “the feet that walked” great distances in the Rāma and other incarnations “became sore”? Or, when “the world trembled” because Hiraṇyākṣa had carried it away, You “became the Boar” and “raised it”—did “Your body tremble/become exhausted” from that effort? “Rise and sit, and speak,” so that I may know. Yet, “may You live!” — remain flourishing in the very reclining posture.

Some include the Trivikrama measurement under “the feet that walked.” In general every incarnation in which true walking occurred may be intended. The second possibility is greater still: not merely the feet but the whole body may have become weary. Walking over a stony and thorny earth is strenuous; lifting the entire earth is far more so.

“Rise, sit, and speak.” If You answer while continuing to lie down, I will fear that Your pain is severe. If You rise and sit while speaking, I can take comfort that the fatigue is less. Such is the Āḻvār’s tender concern.

Tradition says that Ārāvamudan originally reclined fully, like Raṅganātha. Hearing the Āḻvār’s command “rise and sit and speak,” He began to rise, breaking the immobility proper to the arcā image in order to bless His devotee. The Āḻvār, seeing the beauty of that very act, immediately interrupted with the benediction “may You flourish!” and thus made Him stop midway. Hence He gives darśana in the distinctive utthāna-śayana, the reclining posture as though in the act of rising. This also answers the question, “The Āḻvār asked Him to rise—why did He not?” He did begin to rise; the Āḻvār’s own maṅgalāśāsana stopped Him.

Another version says: He was already reclining in this semi-risen posture. On hearing “rise and sit,” He intended to rise fully; the Āḻvār then said “may You flourish [as You are]” to prevent it. Yet another construction joins “rise and sit” with “the way You lie”: “Why do You lie as though partly risen?” The Āḻvār interprets the pose as an eagerness to rise obstructed by fatigue and blesses Him so that the fatigue may vanish.

Elsewhere the Lord commonly breaks the stillness of arcā, speaks with the Āḻvār, approaches, and interacts. Ārāvamudan alone seemed to remain silently reclining, and this may be why the Āḻvār questions Him so. Compare the earlier “Tell me so that I may understand how You lay there” in pāsuram 20.

Nontha is a past plural finite form from the verb “to suffer/be sore,” without the usual personal ending. Kuluṅga is a verbal participle. Curam can mean forest or desert. “Keśa” may be used as a shortened form corresponding to Keśava, with a suffix taught in grammar. A theological derivation is also possible: O inner ruler of Rudra, who is himself the inner-ruler-dependent of Brahmā designated by the syllable ka.

Pāsuram 62

Tamil Mūlam

கரண்டமாடு பொய்கையுட் கரும்பனைப் பெரும்பழம்
புரண்டுவீழ வாளைபாய் குறுங்குடி நெடுந்தகாய்!
திரண்டதோ ளிரணியன் சினங்கொளாக மொன்றையும்
இரண்டுகூறு செய்துகந்த சிங்கமென்ப துன்னையே.

IAST Transliteration

karaṇṭamāṭu poykaiyuṭ karumpaṉaip perumpaḻam
puraṇṭuvīḻa vāḷaipāy kuṟuṅkuṭi neṭuntakāy!
tiraṇṭatō ḷiraṇiyaṉ ciṉaṅkoḷāka moṉṟaiyum
iraṇṭukūṟu ceytukanta ciṅkameṉpa tuṉṉaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord of great excellence at Tirukkuṟuṅkuṭi, where dark, great palmyra fruits fall and roll into ponds in which water-crows play, making the vāḷai fish leap away thinking them birds! They say that the Lion who delighted after splitting into two the single wrath-filled body of Hiraṇya, whose shoulders were massive with strength and training, is none other than You.

English Translation of the Commentary

After enjoying Ārāvamudan, the Āḻvār becomes absorbed in another arcā manifestation, the Lion-Lord at Tirukkuṟuṅkuṭi.

Word-by-word gloss. “Water-crows,” “in the pond where they play and dive.” “The dark, great palmyra fruit,” “rolling and falling” into the pond; the “vāḷai fish leaps” elsewhere, mistaking it for a water-crow. “O One of great stature/excellence at Kuṟuṅkuṭi!” “With massive shoulders” — Hiraṇya’s shoulders thickened by strength and training. “His wrath-filled body,” “that one incomparable body also,” “making two portions,” splitting it in two. “The Lion who delighted,” rejoicing because the danger to Prahlāda and the devotees had ended—“they say that is You alone.”

“Great excellence” (neḍuntakai) can indicate greatness, beauty, or graciousness; compare peruntakāy in Tiruvāymoḻi 3.8.10. When one sees the way You now stand manifest here, it becomes evident that You Yourself were the One who tore Hiraṇya apart. The marvel is that Hiraṇya, though strong-shouldered, failed to fear even when he actually saw before him the One capable of splitting him into two and was therefore destroyed.

The pond scene follows the proverb “to the frightened man, everything dark is a ghost”: the vāḷai fish, seeing a fallen palmyra fruit roll in the water, mistakes even that for a predatory water-crow and darts away.

Pāsuram 63

Tamil Mūlam

நன்றிருந்து யோகநீதி நண்ணுவார்கள் சிந்தையுள்
சென்றிருந்து தீவினைகள் தீர்த்த தேவதேவனே!
குன்றிருந்த மாடநீடு பாடகத்து மூரகத்தும்
நின்றிருந்து வெஃகணைக் கிடந்ததென்ன நீர்மையே.

IAST Transliteration

naṉṟiruntu yōkanīti naṇṇuvārkaḷ cintaiyuḷ
ceṉṟiruntu tīviṉaikaḷ tīrtta tēvatēvaṉē!
kuṉṟirunta māṭanīṭu pāṭakattu mūrakattum
niṉṟiruntu veḵkaṇaik kiṭantateṉṉa nīrmaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O God of gods who enters and abides firmly in the minds of those who sit well established, approach the righteous discipline of yoga, and thereby destroys their evil karmas! What wondrous nature is Yours: in Kāñcī, among lofty mansions rising mountain upon mountain, You sit at Tiruppāḍagam, stand at Tiruvūrakam, and lie at Tiruveḵkā as the dam that checked the flood!

English Translation of the Commentary

Having contemplated the southern divine places through Tirukkuṟuṅkuṭi, the Āḻvār turns to several sacred sites in Kāñcī.

Word-by-word gloss. “Sitting well,” being properly established in excellent yogic posture and disciplines such as yama and niyama; “those who approach the righteous way of yoga.” “Entering their thought” as the object of contemplation and “abiding” steadily there; “destroying their evil deeds,” uprooting their harmful karmas. “O God of gods!” Then, in the city whose “lofty mansions stand like mountains,” “at Pāḍagam” and “at Ūragam,” “sitting and standing”; “at Veḵkā, lying as the dam.” “What a nature!” What a great gracious quality!

The Āḻvār had separately enjoyed the Lord’s different postures in different holy places. Now he delights in seeing that within the one city of Kāñcī the Lord has taken several postures in several shrines, and gathers them into one pāsuram.

You eternally enjoy Yourself with the Nityasūris in the heavenly world where there is no place at all for karma. Yet You show affection even toward those who still possess karmas, provided they have labored through many births, passed successively through karma-yoga and jñāna-yoga, and entered parabhakti. You enter their hearts, remain there, and, rather than withdrawing in disgust on seeing the defects that still cling to them, bless them and eradicate those evils. This graciousness is magnificent.

But what can I say of Your further nature? To bless even foolish human beings who build mountain upon mountain of mansions and plunge into worldly prosperity, You have taken many arcā postures in places such as Pāḍagam and made Yourself an object of sight for everyone, cutting away both kinds of karma merely by being seen.

“Pāḍagam” is Tiruppāḍagam, the shrine of Pāṇḍava-dūta Kṛṣṇa. A traditional story relates that when Kṛṣṇa came as the envoy of the Pāṇḍavas, Duryodhana prepared a subterranean chamber filled with killers and troops, covered it, placed a jeweled throne above it, and intended Kṛṣṇa to fall into the trap when He sat. Bhagavān assumed the cosmic form there and frustrated the scheme. When He stood as the universal form, the underground area around His feet looked merely like a pāḍagam, an anklet ornament, and the shrine acquired that name. Alternatively, pāḍu + agam yields “the place where greatness becomes manifest.”

“Ūragam” is the shrine of Ulagāḷanda Perumāḷ, the Lord who measured the world. Ūr can mean world, which He made His agam, dwelling or interior domain, by measuring it in the Trivikrama incarnation. Since Ādiśeṣa is also famous there as a granter of boons, some derive the name from Sanskrit uraga, serpent, with phonetic development to Ūragam.

“Veḵkā-dam.” This is the Yathoktakārī shrine at Tiruveḵkā. When Sarasvatī came rushing as the river Vegavatī to obstruct Brahmā’s sacrifice, the Lord reclined across her like a dam; hence in Sanskrit He is called Vegā-setu. Śrī Deśika praises Him: “Victorious is the vast Vegā-setu, the unique cause of the protection of the universe.” On this meaning, “lying as the Veḵkā dam” is literal. Others simply take Veḵkā as the name of the place where He reclines.

The sequence in the text says “standing and sitting” for metrical alliteration, but in geographic/shrine order it is to be understood “sitting [at Pāḍagam], standing [at Ūragam].” The next pāsuram makes the order explicit.

Pāsuram 64

Tamil Mūlam

நின்றதெந்தை யூரகத்திருந்ததெந்தைபாடகத்து
அன்று வெஃகணைக்கிடந்த தென்னிலாதமுன்னெலாம்
அன்று நான்பிறந்திலேன் பிறந்தபின்மறந்திலேன்
நின்றதுமிருந்ததும் கிடந்ததுமென்நெஞ்சுளே.

IAST Transliteration

niṉṟatentai yūrakattiruntatentaipāṭakattu
aṉṟu veḵkaṇaikkiṭanta teṉṉilātamuṉṉelām
aṉṟu nāṉpiṟantilēṉ piṟantapiṉmaṟantilēṉ
niṉṟatumiruntatum kiṭantatumeṉneñcuḷē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

My Father stood at Ūragam; my Father sat at Pāḍagam; and long ago He lay at Veḵkā as the dam. In all the time before I existed in this true birth, I was not born. But after I was born, I never forgot. Now His standing, His sitting, and His lying are all within my own heart.

English Translation of the Commentary

Having marveled that the Lord made Himself suitable even for those who are not yogins by taking these various local arcā forms, the Āḻvār shows their fruit through his own experience.

Word-by-word gloss. “Before me,” throughout the former time when “my [true] being was not”; “my Father stood” in Tiruvūrakam; “my Father sat” in Tiruppāḍagam; “He lay” long ago “at Veḵkā as the dam,” at the time of Brahmā’s sacrifice. “At that time I had not been born” — I had not yet obtained the true birth consisting in knowledge that I am the Lord’s dependent. “After being born,” after that knowledge arose, “I have not forgotten” even for a single day or moment. Therefore now my Father’s “standing, sitting, and lying” are “within my heart alone.”

“Veḵkā-dam” may also simply mean “at Tiruveḵkā.”

The Lord’s intention from the beginning was that I should place Him in my heart. Because He is my Father, my Master, He considered it His duty to do what is beneficial for me and to protect His own property; therefore long ago He came and stood, sat, and lay in shrines such as Ūragam. When I did not know Him as He truly is, He could not yet stand permanently in my heart.

Even if I had approached Him merely like some other deity, bowing only when a temporary fruit was wanted, He would still not remain in my heart; He would have to continue waiting in Ūragam and the other places. But now I have understood Him. Moreover I have not been “born” like one who resorts to Him for some other benefit: I know Him to be both the goal to be attained and the means of attainment, prāpya and prāpaka, and I do not forget this. Why then should He remain merely in those external shrines? He has become firmly established within my heart itself.

“Before en was.” In context the elders take en to mean “I,” though the surface form ordinarily means “my” or “why.” One could attempt a different interpretation: “Why were those acts of standing, sitting, and lying, which otherwise would not have existed? For what purpose did they occur?” Or “Throughout the time when I did not exist [in this realized condition], why did He stand, sit, and lie in those places?” Answer: solely to bless me in advance, though unfortunate I was born into realization only later.

The next pāsuram explicitly says “before I was,” supporting the interpretation “I” here. Because the jīva as a substance is eternal and never literally comes into existence, we preserve the word en and supply “my true nature”: what was absent was not the self but the manifested awareness of its essential servitude (śeṣatva-jñāna).

Pāsuram 65

Tamil Mūlam

நிற்பதுமோர் வெற்பகத் திருப்பும்விண் கிடப்பதும்
நற்பெருந் திரைக்கடலுள் நானிலாத முன்னெலாம்
அற்புத னந்தசயநன் ஆதிபூதன் மாதவன்
நிற்பதுமிருப்பதும் கிடப்பதும் மென்நெஞ்சுளே.

IAST Transliteration

niṟpatumōr veṟpakat tiruppumviṇ kiṭappatum
naṟperun tiraikkaṭaluḷ nāṉilāta muṉṉelām
aṟputa ṉantacayanaṉ ātipūtaṉ mātavaṉ
niṟpatumiruppatum kiṭappatum meṉneñcuḷē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Before the time when I, in this realized sense, existed, He stood within the incomparable sacred hill, sat in the supreme heaven, and lay in the good, great, wave-filled Milk Ocean. But now that wondrous Mādhava—Ananta-śayana in the ocean and the Primal Being in heaven—His standing, sitting, and lying are all within my heart.

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār now says that it is not merely that the Lord presently manifests within his heart in the same three postures found in the shrines just mentioned. Long before the Āḻvār acquired the “birth” of knowledge of servitude, the Lord had already established Himself there as the inner goal of that future realization. By giving opportunities to see Him externally, He stabilized the Āḻvār’s awareness so that it would never be forgotten, and thereby made His ancient standing, sitting, and lying forms manifest within the heart.

Word-by-word gloss. “Within one incomparable hill,” Tirumalai, “standing.” “In heaven,” Paramapada, “sitting.” “Within the good and great wave-filled ocean,” the Milk Ocean, “lying.” All this belonged to “the whole former time when I was not” — before my realized birth. “The wondrous One,” at Tirumalai; “Ananta-śayana,” lying upon Ādiśeṣa in the Milk Ocean; “the Primal Being,” the original Vāsudeva in heaven; “Mādhava,” the Lord on whose chest the Lotus-Lady dwells. Now His “standing, sitting, and lying” are “within my heart alone.”

A reading “the sitting in heaven” is also transmitted, explicitly preserving the conjunctive particle. When the Āḻvār first began turning toward arcā he said, “standing on the hill, abiding in heaven, lying in the long ocean.” After contemplating many divine places in standing, sitting, and reclining postures, he closes the movement by again recalling Tirumalai together with the heavenly and oceanic abodes. This deliberate framing is noteworthy.

Tradition sees a special greatness in Tirumalai comparable in one respect even with Paramapada and the Milk Ocean. The Upaniṣadic expression “beyond the waters, in the midst of the world, on the back of heaven” is traditionally connected with these three loci. Nammāḻvār too joins them: “the ocean, the mountain, and the sky seek [Him] as we do” (Tiruvāymoḻi 2.3.4).

The three titles in the third line correspond respectively to the three locations: “the wondrous One” to Tirumalai, “Ananta-śayana” to the Milk Ocean, and “Primal Being” to the heavenly Vāsudeva. The word Ananta especially conveys the great spread of Ādiśeṣa’s form, with many hoods and coils, on which the Lord reclines at ease. In “Primal Being,” bhūta is used in the Upaniṣadic sense seen in “of this great Being,” indicating the Supreme who possesses an extraordinary form. “Mādhava,” Lord of Śrī, is common to all three manifestations.

Pāsuram 66

Tamil Mūlam

இன்றுசாத னின்றுசாத லன்றியாரும் வையகத்து
ஒன்றிநின்று வாழ்தலின்மை கண்டும் நீச ரென்கொலோ
அன்றுபா ரளந்தபாத போதையுன்னி வானின்மேல்
சென்று சென்று தேவரா யிருக்கிலாத வண்ணமே.

IAST Transliteration

iṉṟucāta ṉiṉṟucāta laṉṟiyārum vaiyakattu
oṉṟiniṉṟu vāḻtaliṉmai kaṇṭum nīca reṉkolō
aṉṟupā raḷantapāta pōtaiyuṉṉi vāṉiṉmēl
ceṉṟu ceṉṟu tēvarā yirukkilāta vaṇṇamē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

In this world, everyone—however great—must die either today or after remaining for some time; none can remain joined to this body and live here forever. Though they see this directly, why do these ignorant ones not contemplate the lotus-feet that measured the earth on that day, set their aim upon the heaven beyond, go there in due time, and remain like the eternal gods?

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār had marveled that the Supreme Person descends into so many sacred places in this kṣetra-like world and assumes many postures, and he rejoiced at the immense benefit he himself received. He now marvels at the opposite fact: even after the Lord has made Himself so available, the world still wanders without taking the way to salvation.
