Word-by-word gloss. “In this world,” the realm of Prakṛti; “anyone,” however exalted; “dying today,” immediately, or “standing and dying,” dying after some delay—apart from these, “remaining joined and living” permanently “does not exist.” Though “seeing” this directly, why do “the low/ignorant” not “think upon the lotus-foot” that “measured the earth on that day,” in the Trivikrama incarnation; “set the upper heaven” as the goal; “go,” and in due course “go there,” “becoming gods,” remaining equal in status to the Nityasūris? “Why is it that they cannot be so?”

This pāsuram arises from the Āḻvār’s anguish when, after enjoying the Lord’s accessibility within his own heart, he looks at the suffering of other beings. “Why do my people not simply think of this Lord and attain the supreme human end? I cannot understand it.” Whatever one’s birth or status, death is certain. It may come today or after some span of time, but it comes. No one is ignorant of this.

Even the smallest creature trembles when the slightest danger occurs, fearing that life may be lost. The Mahābhārata’s famous reflection says: “Day after day beings enter the abode of Yama; those who remain nevertheless desire permanence. What could be more astonishing than this?”

Could people have abandoned the Lord because they suppose He lacks tenderness? That cannot be. Without anyone asking, He became Trivikrama, measured all the earth, touched everyone with His lotus-feet, and purified them—a Lord of boundless generosity. Nor can they say that the means is too difficult. Merely thinking upon that foot is enough. Nor can they say there is no stable goal: the supreme “heaven” exists, as scripture says, “in the supreme imperishable space.” Nor can they say that suffering continues even after reaching it: they become godlike, attaining full manifestation of their essential nature and supreme similarity to the Lord. Why then do they not proceed?

“Low ones” can simply mean the utterly ignorant or greatly sinful. Another nuance is: those who recognize themselves as nīca, lowly, have all the more reason to bow; for, as the saying goes, “It is the nature of both the low and the high that the former should bow and the latter be bowed to.” If they only understand this relation and bow, that itself suffices.

“Thinking of the foot and going to the heaven above.” Those who meditate on the divine feet should recognize the superiority of that heaven over every other fruit and take it as their sole goal rather than seeking lesser rewards. Kilāta is from a verbal form meaning ability or capability.

Pāsuram 67

Tamil Mūlam

சண்டமண்ட லத்தினூடு சென்றுவீடு பெற்றுமேல்
கண்டுவீ டிலாதகாத லின்பநாளு மெய்துவீர்
புண்டரீக பாதபுண்ய கீர்த்திநுஞ்செவிமடுத்து
உண்டு நும் உறுவினைத் துயருள்நீங்கி யுய்மினோ.

IAST Transliteration

caṇṭamaṇṭa lattiṉūṭu ceṉṟuvīṭu peṟṟumēl
kaṇṭuvī ṭilātakāta liṉpanāḷu meytuvīr
puṇṭarīka pātapuṇya kīrttinuñcevimaṭuttu
uṇṭu num uṟuviṉait tuyaruḷnīṅki yuymiṉō.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

You who are destined to pass through the fierce solar orb, traverse the entire Arcirādi path, attain liberation, behold Him there, and forever obtain the unceasing bliss of love—place in your ears and consume as food the holy fame of the Lord whose feet are red lotuses. Escape the sorrow produced by the karmas that cling to you, and be saved!

English Translation of the Commentary

Up to this point the Āḻvār has chiefly spoken of his own experience. Seeing that others fail to obtain the same gain, he cannot remain indifferent. When correction is necessary, one who knows the truth has the duty to teach; genuine knowledge cannot simply neglect others. Desiring that they too obtain what he has obtained, the Āḻvār begins a direct instruction that continues through pāsuram 81.

Word-by-word gloss. “Through the fierce orb” — through the blazing solar sphere; “going through [its middle],” then traversing the full Arcirādi path; “obtaining the Home,” Paramapada. “Seeing above,” seeing the Lord there. “Love-bliss that has no departure,” supreme bliss joined with parabhakti, never to be lost; “every day/for all time,” “you shall attain.” O people born for this destiny! “The holy, meritorious fame of the Lord whose feet are lotuses”; “place [it] in your ears,” “eat it,” experience it as nourishment. “Your clinging karma,” the deeds you have done that do not let you go; escaping “the sorrow” produced by them, “be saved.”

“Fierce orb” is the blazing solar disc, caṇḍāṃśu-maṇḍala, not the lunar sphere. Caṇḍa itself can denote the sun. Some understand “you shall attain” as “you who desire to attain,” but since the Āḻvār is now actively trying to make ordinary people into seekers of liberation, it is especially fitting to address them as beings born for this great destiny.

Some connect “seeing” with the “bliss”: “seeing and experiencing that bliss.” In any case, the point is that the supreme result is already available to you; do not throw it away. You keep going to the lunar sphere and returning. If instead you enter the solar path of arcirādi, you need not return. Its bliss has no end and continually intensifies through devotion.

What must you do for it? Hear the teaching. Hear with devotion about Him, the beauty of His divine body, and the majesty of His deeds. That very hearing and experience is true food; other food is ultimately futile. Without it the karma you have accumulated will not leave you and sorrow will not cease. Therefore listen to the instruction and live.

This is an especially remarkable pāsuram, compressing in a wondrous way the whole range of essential meanings.

Pāsuram 68

Tamil Mūlam

முத்திறத்து வாணியத் திரண்டிலொன்றும் நீசர்கள்
மத்தராய் மயங்குகின்ற திட்டதிலிறந்துபோந்து
எத்திறத்து முய்வதோ ருபாயமில்லை யுங்குறில்
தொத்திறுத்த தண்டுழாய்நன் மாலைவாழ்த்தி வாழ்மினோ.

IAST Transliteration

muttiṟattu vāṇiyat tiraṇṭiloṉṟum nīcarkaḷ
mattarāy mayaṅkukiṉṟa tiṭṭatiliṟantupōntu
ettiṟattu muyvatō rupāyamillai yuṅkuṟil
tottiṟutta taṇṭuḻāynaṉ mālaivāḻtti vāḻmiṉō.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Among the three kinds of gains, the lowly who attach themselves to one of the other two, maddened and deluded, cast themselves into the means for that fruit, die and go forth, only to remain confused. In any such result there is no true means of salvation. If you would be saved, praise the excellent Māl adorned with cool tulasī broken in clusters, and live!

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār teaches that among the three broad classes of results, mokṣa—the attainment of Bhagavān—is the truly great fruit.

Word-by-word gloss. “Of three kinds,” “among the gains/trades.” “In the two” other than the principal one, “lowly people who join themselves to one [of them].” “For that [fruit],” “casting themselves” into the required means; “dying and going forth.” Remaining “intoxicated,” with identifications such as body-as-self and self-as-independent, they “remain deluded.” “In any other kind [of result],” “there is no one means of being truly saved.” “If you wish to be saved,” “the cool tulasī broken in clusters,” “the excellent Māl” who wears it; “praising” Him, “live.”

Vāṇiyam is related to Sanskrit vāṇijya, trade or commerce, and in Tamil can mean business or gain. Here it stands generally for the fruit sought by living beings.

What are the three kinds of fruit? One proposal is sāttvika, rājasa, and tāmasa results. If these mean respectively fruits enjoyed under the dominance of sattva, rajas, and tamas, all three remain material. Or, in accordance with the Gītā—“sāttvika persons worship the gods, rājasa persons Yakṣas and Rākṣasas, and tāmasa persons ghosts and hosts of spirits”—the three can be fruits obtained through three corresponding classes of worshiped beings; again all would be material.

If instead one divides results according to the kind of renunciatory disposition accompanying action—sāttvika renunciation versus rājasa and tāmasa—mokṣa can enter the first class. Another possible threefold division is: fruits attained here by one’s own capacity; fruits obtained through other jīvas such as gods; and the fruit that must be obtained through the Supreme Lord Himself. Most naturally, however, the familiar three are aiśvarya (worldly/celestial sovereignty), kaivalya (isolated self-experience), and mokṣa (Bhagavad-attainment). Then “the other two” includes everything except mokṣa.

“Maddened and deluded.” The grammatical construction “the lowly ones’ being deluded” can be understood like an impersonal or abstract bhāva usage. They obtain a small and impermanent result and form a completely inverted estimate of it. Even in kaivalya one can become so absorbed in the gain that one thinks, in the spirit of the Gītā’s phrase, “having attained which, one considers no other gain superior to it,” though the highest Bhagavad-experience is in fact still beyond it.

“Going forth.” The Tamil form pōntu is variously explained. In some usages it appears to mean “coming,” perhaps through phonetic development from a compound meaning “enter and come”; elsewhere it retains a sense from “going.” Here the latter is natural: carrying with them the learning and karmic merit they have accumulated, they go to the region where the chosen fruit is experienced.

“There is no means of true salvation.” Merely attaining one of those fruits must not be mistaken for being saved. When one later falls from it and returns to saṃsāra, it becomes evident that the means previously performed were not means to genuine salvation.

“If you wish to be saved.” The form is contracted from “if you desire to be saved.” “The excellent Māl”: here Māl directly denotes the Lord and is the object of the verb “praise.” Some explain “praising” as the means and “living” as the result. More contextually, praising the Lord alone—abandoning other deities—is itself the true life; no lesser fruit deserves to be called life.

Pāsuram 69

Tamil Mūlam

காணிலு முருப்பொலார் செவிக்கினாத கீர்த்தியார்
பேணிலும் வரந்தர மிடுக்கிலாத தேவரை
ஆணமென் றடைந்துவாழும் ஆதர்காள் எம்ஆதிபால்
பேணிநும் பிறப்பெனும் பிணக்கறுக்க கிற்றிரே.

IAST Transliteration

kāṇilu muruppolār cevikkiṉāta kīrttiyār
pēṇilum varantara miṭukkilāta tēvarai
āṇameṉ ṟaṭaintuvāḻum ātarkāḷ emātipāl
pēṇinum piṟappeṉum piṇakkaṟukka kiṟṟirē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Blind ones! You take as your refuge and live by gods whose forms are dreadful to see, whose fame is unpleasant to hear, and who, even when lovingly worshiped, lack the power to grant the highest boon. Instead, cherish our Primal Lord, the Cause sought by us; then you will be able to cut off the affliction called repeated birth.

English Translation of the Commentary

One may accept that mokṣa is the supreme fruit yet reason: “Scripture itself praises other deities. Let us investigate their nature, forms, qualities, and powers, take them as refuge, and obtain mokṣa through them.” The Āḻvār teaches that neither hearing, reflection, meditation, nor vision of their forms and activities is inherently delightful in the way Bhagavān is, and that even if one nevertheless worships them, mokṣa cannot be obtained from them.

Word-by-word gloss. “Even if seen,” their “forms are unpleasant/dreadful.” “Their fame is not pleasing to the ear.” “Even if cherished,” worshiped with affection, “they lack prowess to grant the boon,” the desired supreme result. Such “gods,” Brahmā, Rudra, and others, you “approach as the authority/refuge,” saying “You alone are my protection,” and suppose yourselves to “live.” “O blind ones!” Instead “cherish our Primal One,” the cause of the universe whom we have taken as refuge, and you “will be capable of cutting” “the knot/affliction called your birth,” the chain of repeated rebirth.

What are their visible forms? Five heads, or three fierce eyes, matted hair, ash, elephant-skin, and at times nakedness. What is the fame one hears? Cutting off the father’s head, slaying the father-in-law, favoring asuras and rākṣasas, and making destruction itself one’s cosmic occupation. Who will eagerly plunge into hearing such exploits? Having contemplated them, who will long to see those fearsome forms directly?

Even satisfying such deities is not easy: narratives portray them as gratified by terrible offerings such as giving one’s own head. Suppose one were willing to endure all that because the fruit is great. Are they capable of bestowing mokṣa? Leave mokṣa aside—can they even grant every lesser major boon exactly as requested? They cannot.

What is the reason? None of those deities is the primal source. They themselves stand subordinate to the Primal Lord and receive their forms, fame, powers, and offices according to their karma and His arrangement. The true Primal One is our Master Kṛṣṇa. Every one of His forms is supremely beautiful to the eye; He is adorned with lovely garments and ornaments. His histories are nectar to the ear. He never abandons one who seeks Him and bears all their offenses. As the Gītā says, “a leaf, a flower, a fruit, or water” offered with devotion suffices: He is easily worshiped.

Some explain “our Primal One” as “the One who is the cause of us.” Why speak of the cause of the entire universe as though He were especially our cause? The relish is: “His creating us has borne fruit, for we have become seekers of liberation and turned to Him; the creation of those who do not become mumukṣus seems, from this loving perspective, to have gone fruitless.”

Pāsuram 70

Tamil Mūlam

குந்தமோடு சூலம் வேல்கள் தோமரங்கள் தண்டுவாள்
பந்தமான தேவர்கள் பரந்துவா னகமுற
வந்தவாண னீரைஞ்ஞறு தோள்களைத் துணித்தநாள்
அந்தவந்த வாகுல மமரரே யறிவரே.

IAST Transliteration

kuntamōṭu cūlam vēlkaḷ tōmaraṅkaḷ taṇṭuvāḷ
pantamāṉa tēvarkaḷ parantuvā ṉakamuṟa
vantavāṇa ṉīraiññaṟu tōḷkaḷait tuṇittanāḷ
antavanta vākula mamararē yaṟivarē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

The very gods whom you seek know well the turmoil of that day when, though Rudra and the assembled gods were armed with spears, tridents, lances, javelins, clubs, and swords, they scattered through the sky and entered their homes, while Bāṇa nevertheless came on; and the Lord cut away his thousand—two times five hundred—arms.

English Translation of the Commentary

Those deities themselves know very well that they lack the strength even to grant many small boons independently. The Āḻvār therefore says: if you insist on cherishing them, learn the truth directly from their own experience, with an unmistakable example.

Word-by-word gloss. “Together with spears,” “tridents,” “lances,” “javelins,” “clubs,” and “swords”; gods “equipped/arrayed” with these weapons. “Scattering across the sky,” defeated by Kṛṣṇa; “entering the interior,” their homes. Even after that, “Bāṇa came” into battle. “His two times five hundred shoulders,” all one thousand arms; “on the day [the Lord] cut them off,” mostly destroying them. “That turmoil that came then”—“the immortals themselves know it,” or certainly have not forgotten it.

Rudra, together with his son Subrahmaṇya, commander of the divine armies, and with Agni and the entire retinue headed by the elder brother Gaṇapati, worked desperately to protect his devotee Bāṇāsura. They suffered a crushing defeat and scattered in flight. Yet Bāṇa, intoxicated with pride, still thought he could win by himself. Kṛṣṇa then cut away the very arms that had produced that arrogance—arms obtained through Rudra’s boon. Look at this and decide who is truly supreme.

The full extent of the distress suffered by those gods may not even be completely known from Purāṇic accounts. Ask them themselves and learn. We, having received knowledge free of delusion through the Lord’s grace, could state all the details; but if we tell you aspects not explicitly found in the Purāṇas, you may dismiss them as invented stories. If the gods themselves tell you, you will believe. This is the force of the verse.

“Equipped/arrayed” can qualify both the gods and Bāṇa’s arms: Bāṇa came against the Lord of the five weapons with a thousand weapon-bearing hands, leaving not one hand idle. “Scattering through the sky and entering home” may be divided as “spreading through the sky, entering the interior,” or taken together as “reaching their heavenly homes.”

If one reads “he came after they had fled and hidden,” that itself shows that even Bāṇa had learned their weakness. In “that turmoil that came,” the second element is the verb “came” and “day” goes with it. If the phrase is divided instead as “that, that turmoil,” one must mentally supply “which occurred on that day.” The turmoil includes all stages: Rudra and the others being humiliated and fleeing, then returning in fear after offending the Lord, taking Kṛṣṇa Himself as refuge, and thereby escaping danger.

Pāsuram 71

Before the verse, the commentator explains: Rudra and the other subordinate deities show partiality toward particular persons, whereas for the Supreme Lord all beings, if they stand in the proper relation to Him, are fit recipients of grace. Were not all the later blessings that came even to the hostile Bāṇa due to Kṛṣṇa Himself? Therefore, seeing this clearly, one should trust and take refuge in Him alone as one’s deity - the One described as “the common supreme deity of gods and Dānavas alike” (devānāṃ dānavānāṃ ca sāmānyam adhidaivatam).

Tamil Mūlam

வண்டுலாவு கோதைமாதர் காரணத்தினால்வெகுண்டு
இண்டவாண னீரைஞ்ஞூறு தோள்களைத் துணித்தநாள்
முண்டனீறன் மக்கள்வெப்பு மோடியங்கி யோடிடக்
கண்டுநாணி வாணனுக்கிரங்கினானேம்மாயனே.

IAST Transliteration

vaṇṭulāvu kōtaimātar kāraṇattiṉāl vekuṇṭu
iṇṭavāṇa ṉīraiññūṟu tōḷkaḷait tuṇittanāḷ
muṇṭaṉīṟaṉ makkaḷ veppu mōṭiyaṅki yōṭiṭak
kaṇṭunāṇi vāṇaṉukkiraṅkiṉāṉ emmāyaṉē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Because of the maiden Uṣā, whose garland was roamed by bees, Bāṇa became enraged and came near for battle. On the very day when our wondrous Lord cut away his thousand - two times five hundred - arms, the shaven-headed, ash-smeared one, his sons, the fever-deity, Moḍi, and Agni all fled in rout. Seeing this, and ashamed, our wondrous Lord nevertheless pitied Bāṇa and spared him.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. vaṇṭu ulāvu - where bees move about; kōtai mātar kāraṇattināl - because of the maiden Uṣā, who wore a flower-garland; vekuṇṭu iṇṭa - angrily drawing near for battle; vāṇaṉ - Bāṇāsura; īr aiññūṟu tōḷkaḷai - his one thousand shoulders or arms; tuṇitta nāḷ - on the very day when they were severed. Muṇṭaṉ - the shaven-headed one; nīṟaṉ - the ash-smeared one: these designate Śiva. Makkaḷ - his sons, such as Vināyaka; veppu - the fever-deity; mōṭi - Durgā; aṅki - Agni, one of the attendants. Ōṭiṭa - when all these were defeated, turned their backs, and fled. Kaṇṭu nāṇi - seeing this, and also seeing Bāṇa’s deity Koṭarī stand naked before Him, He became ashamed; em māyaṉē - our wondrous Lord whom we take as refuge; vāṇaṉukku iraṅkiṉāṉ - He took pity on Bāṇāsura and protected him.

Uṣā had been secretly united in the women’s apartments with Aniruddha, who had been brought there without anyone knowing. She wore her bee-haunted flower-garland and delighted in that clandestine union. Bāṇāsura saw this, became furious, bound Aniruddha, and came to war against Kṛṣṇa accompanied by a great host headed by Paramaśiva. When it became clear that Bāṇa’s life could no longer be preserved, Koṭarī, the tutelary deity of his clan, stood naked before Kṛṣṇa. Seeing her - and, as it were, thinking, “What sort of warriors are these? We are fighting against such frightened fugitives!” - Kṛṣṇa felt ashamed and did not cut away Bāṇa’s natural arms; He left him alive. Who other than Kṛṣṇa could show compassion in precisely this way?

The expressions “the shaven-headed one” and “the ash-smeared one” refer to Śiva himself; there is no need to imagine some separate attendant of those descriptions. Kṛṣṇa discharged the Jṛmbhaka weapon against Śiva and made him sit there yawning. Subrahmaṇya and the others likewise had their pride destroyed, some through Garuḍa and some through Kṛṣṇa’s mere roar. “The fever” refers to the Māheśvara-jvara, described as having three feet and three heads. Kṛṣṇa produced from His own divine body the Vaiṣṇava-jvara; the Māheśvara fever was tormented by it, lost its strength, fell at Kṛṣṇa’s feet, received a boon, and fled away.

Some explain “seeing and becoming ashamed” simply as: Kṛṣṇa felt ashamed that He had considered such weak opponents worth fighting. The Viṣṇu Purāṇa, however, says in substance: “When Madhudviṣ was about to release His discus for Bāṇa’s destruction, the naked Daitya-vidyā Koṭarī appeared before Hari. Seeing her before Him, Hari closed His eyes and released Sudarśana toward Bāṇa in order to cut down the forest of his arms.” Because this places Koṭarī’s appearance before the cutting of the arms, it may seem difficult to connect “seeing and becoming ashamed” with what happened afterward.

The Bhāgavata too narrates that Koṭarī stood without clothing in order to preserve Bāṇāsura’s life, that she was Bāṇa’s mother, and that when Kṛṣṇa turned His face away upon seeing her, Bāṇa escaped. A tutelary deity can indeed be spoken of as a mother. Elsewhere, in the sixth skandha, Bāṇa’s mother is said to bear the name Aśanā; that tradition too can be considered in interpreting “seeing and becoming ashamed.” Both Purāṇas further relate that, after the arms had been cut, Maheśvara himself came and pleaded, and because of that Kṛṣṇa showed mercy to Bāṇa. The point is that, however hostile a person may have been, our wondrous Lord’s aim is finally to subdue him, remove his hostility, and protect him. Vekuṇṭu means “having become angry.”

Pāsuram 72

The preceding teaching may provoke an objection: “How can one say that by cherishing Rudra and the other deities one cannot cut the knot of rebirth? The Pāśupata Āgama says Rudra is the Supreme Deity, and there are Vedic expressions such as ‘Rudra is one; there is no second beside him’ and ‘Śiva alone is.’ Therefore one cannot settle the issue merely from the sportive episode of Bāṇāsura’s battle.” The Āḻvār clarifies this objection.

Tamil Mūlam

போதின்மங்கை பூதலக்கிழத்தி தேவியன்றியும்
போதுதங்குநான்முகன் மகனவன்மகன்சொலில்
மாதுதங்குகூறன் ஏறதூர்தியென்றுவேதநூல்
ஓதுகின்றதுண்மையல்லதில்லை மற்றுரைக்கிலே.

IAST Transliteration

pōtiṉmaṅkai pūtalakkiḻatti tēviyaṉṟiyum
pōtutaṅkunāṉmukaṉ makaṉ avaṉ makaṉ colil
mātutaṅkukūṟaṉ ēṟatūrti yeṉṟu vētanūl
ōtukiṉṟatuṇmai allatu illai maṟṟuraikkilē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

The maiden dwelling upon the lotus and the Lady of the earth are His divine consorts. And beyond this, the four-faced one who dwells upon the lotus is His son; if one goes on speaking, that son’s son is the one who bears a woman in half his body and has the bull for his vehicle. What the scripture called the Veda proclaims in this way alone is truth; if one interprets otherwise, there is no truth in that contrary account.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. pōtil maṅkai - Periya Pirāṭṭi, the maiden who dwells on the lotus; pūtala-k-kiḻatti - the Lady belonging to the earth, Bhūdevī; tēvi - these are His consorts. Aṉṟiyum - apart from that; pōtu taṅku nāṉmukaṉ - four-faced Brahmā who dwells on the lotus from the Lord’s navel; makaṉ - is His son. Colil - if we speak still further; mātu taṅku kūṟaṉ - the one in whose one half Pārvatī resides; ēṟu atu ūrti - Śiva, whose vehicle is the bull; avaṉ makaṉ - he is the son of that son. Vēta-nūl eṉṟu ōtukiṉṟatu - what the authoritative scripture called the Veda teaches in this manner; uṇmai allatu - that alone is true; uraikkil maṟṟu illai - if an interpretation is given, no contrary doctrine is true.

The Upaniṣads, in teachings such as the Sad-vidyā, declare liberation to come through knowledge of the Supreme Person who is the material cause (upādāna-kāraṇa) of the universe. Even those other Āgamas do not accept Paśupati as the material cause. The Person who is the material cause is also the efficient cause (nimitta-kāraṇa), that is, the one who actually creates all worlds. This is broadly taught both in the causal passages already cited and in the Puruṣa Sūkta.

A scriptural statement says in substance: “Having contemplated, desiring to create manifold creatures, He created them from His own body; He created the waters…” Since it says that everything is created from His own body, the Puruṣa Sūkta too clearly teaches that He is both material and efficient cause. The Skanda Purāṇa further says that the word puruṣa is properly a name of Viṣṇu, just as the word Śaṅkara is a name of Śiva.

Nor is this all. The later section called Uttara-Nārāyaṇa identifies the Puruṣa praised in the Puruṣa Sūkta as Nārāyaṇa through the sentence, “Hrī and Lakṣmī are Your wives” (hrīś ca te lakṣmīś ca patnyau). The first line of the pāsuram says precisely that Periya Pirāṭṭi and Bhū Pirāṭṭi are His consorts.

An objection may then be raised: “But does not the Veda also say that Rudra existed at the beginning of creation?” The following lines answer. The scriptural tradition declares that four-faced Brahmā arose from Nārāyaṇa’s navel-lotus and Rudra arose from Brahmā, while at dissolution Brahmā, Rudra, and the rest do not independently remain. How, then, can Rudra, who stands in the position of the grandson, be made the supreme causal principle? The Āḻvār deliberately says “if one speaks” to indicate: “We have examined this carefully and are stating it with certainty; take it as firmly established.”

Suppose one asks, “Then what is the meaning of passages saying Rudra is the cause?” The fourth line answers: such passages must be interpreted in a manner that does not contradict the meaning already clearly established. That alone is a true interpretation. In various places the Upaniṣads speak of ākāśa, prāṇa, water, and other entities one by one as though each were the first cause. Just as those passages are interpreted in harmony with the final teaching, so too the Rudra passages must be interpreted.

Rudra himself becomes “Rudra” only through the Lord’s grace; therefore the Supreme Lord who indwells and empowers Rudra may Himself be denoted by the word Rudra. Likewise, in accordance with the expression “the auspiciousness of all auspicious things,” the Lord of Śrī, who is supreme auspiciousness itself, is properly called Śiva, for śiva means auspiciousness. Rudra is a derivative or ordinary “śiva”; he became auspicious by bearing in his matted hair the Gaṅgā that flowed from Tirumāl’s foot at the Trivikrama incarnation. Nārāyaṇa is the unfailing, intrinsic Śiva.

In connection with “the four-faced one who dwells on the lotus,” one may remember scriptural passages such as: “Upon the divine lotus, radiant like the sun, having produced even me from His navel…”; “the four-faced one was born”; “From his forehead, from the furrowed brow blazing with anger, Rudra then arose, shining like the midday sun, with a body half woman and half man, fierce and of enormous form”; and “That lotus which existed first - there Brahmā was born; and from Brahmā too Śiva was born: know this decisively.”

The expression “Vedic scripture” is used in order to exclude Śaiva Āgamas that conflict with the Veda. The final line may also be construed: “What the Veda thus teaches is truth. If maṟṟu, something contrary to this, is uraikkil, stated as an alternative meaning, it is illai, not true.”

Pāsuram 73

The Āḻvār has thus extensively taught, through scriptural proofs and ancient history, that the Lord of Śrī is superior to Rudra and the rest, is the Lord of all, and is the cause of all. Beings born according to karma generally do not retain in another birth the powers and excellences they possessed in a former one. The Lord, however, is eternally complete everywhere. He assumes incarnations by His own will without the slightest diminution of His power and therefore remains capable of everything in every incarnation. Not understanding this, Rudra and the other gods once opposed Him, suffered defeat, and only later understood. Because they opposed Him, He had to manifest His power toward them in that fashion. Those who do not possess unfailing affection for Him may distrust His greatness because they do not know it; but when He takes someone as an object of grace, He creates faith in that person and grants what is desired. No jīva, however elevated, can by himself seize the full, endless enjoyment of liberation; much less can such a jīva bestow it on another. The Lord, even when He appears in the humblest condition, never loses such enjoyment and can grant it to others. The Āḻvār establishes this from an episode in the Rāma incarnation.

Tamil Mūlam

மரம்பொதச் சரந்துரந்து வாலிவீழமுன்னோர் நாள்
உரம்பொதச் சரந்துரந்த உம்பராளி யெம்பிரான்
வரம்குறிப்பில் வைத்தவர்க் கலாதுவான மாளிலும்
நிரம்புநீடுபோக மெத்திறத்தும் யார்க்குமில்லையே.

IAST Transliteration

marampotac caranturantu vālivīḻa muṉṉōr nāḷ
urampotac caranturanta umparāḷi yempirāṉ
varamkuṟippil vaittavarkkalātu vāṉam āḷilum
nirampunīṭupōkam ettiṟattum yārkkum illaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

On a former day He shot an arrow that pierced through the trees; then He shot another through Vāli’s chest so that Vāli fell. Our beneficent Lord, who even while incarnate rules the higher worlds - except for those whom He has placed within the choice of His gracious intention, no one, even though he rule heaven itself, can in any way possess the full and everlasting enjoyment.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. muṉ oru nāḷ - once, in the Rāma incarnation, when Sugrīva had become Rāma’s friend with fire as witness; maram pota - so that the seven śāla trees were bored through; caram turantu - He discharged a single arrow, thereby producing great confidence in His friend; vāli vīḻa - so that Vāli fell and died; uram pota - in order that his chest be pierced; caram turanta - He discharged one arrow. Umpar āḷi - He who, even in an incarnate condition, rules the upper world; em pirāṉ - our great benefactor. Varam kuṟippil vaittavarkku alātu - except those whom He has placed in His excellent intention, His disposition of grace; vāṉam āḷilum - even if they rule Satyaloka and the other heavens; nirampu nīṭu pōkam - enjoyment supreme in fullness and lasting for all time; ettiṟattum illai - it cannot be obtained by any means whatsoever.

Sugrīva lacked confidence. Rāma first produced confidence in him and then sent him out to fight Vāli and fulfilled his desire. In the same way, we must place affection in Him so that He may create in us faith and the other dispositions required for attainment. Therefore abandon attitudes such as attachment to other deities and hostility toward this Lord. The thought that one can obtain the supreme goal without becoming the object of His gracious intention will never succeed.

Even those who possess immeasurable sovereignty and yogic power cannot obtain that supreme result without His grace. Conversely, those who possess nothing at all can obtain it through even the slightest pretext that He, in His grace, accepts from them. Therefore affection for Him must be firmly established. The sign of such affection is living under His command. The Vedic scripture that proclaims Him has fully set forth that command.

“Ruler of the upper world.” Even while Rāma’s complete human self-conception was outwardly operative, He forgot it, as it were, under the force of limitless affection and said to the bird Jaṭāyu, “With My permission, go to the unsurpassed worlds” (mayā tvaṃ samanujñāto gaccha lokān anuttamān), thus granting him the supreme abode. Consider what sovereignty belongs to the One who could do this.

“Even if he rules heaven.” Do not look at the greatness of worldly or celestial sovereignty and imagine that the supreme abode too must lie in the hands of those who possess it. “By any means.” Nor, seeing someone capable of hearing, reflection, and meditation, should one imagine that mokṣa can be attained independently of Bhagavān’s favor. “To anyone.” Nor should one adopt the mistaken idea of equality among the Trimūrti and suppose that Brahmā and Rudra do not require Viṣṇu’s grace. All these possibilities are excluded.

Pāsuram 74

We have accepted that the Lord of Śrī alone is the Supreme Reality and that, unless He places someone within His gracious intention, the endless enjoyment called mokṣa cannot be attained. Yet one might say: it does not follow that one should never seek Śiva merely because he fled in fear together with Agni and the other attendants. A traditional maxim says: “One should seek health from the Sun, prosperity from Fire, knowledge from Śaṅkara, and liberation from Janārdana.” When such a statement is considered, may one not worship certain other deities for prosperity, knowledge, and similar benefits? The Āḻvār answers.

Tamil Mūlam

அறிந்தறிந்துவாமன னடியிணை வணங்கினால்
செறிந்தெழுந்த ஞானமோடு செல்வமுஞ் சிறந்திடும்
மறிந்தெழுந்ததெண்டிரையுள் மன்னுமாலை வாழ்த்தினால்
பறிந்தெழுந்து தீவினைகள் பற்றறுதல் பான்மையே.

IAST Transliteration

aṟintaṟintu vāmaṉaṉ aṭiyiṇai vaṇaṅkiṉāl
ceṟinteḻunta ñāṉamōṭu celvamuñ ciṟantiṭum
maṟinteḻunta teṇṭiraiyuḷ maṉṉumālai vāḻttiṉāl
paṟinteḻuntu tīviṉaikaḷ paṟṟaṟutal pāṉmaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Knowing again and again the truth, if one worships the twin feet of Vāmana, profound knowledge and prosperity will both excel. If one praises Māl who abides in the clear waves that rise and turn upon themselves, evil karmas are uprooted and their grasp falls away: this is their very nature.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. aṟintu aṟintu - knowing each desired result and the way in which the Lord Himself is the means to it; or knowing that, both directly and as the indwelling ruler of other deities, the Lord Himself is the actual giver of the fruits attributed to them. Vāmaṉaṉ aṭi-iṇai - the two feet of the Lord who helps even by taking the Vāmana incarnation; vaṇaṅkiṉāl - if one takes refuge in them seeking what is desired. Ceṟintu eḻunta ñāṉam - knowledge that has risen to fullness, containing the meanings of all śāstras and directed toward the supreme Reality; celvamum - prosperity as well; ciṟantiṭum - will become excellent. Maṟintu eḻunta - rising in overturning waves; teṇ tirai uḷ - within the milk-ocean of clear waves; maṉṉu Māl - Tirumāl who permanently abides there; vāḻttiṉāl - if one sings auspicious praise to Him. Paṟintu eḻuntu - being torn up from the root and expelled; tī viṉaikaḷ - our evil karmas; paṟṟu aṟutal - abandoning the grasp by which they cling to us; pāṉmaiyē - this is their natural course.

Statements that one obtains wealth or knowledge by resorting to Agni, Śaṅkara, and others must be understood as referring to the fact that these deities give results only after receiving the power to do so from the Lord. Once you understand this, you may seek the corresponding result directly from the Lord Himself and receive it from Him. Moreover, the result so obtained will not merely be equal to what is obtained through another deity; it will be superior.

Its superiority consists in being greater in its own nature, growing for a long period, producing a special kind of happiness, gradually generating detachment, and finally establishing the recipient in the highest human goal. This is the distinction between a result gained through some other deity and the same class of result gained from Him directly.

“Vāmana” is itself the proof. He measured the whole universe with His foot, took possession of it, and then gave it to Indra. What greater wealth could there be than that? Again, the brahmacarya āśrama is entered precisely for the acquisition of knowledge. Vāmana appeared as a solitary brahmacārin; therefore it is certain that knowledge too arises through His grace. By touching everyone with His lotus foot He removed their sins; when the sins that obstruct knowledge depart, knowledge naturally arises. And one who contemplates the strategy by which He brought the entire universe under His control can, if such a lesser result is sought, acquire even the worldly intelligence by which affairs in the world are mastered.

The traditional reading is ciṟantiṭum, “will excel,” not ceṟintiṭum, “will become dense/full”; earlier teachers therefore explain ciṟappu as increase or superiority.

The latter two lines direct one away from lesser results toward mokṣa. Why does the Āḻvār first say “Vāmana” and then speak of the Lord reclining in the milk-ocean? The idea is this. He reclines upon the fitting serpent-couch in the milk-ocean, whose clarity is not diminished however much its waves rise, with a divine body soft as a blossoming flower. We ought simply to experience Him in that form and drown joyfully in that flood of bliss. Is it fitting instead to develop great greed for tiny semblances of pleasure mixed with many sorrows, and thereby make Him rise from His couch and wander about in many ways to satisfy our petitions?

If we pray, He will indeed comply. He will put aside all His majesty, come forth more destitute than the destitute devotee, as it were, and bring our request to completion. Yet such behavior on our part is not beautiful or appropriate to the true nature of the self. Therefore the verse does not merely say “worship Him” in this second half but “praise Him.”

“Being uprooted.” The root of sin is its residual impression (vāsanā). All sins depart together with their vāsanās. Formerly they clung to us as though determined never to let go; now, even if we wanted them to remain, they cannot. See the greatness of service performed without seeking a separate reward. “It is their nature.” If acts such as worship of Bhagavān naturally remove our sins even before we ask, then for one who embraces service itself as the goal, without desiring any other result, it is only natural that the service remove the sins obstructing that service. It is likewise natural for the sins themselves to leave. The cessation of the Lord’s punitive intention and the arising of His gracious intention are not two independent things; the presence of the latter itself entails the former.

Another explanation takes “knowledge and wealth” here not as worldly goods but as knowledge of Bhagavān and devotion to Bhagavān. For one who has made bhara-nyāsa at the Lord’s feet, knowledge and devotion arise; and by merely uttering His sacred name, all sins obstructing attainment of Him disappear.

Pāsuram 75

It has now been taught that mokṣa, the full and endless enjoyment, must be obtained through Nārāyaṇa’s grace; that He likewise grants all other fruits; and that one should nevertheless turn away from those fruits and establish oneself in the path of renunciation, praising Him alone. That path is of two forms, bhakti and prapatti. An objection may arise with regard to the former: “One who has entered bhakti-yoga and desires quickly to behold and experience Bhagavān here might take refuge in other deities in order to obtain that result sooner. Brahmā, Rudra, and the rest are surely accomplished in Bhagavad-experience, and they are famous for granting requested boons quickly. Nor is the fruit being requested inconsistent with the path of renunciation.” The Āḻvār replies.

Tamil Mūlam

ஒன்றிநின்று நற்றவஞ்செய் தூழியூழி தோறெலாம்
நின்றுநின்றவன் குணங்க ளுள்ளியுள்ளந் தூயராய்
சென்றுசென்று தேவதேவ ரும்பரும்ப ரும்பராய்
அன்றியெங்கள் செங்கண்மாலை யாவர்காண வல்லரே.

IAST Transliteration

oṉṟiniṉṟu naṟṟavañcey tūḻiyūḻi tōṟelām
niṉṟuniṉṟavaṉ kuṇaṅkaḷ uḷḷiyuḷḷam tūyarāy
ceṉṟuceṉṟu tēvatēvar umparumpar umparāy
aṉṟiyeṅkaḷ ceṅkaṇmālai yāvarkāṇa vallarē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Who can behold our red-lotus-eyed Māl unless, fixing themselves on Him, they remain steady, perform excellent austerity through age after age, dwell successively in the stages of hearing and reflection, contemplate His qualities until the heart becomes pure, advance again and again in meditation, become venerable even to the gods, and rise ever higher and higher in that sustained loving vision?

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. oṉṟi - placing the mind upon red-eyed Māl; niṉṟu - remaining without swerving toward another object; ūḻi ūḻi tōṟu elām - always, through many births; nal-tavam ceytu - performing karma-yoga and the like without desire for fruit. Niṉṟu niṉṟu - successively abiding in the stages of hearing (śravaṇa), reflection (manana), and mature understanding (pāṇḍitya); avaṉ kuṇaṅkaḷ uḷḷi - mentally investigating His auspicious qualities; uḷḷam tūyarāy - purifying the mind with obligatory duties as auxiliaries. Ceṉṟu ceṉṟu - repeatedly renewing meditation day by day; tēva-tēvarāy - becoming worthy of worship even by the gods, rather than, like those gods, directing devotion toward some additional fruit; umpar umpar umpar āy aṉṟi - unless one advances higher and higher, with the practice becoming suitable, deeply assimilated, extending for a long period, taking the form of love, and becoming like direct vision; eṅkaḷ ceṅkaṇ Mālai - the red-lotus-eyed Lover who bestows such grace even on us; yāvar kāṇa vallar - who otherwise can acquire the ability to behold Him? There is no other way within this bhakti-yoga discipline.

Do not imagine that by worshiping other gods for intermediate results, obtaining their vision, and then asking them for a boon, one can easily gain the vision of the Supreme Lord. To behold Him through the path of bhakti one must steadily perform karma-yoga and jñāna-yoga without selfish desire, gradually acquire fitness, remain for a long time in meditation, and bring it to completion without interruption.

For instruction, it is essential to resort to an ācārya - the sort described in the traditional expression “the Lord clad in yellow garments came as the teacher of Brahman.” Once knowledge has arisen through that path, one must enter the corresponding discipline; why then go and seek other deities? Those who are to become “gods of the gods” cannot attain that by worshiping ordinary gods. One must instead bow to the Lord of those gods and proceed step by step under Him.

“Our red-eyed Māl.” Because He is Māl, one passionately attached to His devotees, because He watches carefully to see who is holding fast to Him, and because unlike in the matter of lesser fruits He is not indifferent in this supreme matter, there is no reason to leave Him and ask another deity. His extraordinary generosity is evident from the fact that He grants the supreme abode even to destitute persons like us without demanding bhakti-yoga as a prerequisite.

One cannot object, “Then why does He not also bestow full bhakti immediately in the same way?” The very nature of that desired state prevents it. Saṃsārin jīvas are joined to weak, defective bodies. For devotion to become assimilated into such a condition, it must be produced gradually. Even Mādhava does not choose to overturn the intrinsic capacities of things arbitrarily.

Some construe the text as “the red-eyed Māl who is the God of gods.” Others take “even if they are gods of gods, [they must seek] our red-eyed Māl.” Umpar umpar umpar āy is also explained as being endowed successively with parabhakti, parajñāna, and paramabhakti. One should consider whether the “seeing” spoken of here is vision in this life or vision in the supreme abode. Vision in the supreme abode belongs to prapannas too; this is clear even in the older commentaries. The remaining details may be examined in the same way.

Pāsuram 76

The difficulties involved in practicing bhakti-yoga have just been indicated. The Gītā says, “At the end of many births, the man of knowledge takes refuge in Me.” Bhakti-yoga must therefore be cultivated over many births. Moreover, the excellent austerity consisting of karma-yoga must be completed before one can properly enter bhakti-yoga; hence the great labor already described. The Āḻvār now adds that the strenuous jñāna-yoga which precedes it is itself exceedingly difficult. Why was jñāna-yoga not separately included in the previous pāsuram? To preserve the view that even by karma-yoga alone, with the mind brought under control, the self can in some cases be directly realized. There is no corresponding view that jñāna-yoga alone, wholly without karma-yoga, is sufficient. Thus it is by no means easy to perform this arduous jñāna-yoga and ripen the senses and body so that they become fit instruments for bhakti.

Tamil Mūlam

புன்புல வழியடைத் தரக்கிலச்சினை செய்து
நன்பல வழிதிறந்து ஞானநற்சுடர் கொளீஇ
என்பிலெள்கி நெஞ்சுருகி உள்கனிந்தெழுந்ததோர்
அன்பிலன்றி யாழியானை யாவர்காண வல்லரே.

IAST Transliteration

puṉpula vaḻiyaṭait tarakkilacciṉai ceytu
naṉpula vaḻitiṟantu ñāṉanaṟcuṭar koḷīi
eṉpileḷki neñcuruki uḷkaṉinteḻuntatōr
aṉpilaṉṟi yāḻiyāṉai yāvarkāṇa vallarē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Closing the paths that lead to base objects and sealing them as with lacquer, opening the path to the excellent object, kindling through knowledge the good radiance of the self, until the bony frame grows lean, the heart melts, and the inner mind ripens - apart from the unique love that rises in this way, who can behold the Bearer of the discus?

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. puṉ-pulam - petty or inferior objects; vaḻi aṭaittu - closing the pathways of the senses that run toward them; arakku ilacciṉai ceytu - sealing them with the “lacquer” of firm determination. Nal-pulam - the excellent object; vaḻi tiṟantu - opening the pure mind, which is the path to it; ñāṉam - by jñāna-yoga; nal-cuṭar - the jīva-self, whose nature is consciousness and bliss; koḷīi - making it shine forth. Eṉpil eḷki - the skeletal frame becoming emaciated; neñcu uruki - the heart melting; uḷ kaṉintu - the mind becoming ripe; eḻuntatu ōr aṉpil aṉṟi - except through the incomparable love that then rises ever more strongly; āḻiyāṉai kāṇa yāvar vallar - who can behold the discus-bearing Lord by any other path?

“Closing the path to base objects.” One must practice the yogic limb called pratyāhāra and prevent the senses from running toward their objects. “Sealing it with lacquer.” The Upaniṣadic principle says that objects are stronger than the senses. Even when the senses have been brought under control, external objects can still pull them outward. Therefore the doorway must be sealed so it cannot reopen. If the mind and the faculty of determination are mastered, the objects can do nothing. One must repeatedly reflect on the defects present in all sense-objects, establish a firm determination to turn away, and eradicate even their residual impressions.

“Opening the path to the good object.” The mind is the pathway by which the excellent reality that must necessarily be known - the self - is seen; this is familiar from expressions such as “when the impurity of the mind has departed.” Any self can be apprehended only by a purified mind, not by the external senses.

“The good flame of knowledge.” The adjective “good” indicates that the self, whose very nature is consciousness, is favorable and blissful. Jñāna here means jñāna-yoga.

Some interpret the second line differently: making Bhagavān the object of the senses and kindling the flame of knowledge concerning the Lord’s essential nature, form, qualities, and manifestations. In the introductory remarks the earlier teachers too take this pāsuram as related to jñāna-yoga; therefore the present commentary explains the line primarily with reference to realization of the jīva. If the entire pāsuram is taken as describing the direct practice of bhakti-yoga, the alternative explanation is also suitable. Even one who practices karma-yoga alone must sit in a yogic posture and contemplate in order to see the self. Thus the first two lines may also be taken as elaborating the “excellent austerity” of the preceding verse.

The compounds containing pulam may be divided as pulam vaḻi, where pulam means an object; hence the explanation “inferior object” and “superior object.” Some divide them as pulaṉ vaḻi, “the path of the sense faculty.” In that case one may still interpret the referent as the object or, equally, as the sense organ itself.

“In the bones.” Eṉpu means bone. Il can mean the place in which it is found, hence the body. Why say “the abode of bones” rather than simply “body”? To imply that the body has become so dried through discipline that almost nothing remains but the bones. Neñcu is the heart; uḷ is the internal organ, the mind.

“In love.” The meditation born from the repeated inward recollection spoken of previously must grow until it takes the form of love. “The Bearer of the discus.” Everything may indeed become possible through the grace of the divine discus, the presiding deity of the mind; nevertheless, on the path here under discussion, time and strenuous effort are unavoidable. Thus the pāsuram establishes that direct vision of the Lord in this world is not obtained without great exertion.

Pāsuram 77

The hearers object: “If it is so difficult, how could you tell us, ‘Cherish Him and cut the knot called birth’? If you make us enter a bhakti-yoga that is beyond our capacity, not only shall we fail later to obtain that immense result, we shall also lose the sensory enjoyments available to us now.” The Āḻvār therefore turns to the path of prapatti: to reach the apparently unreachable goal, the eight-letter mantra itself is sufficient as the revealer and sustainer of the proper self-understanding.

Tamil Mūlam

எட்டுமெட்டு மெட்டுமாயொ ரேழுமேழு மேழுமாய்
எட்டுமூன்று மொன்றுமாகி நின்றவாதி தேவனை
எட்டினாய பேதமோ டிறைஞ்சிநின்றவன்பெயர்
எட்டெழுத்து மோதுவார்கள் வல்லர்வான மாளவே.

IAST Transliteration

eṭṭumeṭṭu meṭṭumāy orēḻumēḻu mēḻumāy
eṭṭumūṉṟu moṉṟumāki niṉṟavāti tēvaṉai
eṭṭiṉāya pētamōṭiṟaiñci niṉṟavaṉ peyar
eṭṭeḻuttum ōtuvārkaḷ vallar vāṉam āḷavē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

He stands as eight and eight and eight, as seven and seven and seven, as eight, as three, and as one - the Primal Lord. Those who bow to Him with the distinctions that make up the eightfold surrender, stand without seeking another means, and recite the eight syllables that are His name, are capable of ruling the supreme heaven itself.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. oru eṭṭum eṭṭum eṭṭum āy - becoming the twenty-four principles in three sets of eight; oru ēḻum ēḻum ēḻum āy - becoming the twenty-one worlds in three sevens; eṭṭu mūṉṟum oṉṟum - appearing as an eightfold division of reality, a threefold division, and one ultimate Reality; āki niṉṟa āti tēvaṉai - the Lord, cause of all, who stands in these manifold modes. Eṭṭiṉ āya pētamōṭu - with the distinctions constituted by eight; iṟaiñci niṉṟu - bowing in surrender and remaining without entering another means; avaṉ peyar - the expression that directly names Him; eṭṭu eḻuttum - the sacred Aṣṭākṣara; ōtuvārkaḷ - those who learn, recite, and repeat it; vāṉamē āḷa vallar - they are capable of ruling the supreme abode itself; Paramapada comes under their possession.

One may attain Paramapada by properly bowing to Tirumāl, Lord of both manifestations of His glory. Bhakti-yoga is sufficient even for beholding Him here only after enormous exertion. For those who have entered prapatti, the supreme abode itself becomes as though held in the hand. Therefore, since there is a fitting path by which the knot called birth may be cut, you need not be anxious.

“Eight, eight, and eight.” There is no inherent reason why the twenty-four insentient principles must be divided into three eights. The poetic purpose is to prepare for the “eight syllables” later in the verse. Yet one may also give a conceptual explanation. When one counts the five qualities beginning with sound, one can join to them the ear, nose, and tongue, which apprehend only those corresponding qualities, making one group of eight; the other sense faculties, which apprehend substance together with qualities, together with the organs of action, can supply the remaining groups. Or one may simply recall the familiar “eight prakṛtis” - primordial matter, mahat, ahaṃkāra, and the five great elements - and divide the remaining sixteen into two groups of eight for verbal elegance.

“Seven and seven and seven.” One seven consists of the upper worlds from Bhūloka to Satyaloka. A second seven consists of the subterranean regions situated one below another within the cosmic earth - Atala, Vitala, Sutala, Rasātala, Talātala, Mahātala, and Pātāla - which are enjoyment-realms of asuras, rākṣasas, and the like. The third group of seven may be the hell-realms such as Raurava, counted under the Brahma-sūtra’s indication “and seven also,” lying below the great waters beneath the entire earth. According to subdivisions, the Viṣṇu Purāṇa and other texts enumerate these in larger numbers, and some passages speak of twenty-one in total. Others interpret the three sevens as the seven islands, seven family-mountains, and seven oceans.

“Eight, three, and one.” Brahman or the totality of reality can be divided in eight ways, in three ways, and finally comprehended as one. When the enumeration is primordial prakṛti, nitya-vibhūti, time, bound selves, liberated selves, eternal selves, dharma-bhūta-jñāna, and Paramātman, there are eight. When divided according to the Upaniṣadic teaching “the experiencer, the experienced, and the impeller,” there are three: sentient, insentient, and Lord. When all are understood as modes forming the body of the Lord, Brahman qualified by all modes is the single ultimate Reality.

Alternatively, “eight and three” may denote means, while “one” denotes the one goal, in accordance with the principle that distinct alternatives can yield an equivalent result. The eight may be aṣṭāṅga-yoga as culminating in parabhakti; the three may be forms of prapatti - sva-niṣṭhā, ukti-niṣṭhā, and ācārya-niṣṭhā - or the three forms of surrender, of one’s essential self, burden, and fruit. Some also take the expression as referring to the twelve Ādityas divided as eight, three, and one, or as an allusion to the twelve-syllabled mantra.

“Bow with the distinctions made of eight.” If iṟaiñcutal is understood simply as prostration, the eight distinctions may be the eight-limbed prostration. A traditional verse describes it: placing the four limbs and the head upon the ground like a tortoise and directing the three internal faculties - mind, intellect, and self-reference - toward the Lord. Together these make eight.

Alongside that inherited explanation, another is possible. The five limbs of surrender are: resolve to act favorably (ānukūlya-saṅkalpa), abandonment of what is unfavorable (prātikūlya-varjana), great trust (mahā-viśvāsa), choosing the Lord as protector (goptṛtva-varaṇa), and helplessness (kārpaṇya). Together with the three acts of self-surrender - surrender of the self, surrender of the burden, and surrender of the fruit - the act of prapatti can be spoken of as having eight divisions. When the internal subdivisions of surrender are not separately counted, the same prapatti is described as ṣaḍaṅga-yoga, the six-limbed means. By introducing prapatti here, the Āḻvār teaches that one need not labor in the difficult path of bhakti described below.

“His name.” Because what exceeds the name is common to many mantras, the Aṣṭākṣara is called simply “the name” to show the primacy of the divine name. The verse says “having bowed” first and then adds “and the eight syllables” with the conjunction. This suggests that the Aṣṭākṣara is not here being treated as the formal mantra by which prapatti itself is performed. Rather, since knowledge of one’s essential servitude arises through the Aṣṭākṣara, the mantra is to be recited throughout life so that this knowledge of śeṣatva remains continuously present. In that understanding, the Dvaya or a similar mantra may serve as the formal prapatti-mantra. The connective particle in “the eight syllables also” can further include all scriptures concerning Bhagavān.

Earlier teachers also say that from this pāsuram onward the Āḻvār teaches Bhagavad-worship in the manner of the Itihāsas and Purāṇas, and that the next pāsuram points to the auspicious divine body required as the support of such worship.

Pāsuram 78

Do not suppose that one who has entered prapatti has no need of devotion at all. Although the parabhakti that functions as the independent means called bhakti-yoga is not required, affection, love, and attachment to Bhagavān are essential for a prapanna. This love establishes him in the state of having no other desired goal. Even if the mind is not employed in the great technical discipline of meditation required for bhakti-yoga, that love can be expressed through external service performed with body and senses, including such acts as reciting the Aṣṭākṣara. Love prevents the mind from wandering elsewhere. If such love were absent and the mind continually ran toward other objects, the person’s spiritual condition itself would change and doubt could arise whether his act of surrender had been properly performed. The Āḻvār therefore sketches the continuing state appropriate to a prapanna.

Tamil Mūlam

சோர்விலாதகாதலால் துடக்கறாமனத்தராய்
நீரராவணைக்கிடந்த நின்மலன்நலங்கழல்
ஆர்வமோடிறைஞ்சிநின்றவன்பெயரெட்டெழுத்தும்
வாரமாகவோதுவார்கள் வல்லர்வானமாளவே.

IAST Transliteration

cōrvilāta kātalāl tuṭakkaṟā maṉattarāy
nīrarāvaṇaikkiṭanta niṉmalaṉ nalaṅkaḻal
ārvamōṭiṟaiñci niṉṟavaṉ peyareṭṭeḻuttum
vāramākavōtuvārkaḷ vallar vāṉam āḷavē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

With unfailing love and a mind whose bond does not loosen, eagerly surrendering to the delightful feet of the stainless Lord who reclines upon the serpent-couch in the waters, and remaining there, those who regularly recite the eight syllables that are His name are indeed capable of ruling the supreme heaven.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. nīr arā-aṇai kiṭanta - reclining in the ocean upon the serpent that serves as His bed; niṉmalaṉ - the supremely pure Lord; nalam kaḻal - His delightful, auspicious feet; ārvamōṭu - with intense longing to obtain Him alone; iṟaiñci - performing prapatti; niṉṟu - remaining without entering another means. Cōrvu ilāta kātalāl - through love that never weakens; tuṭakku aṟā maṉattarāy - having minds whose bond with Him does not break; avaṉ peyar eṭṭeḻuttum - the sacred Aṣṭākṣara containing His divine name; vāramāka ōtuvārkaḷ - those who repeat it in a regular, ordered manner; vāṉam āḷavē vallar - they are capable even of ruling Paramapada.

For prapannas who expect mokṣa when they are released from the body, pleasure and pain still occur during the remaining lifetime as results of karmas that have already begun to bear fruit. By the principle expressed in the Lord’s words, “When I desire to show special favor to someone…,” difficulties such as loss of wealth or separation from relatives may sometimes be brought about by the Lord Himself from a disposition of grace. Blind people, even if outwardly living correctly, become resentful in such times. With ego and possessiveness they think, “It is improper that He should treat me like this,” and step by step lose their love for Him.

The Āḻvār says “with love that does not weaken” to teach the opposite: one should rejoice that evil karmas are being exhausted and remain in love. When love does not diminish, the bond too remains firm; hence “with minds whose bond does not break.” The thought also includes the opposite danger: if periods of prosperity and power arise, the mind must not run toward those things and their associated persons and possessions and thereby forget Him.

Some interpret the pāsuram not specifically of prapannas but more generally of those engaged in Bhagavad-worship. In that case, the Aṣṭākṣara is understood as one of the mantras required in the discipline of bhakti.

Pāsuram 79

Earlier the Āḻvār said, “Praise Māl who abides in the clear waves that rise and turn,” and again spoke of “the stainless One’s auspicious feet as He reclines upon the serpent-bed in the water.” The emphasis on the Lord of the milk-ocean is because, as the statement “This Nārāyaṇa, endowed with Śrī, dwells in the milk-ocean” teaches, He is the object denoted by the Dvaya, the mantra of prapatti, and because the Aṣṭākṣara contains the word Nārāyaṇa. This does not mean that only that particular form may be worshiped. For the welfare of the fourteen worlds the Lord has assumed innumerable incarnations. A person may worship the divine form in which his mind becomes firmly attached and attain liberation.

Tamil Mūlam

பத்தினோடுபத்துமாய் ஒரேழினோடொ ரொன்பதாய்ப்
பத்தினான் றிசைக்கணின்ற நாடு பெற்றநன்மையாய்
பத்தினாயதோற்றமோடு ஒராற்றன்மிக்க வாதிபால்
பத்தராமவர்க்கலாது முத்திமுற்றலாகுமே.

IAST Transliteration

pattiṉōṭupattumāy orēḻiṉōṭo roṉpatāyp
pattiṉāṉṟicaikkaṇiṉṟa nāṭu peṟṟanaṉmaiyāy
pattiṉāya tōṟṟamōṭu orāṟṟaṉmikka vātipāl
pattarāmavarkkalātu muttimuṟṟalākumē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

As ten together with ten, as one seven together with one nine, He has appeared in manifold incarnations - the good fortune obtained by the worlds that stand in the fourteen regions. Those manifestations arise through the devotion of their inhabitants, and the Primal Cause is great in His incomparable forbearance. Can liberation ever ripen for any except those who become devoted to Him?

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. pattiṉōṭu pattum āy - becoming one ten together with another ten; oru ēḻiṉōṭu oru oṉpatu āy - with another seven and another nine. Pattiṉāṉṟu isaikkaṇ niṉṟa nāṭu - the countries or worlds situated in the fourteen regions; peṟṟa naṉmai āy - being the good fortune they obtained. Pattiṉ āya tōṟṟamōṭu - appearing in incarnations brought about, as it were, by the devotees’ devotion; oru āṟṟal mikka āti-pāl - toward the Primal One who excels in unparalleled patience and forbearance; pattar āmavarkku alātu - apart from those who become His devotees; mutti muṟṟal ākumē? - can liberation become fully ripe? It cannot.

Earlier teachers take the first line as referring to the ten directions, ten guardians of the directions, seven notes beginning with niṣāda, and nine rasas beginning with śṛṅgāra. Their intention is that, because the universe is the Lord’s body, all these too may be called Him. They interpret “being the good” as “for the welfare [of the world].”

Here, however, the introduction has stated that the focus is upon incarnations. Accordingly, the first line is taken as enumerating thirty-six manifestations. The second line then says that these manifestations constitute the fortune obtained by the worlds. Naṉmaiyāy is joined with the āy in pattiṉāy. The phrase that literally expands “fourteen” is pattiṉāṉṟu isaikkaṇ niṉṟa. Since the incarnations have already been indicated in the first line, pattiṉāya in the third line should not be read as “tenfold” but as “arising from devotion” - that is, brought about through the devotion of the great persons living in those times.

Thus the third line describes incarnation as universally enjoyable, as occurring in response to the devotees’ love, and especially as displaying supreme forbearance. The point is that, compared with His transcendent condition, the incarnate state makes Him even more approachable by persons burdened with faults.

What are the thirty-six incarnations indicated in the first line? They are the same group referred to earlier in pāsuram 4 by the expression “three and thirty-six.” Sages have said, “All the forms beginning with Padmanābha are manifestations of the vibhava mode; they are counted principally as thirty-six,” and again, “The forms beginning with Padmanābha are differentiated into thirty-six divisions.” The qualification “principally” implies both that other incarnations exist and that these are being singled out because of a special importance. Therefore this does not contradict the earlier count of thirty-nine. To show the special status of the ten famous incarnations, the Āḻvār begins with a group of ten and then divides the remainder accordingly. There may be additional reasons for the precise division of thirty-six; one should accept them where appropriate.

The verse thus teaches that one may become devoted to the Lord in any of His incarnate forms. The devotion here is the common affection that belongs both to practitioners of bhakti-yoga and to prapannas, as already explained.

Pāsuram 80

Having taught that any manifestation of the Lord can grant liberation, the Āḻvār now says that attachment to the Kṛṣṇa incarnation is especially fitting. It is the incarnation that shone in the Kali age’s immediate memory, fully manifested sovereign lordship, humbled Brahmā, Rudra, Indra, and others and clarified His supremacy, became the teacher of the Gītā and taught both bhakti and prapatti to human beings, and granted mokṣa even to persons such as Dadhipāṇḍa.

Tamil Mūlam

வாசியாகி நேசமின்றி வந்தெதிர்ந்த தேனுகன்
நாசமாகி நாளுலப்ப நன்மைசேர் பனங்கனிக்கு
வீசிமேல் நிமிர்ந்ததோளி னில்லையாக்கினாய் கழற்கு
ஆசையா மவர்க்கலால் அமரராக லாகுமே.

IAST Transliteration

vāciyāki nēcamiṉṟi vantetirnta tēṉukaṉ
nācamāki nāḷulappa naṉmaicēr paṉaṅkaṉikku
vīcimēl nimirntatōḷiṉ illaiyākkiṉāy kaḻaṟku
ācaiyām avarkkalāl amararāka lākumē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Dhenuka had made the palm-grove his dwelling and, without any friendly welcome, came forth to oppose You, destroying himself and bringing his own span of life to an end. For the sake of the blessed palm fruits You whirled him and, with Your upraised arm, made him cease to be. Apart from those who possess love for the feet of that Lord, can anyone become deathless - truly liberated?

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. vāci āki - having made the palm-forest his settled residence; nēcam iṉṟi - without affection, unwilling to welcome those who came; vantu etirnta - coming forward himself to fight; Tēṉukaṉ - the ass-demon Dhenuka. Nācam āki - having lost all sound judgment and bringing about his own destruction; nāḷ ulappa - in order to terminate his own lifespan; naṉmai cēr paṉaṅ-kaṉikku - for the sake of the fortunate, supremely enjoyable palm fruits; vīci - having flung him; mēl nimirnta tōḷiṉ - with the arm then raised upward; illai ākkiṉāy - You made him nonexistent, destroyed him; kaḻaṟku ācai ām avarkku alāl - apart from those who possess love for His sacred feet; amarar ākal ākumē? - can anyone become deathless, that is, liberated?

Vāci is understood as a Tamilized form of the Sanskrit vāsin, “one who dwells.” The narrative is that Dhenukāsura had taken that palm-grove as his own dwelling and lived there with his retinue. Therefore, when the cowherd boys asked for palm fruits and Kṛṣṇa entered with Balarāma, Dhenuka opposed them. Some derive vāci from vāji, “horse.” But the horse-demon is Keśin, whereas Dhenuka is an ass. If one follows that derivation, the earlier teachers must be understood to have loosely called the ass a horse on account of some perceived similarity between the two animals.

One may also make Kṛṣṇa the agent of the verb ulappa, “bringing the lifespan to an end.” Then nācamāki must be treated as a verbal form meaning “causing destruction” or as a grammatical transformation. In the word-by-word interpretation adopted above, however, the syntax is: Dhenuka himself came forward “to bring his own days to an end.”

“Blessed palm fruits.” These were not like the wood-apples that fell through the use of Vatsāsura as a projectile; these palm fruits were specifically shaken down because the cowherd boys desired to eat them.

The received text has vīci mēl, not vīca mēl. Thus vīci may be taken as an adjectival verbal form applying to Kṛṣṇa. One could construe it as “the arm raised after throwing,” but that is less satisfactory. The phrase can also mean “throwing with the arm raised high.” Yet the Purāṇic narrative says Dhenuka was not killed after being thrown. Kṛṣṇa first seized him in His hands, whirled him around repeatedly until he died, and only then threw his corpse into the palm tree so that the fruits would fall. A Sanskrit verse says in substance: “Having seized and whirled him, when the ass had lost his life in the sky, He flung Dhenuka into that palm tree. Then many fruits fell from the palms; the falling ass caused them to fall upon the earth like a mighty wind casting down clouds.” Therefore the proper sense is: He killed Dhenuka with His upraised arm while whirling him, and afterward hurled the body toward the palms for the fruit.

“At the feet of You who made him cease to exist.” The form ākkiṉai can appear as ākkiṉāy, second-person singular, so the natural meaning is “at Your feet - You who did this.” Yet an important textual question arises. We have already said that from pāsuram 67 through pāsuram 81 the Āḻvār is primarily instructing others. Earlier teachers, though not always making the division with complete explicitness, also recognize a section of public instruction; and at the following pāsuram, seeing the imperative “be saved,” they say that the Āḻvār is “bringing the instruction to its conclusion.” In the middle of such a sequence, directly turning to the Lord and saying “You made [him perish]” is somewhat awkward. It is therefore possible that the original reading was ākkiṉāṉ, “He made,” rather than ākkiṉāy, “You made.”

This resembles the textual question in the next pāsuram over the phrase referring to Vāli’s younger brother. If ākkiṉāy is retained as the authentic reading, one must seek some other syntactic explanation. One possibility is to divide differently: ākku in āy - “the sweet mother who produced [the fruit]” - taking Kṛṣṇa as a mother because He gives the desired fruits; then “to His feet.” One should adopt whichever explanation proves most coherent with the established text and context.

Pāsuram 81

Having said that one may seek the Lord in His many vibhava incarnations manifested throughout the fourteen worlds, the Āḻvār now addresses those who wonder where such incarnations may be approached today. Since those historical manifestations are no longer presently visible, he teaches that the Lord’s arcā forms - beginning with Tiruvēṅkaṭam - are themselves the refuge.

Tamil Mūlam

கடைந்த பாற்கடற்கிடந்து காலநேமியைக் கடிந்து
உடைந்த வாலிதன் தம்பிக்கு உதவவந்து இராமனாய்
மிடைந்த வேழ்மரங்களும் அடங்கவெய்து வேங்கடம்
அடைந்த மாலபாதமே அடைந்துநாளும் உய்ம்மினோ.

IAST Transliteration

kaṭainta pāṟkaṭaṟ kiṭantu kālanēmiyai kaṭintu
uṭainta vālitaṉ tampikku utavavantu irāmaṉāy
miṭainta ēḻ maraṅkaḷum aṭaṅkaveytu vēṅkaṭam
aṭainta māla-pātamē aṭaintu nāḷum uymmiṉō.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

He reclined in the churned Milk Ocean and destroyed Kālanemi; becoming Rāma, He came to aid Vāli’s broken and refuge-less younger brother and pierced all seven closely standing trees with a single arrow. Now Māl has taken His abode at Veṅkaṭa. Take refuge in those very feet of Māl, every day, and live - be saved.

English Translation of the Commentary

Word-by-word gloss. kaṭainta pāl-kaṭal - the Milk Ocean that was churned for nectar; kiṭantu - reclining there; Kālanēmiyai kaṭintu - destroying the demon Kālanemi; Irāmaṉāy - incarnating as the son of the emperor Daśaratha; uṭainta Vāli-taṉ tampikku - for the younger brother of Vāli, Sugrīva, whose body and spirit had been broken and who had become one having no other recourse; utava vantu - coming to help him, even ascending Ṛśyamūka; miṭainta ēḻ maraṅkaḷum aṭaṅka eytu - piercing with one arrow all seven sāla trees that stood close together; Vēṅkaṭam aṭainta - now abiding permanently at Tiruvēṅkaṭam; Māla-pātamē aṭaintu - taking refuge only in the feet of Tirumāl; nāḷum uymmiṉ - live and be saved every day, or at least on whatever day you are able to reach Him.

The Lord who was reclining in the Milk Ocean arose to fulfill the request of the gods and even allowed His own dwelling-place to be disturbed for their sake. He did not reason, “If the milk is churned it will be spoiled, it will acquire an unpleasant smell, and will it still deserve the name Milk Ocean afterward?” He submitted to all the trouble of the churning. Why? Because He is Māl, one overcome by love for His dependents.

The transmitted expression concerning Vāli’s brother is difficult and has attracted several explanations. The compact wording vālitaṉtaṉukku may, through contraction, yield the sense “for Vāli’s younger brother.” Yet, if each word is separately analyzed, one may ask how tantaṉ can itself mean “younger brother.” Some read Vāli taṉ-taṉakku. Others suppose an older reading such as taṉ-piṉṉukku or taṉ piṉukku, “for the one born after Vāli.” With the reading Vāli-taṉ taṉakku, the meaning would be “for that one belonging to Vāli,” namely his brother. If tanta could mean the summit of a mountain, one might even investigate whether Sugrīva is described as “the one whose station was the peak” of Ṛśyamūka, the mountain to which the accursed Vāli could not climb. Since Sanskrit tanu can signify smallness, one may also understand a form such as Vāli-tan-tanu as “one smaller or younger than Vāli.” The dative taṉukku could then stand as atukku does in Tamil. Other readings such as vālitaṃ or vālitaṉtaṉukku have likewise been proposed. The word uṭainta, “broken,” appropriately describes Sugrīva, whose mind and body had both been shattered by his plight; indeed, Vāli himself was distressed that Sugrīva remained on Ṛśyamūka beyond his reach.

The phrase “He came to help as Rāma” should not unnaturally be transposed as “He came and, for the sake of helping, became Rāma.” The straightforward sense is that He had incarnated as Rāma and then came to Sugrīva’s aid.

“Piercing them all.” Some explain that just as He pierced the seven sāla trees, His arrow also pierced the seven great mountains and the seven subterranean regions. The evidence for that extension should be examined carefully. A celebrated Sanskrit expression, sālāṃś ca sapta sagirīn sarasātalān yaḥ, does not by itself make it completely clear that seven mountains and seven netherworlds were all separately pierced. It is therefore safest to preserve the direct reference to the seven trees.

Māla means “of Māl”; the final -a functions as a genitive marker. Aṭaintu nāḷum may be construed as “reaching Him every day.” Since the arcā form remains steadily available, it can also carry the compassionate sense: “At least on some day, reach Him!” Thus the instruction begun for the benefit of others is brought to its conclusion: when the historical incarnations are not visible, the stable arcā at Tiruvēṅkaṭam is the accessible refuge.

Pāsuram 82

Having earnestly instructed others so that they too may surrender to the Lord and attain bliss, the Āḻvār now turns again directly to the Lord. He begins to state his own certainty and to ask for what he personally desires.

Tamil Mūlam

எத்திறத்தும் ஒத்துநின்று உயர்ந்துயர்ந்த பெற்றியோய்
முத்திறத்து மூரிநீர் அராவணைத் துயின்றநின்
பத்துறுத்த சிந்தையோடு நின்றுபாசம் விட்டவர்க்கு
எத்திறத்தும் இன்பமிங்கும் அங்குமெங்கும் ஆகுமே.

IAST Transliteration

ettiṟattum ottuniṉṟu uyarntuyarnta peṟṟiyōy
muttiṟattu mūrinīr arāvaṇait tuyintaniṉ
pattuṟutta cintaiyōṭu niṉṟu pācam viṭṭavarkku
ettiṟattum iṉpam iṅkum aṅkum eṅkum ākumē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O You whose nature is to stand on equal terms in every condition and yet thereby become higher and higher! You sleep upon the serpent-bed in the ancient, mighty ocean of threefold waters. For those whose minds have been firmly formed by devotion to You and who have abandoned every other attachment, bliss arises in every way - here, there, and everywhere.

English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord’s extraordinary sauśīlya and saulabhya are indicated first. Whatever lowly birth or social condition He enters in an incarnation, He stands as though equal to those around Him. He does not merely tolerate their level from a distance. He assumes the same kind of birth, moves among them, befriends persons such as Guha and the cowherds, and conceals the majesty that would otherwise separate Him from them. Yet precisely through this condescension His excellence rises higher and higher. His greatness is not diminished by accessibility; accessibility itself becomes a still greater greatness.

Muttirattu mūri-nīr can be taken as the ocean made up of three kinds of waters - waters arising from springs, from rain, and from rivers. Mūri may mean ancient, mighty, or abundant. In that vast ocean He reclines upon the serpent couch.

The distinction between pattu and pācam is deliberate. Pattu is attachment in the noble sense - devotion or bhakti directed to the Lord. Pācam is the binding attachment that ties one to other objects. Hence “a mind in which pattu has become firm” means a mind formed and stabilized by devotion to Him; “abandoning pācam” means releasing the bonds of other goals and objects. One may recall the familiar expression describing those devotees who possess attachment to the Lord’s feet.

For such a person, Bhagavad-anubhava is bliss “in every way.” It is not restricted to one geographical place or one state of existence. “Here, there, and everywhere” may first contrast this world and the other world: the devotee tastes Him here and in Paramapada there. But an intense devotee can experience Him wherever he happens to be, because the Lord, out of love, makes Himself present where His devotee is. The wording therefore also establishes an equality between “here” and “there” so far as the essential sweetness of the Lord’s experience is concerned.

Yet the abandonment of other attachments does not arise independently. The mind stands firmly in Him because He has made Himself accessible in this astonishingly condescending way. Since His own accessibility produces the devotion that displaces worldly bonds, the resulting bliss is secure.

Pāsuram 83

The Āḻvār now compares even the imperishable bliss of heaven with the extraordinary sweetness of binding the mind to the Lord here through unwavering love.

Tamil Mūlam

மட்டுலாவு தண்டுழாய் அலங்கலாய்! புலன்கழல்
விட்டுவீழ் விலாதபோகம் விண்ணில்நண்ணியேறினும்
எட்டினோ டிரண்டெனும் கயிற்றினால் மனந்தனைக்
கட்டிவீ டிலாதுவைத்த காதலின்ப மாகுமே.

IAST Transliteration

maṭṭulāvu taṇṭuḻāy alaṅkalāy! pulaṉ-kaḻal
viṭṭu vīḻvilāta pōkam viṇṇil naṇṇi ēṟiṉum
eṭṭiṉōṭu iraṇṭeṉum kayiṟṟiṉāl maṉantaṉaik
kaṭṭi vīṭilātu vaitta kātal-iṉpam ākumē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord adorned with a cool tulasī garland in which honey and bees abound! Even if, leaving the feet that are perceptible here, one ascends to heaven and attains an enjoyment that never falls away, can it equal the bliss of love by which the mind is tied fast, never released, with the rope called “eight together with two”?

English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord’s cool tulasī garland overflows with honey, and bees immerse themselves in it. One might almost say that the bees prefer the tulasī touched by Him even to the Lord Himself. In the same manner, embodied devotees here are able to experience His accessible form with these very bodily senses and eyes. Some might nevertheless hasten toward the imperishable bliss of Paramapada. The Āḻvār asks whether even that surpasses the distinctive sweetness of holding the mind fast to Him here.

In Paramapada there is no need for these material organs, nor for the strenuous restraint of an unruly mind. Here, however, body, senses, and mind are required for worship, while those very instruments continually run toward other objects. Of them, the mind is especially difficult to control. To bind such a mind to the Lord and prevent its departure is therefore an astonishing achievement, and the love that accomplishes it has a special relish.

“Eight together with two” gives ten. The expression deliberately avoids the direct word for ten, pattu. Once the hearer arrives at pattu by calculation, the identical-sounding word pattu, “attachment, devotion,” is suggested.
