kēṭilcīr varattaṉāyk keṭumvarattu ayaṉaraṉ
nāṭiṉōṭu nāṭṭamāyirattaṉāṭu naṇṇiṉum
vīṭatāṉa pōkameyti vīṟṟirunta pōtilum
kūṭum ācai allatoṉṟu koḷvaṉō kuṟippilē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Even if I reached the realms of Brahmā and Rudra, who possess boons - some excellences imperishable and some lordly grants destined to perish - and the heaven of the thousand-eyed Indra; even if I attained the enjoyment that is mokṣa itself and sat there in sovereign glory, would my intention contain anything other than the desire to be ever joined with You?

English Translation of the Commentary

The stations of Brahmā, Rudra, and Indra are regarded as exceedingly great. Their occupants are in a double condition. On one side, their experience of the Lord can be immense. On the other, because of the offices they hold within saṃsāra, they can also become deeply entangled in delusion. Among the boons they receive from the Lord, Bhagavad-anubhava is an imperishable excellence; it grows higher and higher and can culminate in mokṣa. But the powers of creation, destruction, and cosmic administration are perishable grants tied to office.

Therefore, even if the Āḻvār were to reach those worlds, become like their rulers, or obtain a form of sāyujya with them, the attendant dangers and limitations would prevent desire for the Lord from disappearing. Then he makes the comparison even stronger: suppose he attained the very sovereignty of mokṣa, where no perishable boon remains. Even there, the Lord’s svarūpa, form, qualities, and infinite manifestations appear ever new as they are enjoyed. The more they are experienced, the more desire grows rather than becoming exhausted. This is why the tradition speaks of parabhakti, parajñāna, and paramabhakti even in relation to the ever-fresh experience of Paramapada.

The Āḻvār therefore says: “I will never stop this so-called babbling of desire. You are not a goal whose enjoyment ends by satisfying desire once and for all.” Nammāḻvār expresses the same inexhaustibility: “At every moment, through day, month, year, aeon after aeon, You are nectar that is fresh at that very moment and can never be exhausted.” The Lord is ārā-amudu, nectar from which one can never become satiated.

The grammatical distribution of “imperishable excellence” and “perishable boon” admits alternatives. Both qualifications may be applied to the offices beginning with Brahmā. Some assign the first especially to Brahmā and the second to Rudra: Brahmā’s boon is exalted, while Rudra’s power is associated with destruction and therefore is a “destroying boon.” Others say Brahmā has imperishable excellence together with the power of granting boons, while Rudra has power of destruction together with boon-bestowal. If keṭum is taken transitively, it must mean “causing destruction” rather than “itself perishing.” Certain constructions may also favor the variant varattaṉām over varattaṉāy. The singular can be explained by allowing the phrase to connect separately with the thousand-eyed Indra. The commentary deliberately records these possibilities.

Some interpreters say the verse teaches that desire for divine experience is itself sweeter than the experienced object. That raises the question: why not cultivate desire alone without ever attaining the object? The better emphasis is that the Āḻvār, who has already rejected aiśvarya and prayed to have boundless births ended, is not now returning to worldly sovereignty or disparaging mokṣa. He uses the greatest conceivable temporal sovereignty as an analogy to show that even in mokṣa the longing inherent in divine experience continues at every moment because its object is inexhaustibly new.

Nāṭṭam means eye; “the thousand-eyed one” is Indra. Since both “their worlds” and Indra’s “world” are named, the verse can be heard as gathering all three exalted destinations before surpassing them with the desire for the Lord. With this pāsuram the Āḻvār is understood to have asked the Lord for what he required for mokṣa and to have received it.

Pāsuram 109

The first 108 pāsurams have praised the Lord extensively, instructed others concerning tattva, hita, and puruṣārtha, and presented the Āḻvār’s own requests. He now begins the conclusion of the prabandha. The remaining pāsurams include prayer for pardon of faults in the hymn, consolation of the mind that has reached this stage, and description of the fruit obtained. This pāsuram marks the completion of the main stotra.

Tamil Mūlam

சுருக்குவாரை யின்றியே சுருங்கினாய் சுருங்கியும்
பெருக்குவாரை யின்றியே பெருக்கமெய்து பெற்றியோய்
செருக்குவார்கள் தீக்குணங்கள் தீர்த்த தேவதேவனென்று
இருக்குவாய் முனிக்கணங்கள் ஏத்த யானுமேத்தினேன்.

IAST Transliteration

curukkuvārai iṉṟiyē curuṅkiṉāy curuṅkiyum
perukkuvārai iṉṟiyē perukkameytu peṟṟiyōy
cerukkuvārkaḷ tī-kkuṇaṅkaḷ tīrtta tēvatēvaṉeṉṟu
irukkuvāy muṉikkaṇaṅkaḷ ētta yāṉum ēttiṉēṉ.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Without anyone or anything causing You to contract, You contracted Yourself; and even after becoming tiny, without anyone causing You to grow, You expanded beyond measure. O Lord of such nature, who removed the evil qualities of the arrogant and became the God of gods! Hosts of sages, with the Ṛks upon their lips, praise You in this way - and I too have praised You.

English Translation of the Commentary

The bodies of ordinary souls become small or great as effects of their karma. They cannot, in a fraction of a moment, become unimaginably minute and then instantly expand beyond the cosmos. The Lord, however, became the tiny Vāmana by mere resolve, and as soon as Bali gave the requested gift He grew beyond the cosmic sphere as Trivikrama. No external cause contracted or expanded Him.

By this and similar līlās He humbled the pride of persons such as Śukra, removed the harmful qualities of Bali and others, and supplied them with good qualities. He fulfilled the wishes of the gods and even granted daivī-sampad, divine disposition, to asuras; in that sense He turns all into “devas” and is therefore Devadeva, the God of gods.

Groups of sages praise this very event. The Ṛks, which are the revealed utterances associated with the seers, explicitly celebrate the strides of Trivikrama. The Āḻvār says: “The Lord whom those hosts of sages praise, I too have praised in these 108 pāsurams.”

One might ask: “Where are those great sages, and where am I?” Yet the Lord is equal in His receptivity toward all who love Him, and the Āḻvār’s love for Him is not less than theirs - indeed, from the standpoint of exclusive love it may be greater. Another interpretation is that unlike sages who may praise for a desired result, the Āḻvār praises as a self-sufficient act of service without a separate fruit. There is also a deliberate suggestion of the dignity of this Tamil Veda: the same Vedic meaning voiced in the Ṛks has been manifested in Tamil through this prabandha.

Eytu means to obtain or attain: He “attained expansion” without an external agent causing it.

Pāsuram 110

The Āḻvār now asks the Lord whom Veda-bearing sages praise to forgive whatever fault may have occurred when a lowly person such as himself dared to praise Him.

Tamil Mūlam

தூயனாயும் அன்றியும் சுரும்புலாவு தண்டுழாய்
மாய! நின்னை நாயினேன் வணங்கிவாழ்த்து மீதெல்லாம்
நீயுநின் குறிப்பினிற் பொறுத்து நல்கு வேலைநீர்
பாயலோடு பத்தர்சித்தம் மேயவேலை வண்ணனே!

IAST Transliteration

tūyaṉāyum aṉṟiyum curumpulāvu taṇṭuḻāy
māya! niṉṉai nāyiṉēṉ vaṇaṅkivāḻttum ītellām
nīyu niṉ kuṟippiṉiṟ poṟuttu nalku vēlai-nīr
pāyalōṭu pattar-cittam mēya vēlai-vaṇṇaṉē!

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O wondrous One wearing cool tulasī where bees roam! O ocean-colored Lord who, together with Your ocean couch, has made the hearts of devotees Your dwelling! All this bowing and praise that I, a dog-like one, have offered to You - sometimes in purity and sometimes otherwise - You must, in Your gracious intention, bear with and accept, and then grant Your blessing.

English Translation of the Commentary

The ocean-colored Lord departed from Paramapada, took residence in the Milk Ocean, and from there came, as it were carrying even His couch with Him, to establish Himself within the hearts of devotees. The Āḻvār was overwhelmed by this saulabhya and by the sweetness of the Lord adorned with cool tulasī. He found no leisure to calculate at every moment, “Is it formally proper for me to praise now or not? Have I fulfilled every preliminary condition?”

During the composition and recitation of praise, the many required kinds of purity may have been present at some times and absent at others. The most important purity is performance with sāttvika-tyāga, relinquishing egoistic agency and ownership. Some portions of the hymn may therefore count as proper upacāra, reverential service, while some accidental element may amount to apacāra. The Āḻvār asks: “Forgive the element of offense and grant fruit for the service. Even if association with a fault were judged to contaminate the whole, then at least regard the favorable intention behind it and grant grace on that account.”

Pāyalōṭu can be construed in more than one way. The Lord came to the couch in the Milk Ocean and also came to the devotees’ hearts. Or it may poetically suggest that when He came to the devotee He brought with Him the whole environment of His oceanic repose - shore, water, and couch. In either case the point is the startling intimacy of His indwelling presence.

The two verbs in the prayer, “bear with” and “grant,” are unpacked separately by the next two pāsurams. Pāsuram 111 explains what it means for Him to “bear with” the fault; pāsuram 112 explains the “granting” that follows.

Pāsuram 111

The preceding pāsuram asked the Lord to “bear with and grant.” This pāsuram expands the first request, “bear with.” The Āḻvār appeals to the Lord’s astonishing willingness to transform even grave hostility into an occasion for grace, while carefully restricting the principle so that it does not license deliberate wrongdoing by devotees.

Tamil Mūlam

வைதுநின்னை வல்லவா பழித்தவர்க்கு மாறில்போர்
செய்துநின்ன செற்றதீயின் வெந்தவர்க்கும் வந்துன்னை
எய்தலாகு மென்பராத லாலெம்மாய! நாயினேன்
செய்தகுற்றம் நற்றமாக வேகொள் ஞாலநாதனே.

IAST Transliteration

vaituniṉṉai vallavā paḻittavarkku māṟilpōr
ceytuniṉṉa ceṟṟatīyiṉ ventavarkkum vantuṉṉai
eytalākum eṉpar ātalāl em māya! nāyiṉēṉ
ceyta kuṟṟam naṟṟamākavē koḷ ñāla-nātaṉē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O our wondrous Lord! O Lord of the world! The sages say that even those who reviled You as forcefully as they could, and those who fought You without retreat and were burned by the fire of Your wrath, nevertheless came and attained You. Therefore regard the fault committed by this dog-like me as though it were a good act itself.

English Translation of the Commentary

Ceṟṟa tī may mean “the fire of wrath,” since ceṟṟam means anger; or ceṟṟa may be taken verbally, “the fire that destroys.”

The Lord is imagined to object: “If what you did lacked the required purity and was therefore faulty, how can I simply forgive it? If all faults are to be forgiven in this manner, would I not have to grant mokṣa to everyone in the world immediately, no matter what they do?” The Āḻvār answers that the sages themselves have established forgiveness of the faults of dependents as natural to Him.

Śiśupāla heaped false defects upon the Lord. He said that Kṛṣṇa was not a proper kṣatriya, was merely a cowherd, was unfit to marry Rukmiṇī, and was unworthy to receive the first honor at the Rājasūya. He also turned divine acts done for the grace of the cowherd women - the theft of butter and the rāsa sport - into grounds for abuse. What fruit did all that hostility finally produce? Śiśupāla was touched by the Lord’s Sudarśana and attained the Lord’s own feet. He reviled the Lord and yet reached Him.

Dantavaktra, Vidūratha, and others fought without retreat, were consumed by the Lord’s fire of wrath, and likewise reached Him. The Āḻvār argues: “If even those who deliberately abused or opposed You could be brought to You, surely You can show grace to me.”

A possible divine reply is anticipated: “Śiśupāla and the others had formerly been My devotees. Through a curse they acquired hostility toward Me. When the period of the curse ended, I reclaimed them. Because their offenses arose as the fruit of that curse, I did not assign further hellish punishments beyond the suffering already undergone. Their hostile acts themselves became the events through which the curse terminated, and in that restricted sense they functioned like favorable acts.”

The Āḻvār answers that his own case is stronger: “I entered this hymn with the good intention of offering You fitting praise. Because I call myself a dog and am ignorant, some defects contrary to purity may have entered unknowingly; fault may have become mixed with praise. If You search for a way to forgive even consciously committed offenses in the special cases just mentioned, it is all the more fitting to forgive faults that occurred unknowingly in an act undertaken for Your pleasure. Treat this praise, though defective, as a good service.”

A serious doctrinal clarification follows. Some have proposed a “tradition” according to which the Lord positively enjoys the faults of devotees, so that even deliberate transgressions committed after becoming a devotee count as virtues equivalent to scripturally prescribed good deeds; on that theory a devotee could behave however he wished and every act would produce grace. The commentator rejects this as a new and unsound doctrine, contrary both to the Āḻvār’s repeated instruction to “bow and praise” and to the earlier commentary of Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai.

Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai’s introduction explains the principle much more narrowly: a fault that arises incidentally in the course of an activity favorable to the Lord, undertaken for passing one’s time in His service, or a fault committed through inadvertence (pramāda), is to be treated as a quality. His gloss addresses the Lord as the wondrous One who has revealed astonishing qualities such as compassion and forbearance toward the offenses of dependents, and explains: “I, having no other refuge and looking only to Your hand, have accidentally committed a fault; regard even that as a quality.” Thus the doctrine is not that every fault of a devotee is acceptable, still less that the Lord takes pleasure in sin. Only a defect arising accidentally in an otherwise favorable service can, through His gracious acceptance, be counted as naṟṟam, a good act.

The expression “battle without retreat” also requires care in choosing examples. Jarāsandha and others repeatedly fought but also fled on many occasions; even if they were eventually killed through the Lord’s wrath, they do not fit the specific description and did not immediately attain Him. Bāṇāsura fought fiercely but, because Rudra and others interceded, became the object of the Lord’s mercy and was not consumed by that wrath. Dantavaktra and similar enemies fit the wording more directly.

Earlier interpretation has also used Vāli as an example. Vāli did not actually fight Rāma and had not developed prior hostility toward Him before Rāma’s arrow entered his chest; Rāma punished him for his offense rather than burning him in anger. Nevertheless scripture says, rāma-bāṇāhataḥ kṣipram āvahat paramāṃ gatim - “struck by Rāma’s arrow, he quickly attained the supreme state.” On account of that declared supreme attainment, he too can illustrate the breadth of the Lord’s grace, though he is less exact as an example of the phrase “battle without retreat.”

Pāsuram 112

By saying “regard it as good,” the Āḻvār has already implied the request “grant.” Yet the hymn itself is not the ultimate saving cause. Offering praise belongs to the servant’s nature; the Lord’s delight in it and His compassion are what grant the fruit. Because purity chiefly belongs to the mind, the Āḻvār now turns from speaking directly to the Lord and addresses his own mind.

Tamil Mūlam

வாள்களாகி நாள்கள்செல்ல நோய்மை குன்றி மூப்பெய்தி
மாளுநாள் அதாதலால் வணங்கிவாழ்த்து என்நெஞ்சமே!
ஆளதாகு நன்மையென்று நன்குணர்ந்த தன்றியும்
மீள்விலாத போகம் நல்க வேண்டுமால பாதமே.

IAST Transliteration

vāḷkaḷāki nāḷkaḷ cella nōymai kuṉṟi mūppeyti
māḷunāḷ atātalāl vaṇaṅkivāḻttu eṉ neñcamē!
āḷatāku naṉmaiyeṉṟu naṉkuṇarnta taṉṟiyum
mīḷvilāta pōkam nalka vēṇṭum māla-pātamē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O my mind! The days pass like swords cutting away life; the body loses strength through disease, reaches old age, and the day that makes one die is near. Therefore bow and praise! Understand well that this itself is the good of becoming one who belongs to Him and serves Him. And beyond that, the feet of Māl themselves intend to grant the enjoyment from which there is no return.

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār had already said, “All this bowing and praise of mine - forgive its defects and grant.” He now warns the mind not to conclude that the praise already offered is enough. One must continue bowing and praising.

Nor should the mind postpone service until the final stage of life. At that time the body will be weakened by disease, then by old age; knowledge and power of action will diminish. Childhood has already passed without mature understanding. One cannot safely say, “I shall begin before youth ends,” for the days of youth themselves move silently like swords, cutting away the remaining span of life without our noticing. Even all one’s days together are scarcely sufficient for praising Him.

Why offer such extensive praise? If it is performed with sāttvika-tyāga, without possessiveness toward the act or its result, then asking “What separate benefit will I get?” is inappropriate. Praise is itself kainkarya, the natural purpose of one who belongs to Him. That is the first meaning of “understand that it is the good of becoming one who is ruled by Him.”

Nevertheless there is also a fruit beyond the act itself. The devotee wishes to attain in Paramapada the enjoyment from which there is no return. The Lord’s feet grant that fruit. Hence, if one asks, “Why praise the feet?” the answer is: the feet themselves are the true saving means. Praise, the devotion that grows from it, and prapatti are in this perspective only vyāja-mātra, slight occasions or subordinate means; the principal upāya is the Lord’s feet themselves. Yet that does not make the prescribed response of the soul meaningless, for the fruit is not ordinarily given while the soul deliberately refuses every such favorable turning.

The emphatic particle in “the feet alone” can therefore be taken not as a mere metrical ending but as an assertion that there is no independent second principal means. At the same time the words “and besides that” show that praise has an instrumental relation, at least mediately, to the result. Scripture and tradition recognize praise, prostration, and recitation as elements of bhajana and hence as entering the discipline of the means.

If the Āḻvār is understood to lack eligibility to develop bhakti-yoga further and therefore to adopt prapatti, the praise before surrender may be what brings him to surrender, while praise after surrender can serve as expiation for later consciously committed sins and thereby remove delays to Paramapada. Another explanation is more general: praise possesses two characters - it is intrinsically a goal and service in itself, and for some persons it also functions within the means to mokṣa. The pāsuram need not insist that it is itself the Āḻvār’s independent saving cause.

Pāsuram 113

The mind might still think, “With sins as powerful as mine, how can I dare approach Him?” The Āḻvār answers that one should not imagine that the Lord can be reached without thought or effort, but neither should strong sin prevent one from boldly remembering and praising Him.

Tamil Mūlam

சலங்கலந்த செஞ்சடைக் கறுத்தகண்டன் வெண்டலை
புலன்கலங்க உண்டபாதகத்தன் வன்றுயர் கெட
அலங்கன்மார்வில் வாசநீர் கொடுத்தவன் அடுத்தசீர்
நலங்கொள்மாலை நண்ணும்வண்ணம் எண்ணுவாழி நெஞ்சமே.

IAST Transliteration

calaṅkalanta ceñcaṭaik kaṟuttakaṇṭaṉ veṇṭalai
pulaṉkalaṅka uṇṭa pātakattaṉ vaṉtuyar keṭa
alaṅkaṉmārvil vāsanīr koṭuttavaṉ aṭuttacīr
nalaṅkoḷ mālai naṇṇumvaṇṇam eṇṇuvāḻi neñcamē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O my mind, live by thinking upon the way to reach Māl, who is full of bliss and whose noble qualities attach themselves to those who seek Him - He who, to end the terrible suffering of that great sinner with red matted locks containing the Gaṅgā, dark throat, and white skull from which he begged and ate until his senses reeled, gave fragrant water from His garlanded chest.

English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord removes every kind of suffering in others. He is not merely free from suffering Himself; He is supreme bliss, and He possesses an astonishing attachment toward other beings. To “reach” such a Lord must be understood correctly. It is not that our own effort is completely unnecessary and His compassion alone acts while we refuse every appropriate response. Nor is it that His compassion is unnecessary and our independent prowess produces the result. One must know the proper mode of approach: the soul performs the slight, fitting vyāja that belongs to its dependent nature, while His compassion remains the decisive saving cause.

The description of Rudra illustrates this. “Matted hair joined with water” recalls his greatness in holding the mighty Gaṅgā in his locks. Tradition says, tīrthena mūrdhni vidhṛtena śivaḥ śivo ‘bhūt - “Śiva became auspicious by holding that sacred water upon his head.” Thus the Gaṅgā is both a sign and a cause of purification and power. His red matted hair likewise signals power. The black throat produced by drinking the poison that arose during the churning of the ocean is an effect of that power and endurance.

Yet this powerful Rudra became proud, tore off Brahmā’s head in fierce anger, and was afflicted by the sin of brahmahatyā. The flesh of the severed head disappeared until only the white skull remained stuck to him. He wandered begging food into that skull, his senses distressed, unable to find release from the terrible sin, and cried out to the cloud-colored Lord. Immediately Tirumāl caused sacred liquid to flow from His own chest, filled the skull, and freed Rudra from the affliction.

Pāsuram 42 spoke of the skull being filled with red blood. The fragrant sacred water mentioned here and that blood can be understood as two descriptions of the same divine effusion. The Harivaṃśa says in substance, tatas tena svakaṃ pārśvaṃ nakhāgreṇa vidāritam - “Then He tore open His own side with the tip of His nail.” The “side” may well be the side of the chest. Periyāḻvār too appears to remember this tradition when he joins the ideas of filling the skull and the mark upon the Lord’s chest: “He filled the skull - ah! ah! - O One with the mark upon Your chest!”

“Think the way of reaching” can mean: “Think of Him continuously in such a manner that the thought itself brings you near.” It can also mean: “Investigate and understand the means by which He is to be reached.” Both senses suit the context - sustained remembrance and correct knowledge of dependence must come together.

Pāsuram 114

The Āḻvār now explains more explicitly what “the way of reaching” means.

Tamil Mūlam

ஈனமாய வெட்டுநீக்கி ஏதமின்றி மீதுபோய்
வானமாள வல்லையேல் வணங்கிவாழ்த்து என்நெஞ்சமே
ஞானமாகி ஞாயிறாகி ஞாலமுற்றும் ஓரெயிற்று
ஏனமாயிடந்த மூர்த்தி எந்தைபாதம் எண்ணியே.

IAST Transliteration

īṉamāya veṭṭunīkki ētamiṉṟi mītupōy
vāṉamāḷa vallaiyēl vaṇaṅkivāḻttu eṉ neñcamē
ñāṉamāki ñāyiṟāki ñālamuṟṟum ōreyiṟṟu
ēṉamāyiṭanta mūrtti entai-pātam eṇṇiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O my mind, if you desire the ability to remove the eight degrading bonds, go upward without distress, and rule in the supreme heaven, then contemplate the feet of my Father and bow and praise Him - the Supreme Person who becomes saving knowledge, becomes the Sun on the upward path, and, becoming the Boar, lifted the whole earth with a single tusk.

English Translation of the Commentary

One interpretation emphasizes selfless praise: “If you wish to become capable of ruling heaven, praise Him as an act valuable in itself and understand that our own independent effort is nothing; my Father’s feet alone are the true means.” Another, following the surface syntax, says that if the mind wishes to reach Paramapada, it should meditate upon and praise the Lord’s feet. The latter sounds naturally from the verse, while other passages clarify that the acts of thinking and praising are not the independent principal cause of liberation.

The “eight degradations” admit several traditional enumerations. One list is: ignorance, karma, latent impression (vāsanā), taste or inclination (ruci), connection with prakṛti, and the three forms of suffering - ādhyātmika, ādhibhautika, and ādhidaivika. Another is the eight inner defects: desire, anger, greed, delusion, pride, jealousy, ignorance, and envy.

A third reckoning takes the gross body composed of the five elements together with merit, demerit, and wealth. Wealth is included in light of the scriptural statement: “His sons inherit his property, his friends inherit his good deeds, and his enemies inherit his evil deeds.” When liberation occurs, all such appropriated connections are relinquished.

Yet another interpretation concerns the subtle body: the five subtle elemental components, an external sensory complex, mind, and vital breath - eight in all. This is connected with Brahma-sūtra discussions summarized in statements such as “speech merges in mind, from observation and scripture,” “that mind [merges] in prāṇa,” and “it [proceeds] into the elements, according to scripture.” On this explanation the previous imagery concerned leaving the gross body, whereas here the relinquishment of the subtle body is indicated.

The commentator also notes that the Āḻvār himself soon lists eight troublesome factors: fear, bodily disease, mental distress, repeated births, danger, old age, the thought that contains these, and the body in which they are established. These too can supply the intended eight.

“Going above without distress” includes freedom from suffering on the upward arcirādi path and the effortless crossing of Virajā. The mind itself, as a material internal organ, does not literally rule Paramapada. The sense is: “O mind, my companion, if you wish to acquire for me the fitness by which I shall rule the supreme realm…”

The Lord “becomes knowledge” because saving knowledge in the forms of bhakti and prapatti is dependent upon Him and granted by Him. He “becomes the Sun” because the Sun is a principal station of the path of ascent, recalled in the expression sūryadvāreṇa te virajāḥ prayānti - “through the gate of the Sun those stainless ones proceed.” Knowledge removes inner darkness; the sun removes outer darkness. The primordial Varāha raised the whole earth so that embodied beings might once again acquire knowledge and move upward on the ordained path. Hence this Varāha is celebrated as the Lord of knowledge.

Some interpret mūrti here specifically as Para-Vāsudeva, in whom the six divine qualities are fully manifest. In every case, the feet contemplated are those of the Supreme Person who alone makes the entire ascent possible.

Pāsuram 115

One must not reason: “If the Lord is the owner of the soul and must Himself protect what belongs to Him, why should there be any need for our thinking, bowing, or praising?” The Āḻvār addresses this concern while also consoling his wavering mind.

Tamil Mūlam

அத்தனாகி யன்னையாகி யாளுமெம்பிரானுமாய்
ஒத்தொவ்வாத பல்பிறப் பொழித்துநம்மை ஆட்கொள்வான்
முத்தனார் முகுந்தனார் புகுந்துநம்முள் மேவினார்
எத்தினால் இடர்க்கடற்கிடத்தி ஏழைநெஞ்சமே.

IAST Transliteration

attaṉāki yaṉṉaiyāki yāḷum empirāṉumāy
ottovvāta palpiṟap poḻittunammai āṭkoḷvāṉ
muttaṉār mukuntaṉār pukuntunammuḷ mēviṉār
ettiṉāl iṭaṟkaṭaṟkiṭatti ēḻai neñcamē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O poor, unsteady mind! The faultless Mukunda has become our father, become our mother, and become our ruling benefactor. In order to end our many births, alike in some ways and unlike in others, and take us as His servants, He has entered within us and dwells there in love. For what reason, then, do you lie fallen in the ocean of distress?

English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord is truly our Master and intends to protect us. He wants beings to become, so far as their nature permits, like Himself in freedom from defect and fullness of knowledge. Desiring to end birth, He performs every function performed by father, mother, and teacher: He stands as the inner ruler of those external helpers, acts directly through incarnations, and produces within us the knowledge that their instruction communicates.

If He is doing all this, why has the mind remained until now in the ocean of saṃsāra? One answer is: because the appropriate means or favorable response had not yet been performed. The Lord does not simply send everyone indiscriminately to mokṣa without any relation of willing dependence; He looks for the soul’s vyāja, its small consent and turning toward Him. Bowing, praising, and surrender belong to that response. The verse therefore teaches that although His ownership and grace are primary, there remains something fitting for the dependent soul to do.

The pāsuram can also be read chiefly as consolation: “Since the Lord has entered us under every possible relation and is intent on saving us, do not grieve. He Himself will grant mokṣa.” On that reading, “father” may be explained through the maxim that the father who gives Brahman-knowledge is greater than the physical father: He produces the knowledge that becomes the means. As mother, He nourishes and increases that knowledge. As the Master who rules us, He resolves upon the kainkarya that is the fruit of knowledge. With such a Lord determined to take possession of us, the mind should remain confident: “Why do you grieve whether we shall obtain mokṣa?” - the spirit of mā śucaḥ, “do not sorrow.”

A lexical objection is considered. Muttanār might appear to mean “one who was bound and later became liberated,” which would be inappropriate for the eternally unbound Lord. But mukta can mean one from whom sins and defects are completely absent; they never approach Him. The title therefore declares His absolute faultlessness. Mukunda then states that He grants liberation to those who depend upon Him. Another interpretation hears muttanār as “one superior even to the liberated” and mukuntanār as “one superior even to the eternally free.” Either way His transcendence and saving power are maintained.

Pāsuram 116

In consoling the mind, the Āḻvār has revealed that everything required for his mokṣa is present. He now expresses inward certainty: there is no remaining obstacle, liberation is effectively in hand, and he will not return to misery in saṃsāra.

Tamil Mūlam

மாறுசெய்த வாளரக்கன் நாளுலப்ப அன்று இலங்கை
நீறுசெய்து சென்றுகொன்று வென்றிகொண்ட வீரனார்
வேறுசெய்து தம்முளென்னை வைத்திடாமையால் நமன்
கூறுசெய்து கொண்டிறந்த குற்றமெண்ண வல்லனே.

IAST Transliteration

māṟuceyta vāḷarakkaṉ nāḷulappa aṉṟu ilaṅkai
nīṟuceytu ceṉṟukoṉṟu veṉṟikoṇṭa vīraṉār
vēṟuceytu tammuḷeṉṉai vaittiṭāmaiyāl namaṉ
kūṟuceytu koṇṭiṟanta kuṟṟameṇṇa vallaṉē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

The heroic Rāma, in order to end the lifespan of the sword-bearing rākṣasa who set himself in opposition, went to Laṅkā, reduced it to ashes, slew him, and won victory. Since that Hero will not separate me from Himself and refuse to place me among His own, can Yama possibly take up the faults that have died away, divide them into classes, and calculate their punishments?

English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār’s intention is to remain favorable to the Lord. Rāvaṇa’s settled intention was the opposite: to remain perpetually hostile. He abducted the Goddess herself and acted as a direct adversary. Having worshipped another deity and obtained the sword Candrahāsa, he was filled with the arrogance, “Who can equal me?”

The Lord decided that such a Rāvaṇa should no longer continue his long life. Yet even toward him Rāma remained disposed toward grace, as expressed in the famous declaration that refuge would be granted “even if it were Rāvaṇa himself.” Rāma went all the way to Laṅkā and first destroyed what surrounded Rāvaṇa, still allowing opportunity for change. Only when Rāvaṇa continued in opposition did He kill him and complete His victory.

Will such a universally gracious Rāma abandon the Āḻvār, who seeks to be favorable? The Lord has already treated his offenses as dust cast into fire. Can Yama now take these burned remnants, inspect them layer by layer, separate them into categories, and decide which suffering should be assigned at what time? The Āḻvār speaks as though every result produced by merit or demerit that would divert him from the Lord belongs to Yama’s road and therefore to the condition he has escaped.

The final lines also admit another construction. “Because He does not make me different within His own” may mean that He does not separate me from Himself and put me elsewhere. Or tammuḷ can mean “within His divine thought”: He never thinks, “This one is an outsider unrelated to Me.” If the Lord refuses even mentally to classify the devotee as alien, Yama cannot sit in his own realm and think, “This man is a sinner whose unexpiated offense I may judge.” The sins are “dead” because remorse, expiation, surrender, and above all the Lord’s forgiveness have deprived them of the power to separate the soul from Him.

Pāsuram 117

The Āḻvār states with certainty that the Lord will remove every hostile condition and raise him to the supreme realm.

Tamil Mūlam

அச்சநோயோ டல்லல்பல் பிறப்பவாய மூப்பிவை
வைத்தசிந்தை வைத்தவாக்கை மாற்றிவானில் ஏற்றுவான்
அச்சுதன் அனந்தகீர்த்தி யாதியந்த மில்லவன்
நச்சுநாகணைக்கிடந்த நாதன் வேதகீதனே.

IAST Transliteration

accanōyōṭ allalpal piṟappavāya mūppivai
vaittacintai vaittavākkai māṟṟivāṉil ēṟṟuvāṉ
accutaṉ anantakīrtti yātiyantam illavaṉ
naccunākaṇaikkiṭanta nātaṉ vētagītaṉē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

Acyuta, of endless fame, without beginning or end, the Lord reclining upon the poisonous serpent-couch, the One sung by the Vedas - He will remove fear, bodily disease, mental affliction, many births, dangers, old age, the mind in which these are lodged, and the body that houses them all, and He will raise me to the supreme heaven.

English Translation of the Commentary

The latter half consciously gathers Vedic descriptions such as śāśvataṃ śivam acyutam - “eternal, auspicious, unfailing” - and anantam avyayaṃ kavim… - “infinite, undecaying, the seer” - and therefore calls Him “the One sung by the Vedas.” He is Acyuta because He does not let those who have sought Him slip away.

Fear is absent for one whose saving relation to Him has become established; for one who neglects the means, fear can arise along countless paths. Fear itself is the present suffering produced by anticipating future suffering. Bodily disease is distinct from allal, mental affliction. Then come repeated birth, danger or death, and old age.

The preferred reading joins piṟappu with avāyam, “many births and dangers,” where avāyam represents apāya, peril. A variant is read as though āya belongs with “these,” yielding “birth, old age, and things of this kind,” but the commentator considers the first reading superior. Cintai can stand by synecdoche for the whole complex of internal and sensory functions.

The body is the locus in which all these conditions have been placed. The Lord will remove not merely individual symptoms but the embodied condition that sustains them, and then raise the soul to Paramapada.

Thus the pāsuram declares that Śrīman Nārāyaṇa, exactly as proclaimed in the Veda, certainly frees the soul from every misfortune and carries it to the supreme realm. The syntax can also be deliberately read in sequence: “Who is the one who raises me to heaven? Acyuta… the Lord sung by the Veda.” This makes explicit that no other deity shares the decisive role. Bhakti and prapatti are ways by which His grace is received, not independent principal causes replacing Him. The verse is especially about the Āḻvār’s own assured ascent.

Pāsuram 118

The Āḻvār reveals the basis for the certainty just expressed: his mind has never turned back from the lotus feet of the Lord of Śrī.

Tamil Mūlam

சொல்லினும் தொழிற்கணும் தொடக்கறாத அன்பினும்
அல்லுநன் பகலினோடான மாலை காலையும்
வல்லிநாண் மலர்க்கிழத்தி நாதபாத போதினைப்
புல்லியுள்ளம் விள்விலாது பூண்டுமீண்ட தில்லையே.

IAST Transliteration

colliṉum toḻiṟkaṇum toṭakkaṟāta aṉpiṉum
allum naṉ pakaliṉōṭāṉa mālai kālaiyum
vallināṇ malarkkiḻatti nāta-pāta pōtiṉaip
pulliyuḷḷam viḷvilātu pūṇṭumīṇṭatu illaiyē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

In the activities of speech, in bodily action, in the love that never loses its connection, at night, throughout the good day, and at the appointed evening and morning junctions, my heart embraced the lotus feet of the Lord of the Lady who belongs to the tender lotus on the vine-like stalk; having taken those feet as its very attainment, it never loosened its hold and never turned back.

English Translation of the Commentary

The pāsuram can be read as the Āḻvār’s inward recollection, like the following verse, or as a direct statement to the Lord, like the preceding one. On the latter interpretation nāta is vocative: “O Lord!” On the former, nāta-pāta is one compound, “the feet of the Lord [of the Goddess].” This pāsuram describes the mind’s never leaving Him; the next describes the self itself joining Him without separation.

Aṉpiṉum denotes mental activity, but because that mental activity has assumed the form of love and because speech and bodily acts too are suffused by love, the Āḻvār says “love” rather than the colder term “meditation.” Love here is bhakti.

For yogins, meditation can be easier at night, when external contacts are reduced. During the day many encounters and duties create interruption. The Āḻvār therefore calls it “good day”: even during that usually distracting period, remembrance continues. Readings corresponding to “the day joined with” or “and together with the day” are both transmitted. The conjunction may be carried forward to “evening.”

Some people spend night and day in innumerable unrelated activities and remember the Lord only at the two twilight junctions. The Āḻvār says that his condition is different: his mind clings to the feet at all times. Evening and morning are mentioned because śāstra especially designates them for worship, not because remembrance is confined to them. One may also construe “evening joined to night” and “morning joined to day,” thereby covering every temporal transition. Another possible grouping says that some worship during daytime, evening, and morning and merely sleep at night, whereas he remains connected through night as well. All readings converge on uninterrupted attachment.

“Having embraced and worn them without departure” presents the feet not only as a means but as the prāpya, the attainment itself. A variant form corresponding to vīḷvu is also recited, but the required sense remains “without falling away or separating.”

Pāsuram 119

Because the Āḻvār has had Lord Raṅganātha in view from the beginning, he now addresses Him explicitly. The mind has received the Lord’s entire assistance; the individual self, purified by that help, rises out of karmic concealment, abandons everything else, and adheres to the Supreme Self.

Tamil Mūlam

பொன்னிசூழ் அரங்கமேய பூவைவண்ண மாய! கேள்.
என்னதாவி யென்னும் வல்வினையுள் கொழுந்தெழுந்து
உன்னபாத மென்னநின்ற ஒண்சுடர்க்கொழுமலர்
மன்னவந்து பூண்டுவாட்டமின்றி யெங்கும் நின்றதே.

IAST Transliteration

poṉṉicūḻ araṅkamēya pūvaivaṇṇa māya! kēḷ
eṉṉatāvi yeṉṉum valviṉaiyuḷ koḻunteḻuntu
uṉṉapātam eṉṉaniṉṟa oṇcuṭark koḻumalar
maṉṉavantu pūṇṭuvāṭṭamiṉṟi yeṅkum niṉṟatē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

O wondrous One of kāyā-flower hue who dwells in Raṅgam encircled by the Kāverī, listen! The tender shoot called my self, which lay buried within powerful karma, has risen. It came to the rich, radiant flower that stands as what is called Your sacred foot, wore it and remained in it; without any withering or contraction, it now stands everywhere in fullness.

English Translation of the Commentary

When a seed is planted, a shoot emerges. The shoot’s constituents were already present within the seed, yet they lay covered by other aspects of the seed so that their proper form was not visible. After planting, what was hidden becomes manifest. A shoot can mean a tender sprout, a young branch, and in another image even a flame.

So too the Āḻvār’s self had been enveloped by powerful merit and demerit. The Viṣṇu Purāṇa speaks of the conscious power being wrapped or covered by the force that binds the knower of the field. The self’s true nature seemed almost destroyed beneath karmic concealment. Now that soul-shoot has risen free of the body-centered covering.

It does not stop merely by emerging. It receives the fortune of reaching the Lord’s foot, the luxuriant flower of beautiful, enjoyable radiance. It comes there, abides there, and “wears” that flower as its own adornment. Thereafter it no longer withers. “Without withering” means in particular that the contraction of knowledge characteristic of bondage is gone; the self stands with its nature fully manifest in both realms, wherever the Lord’s glory is enjoyed.

In this primary construction, the āvi, the self, is the shoot. Pāsuram 118 described ananya-bhakti: the heart embraced the feet and did not depart. Here the very nature of the self is described as joining those feet in inseparable affection.

The earlier commentators preserve another construction. One can take “the self” itself rhetorically as “powerful karma,” going even beyond the confessional statement “I am the abode of his offenses” and saying, “I am nothing but a mass of sin.” Within that mass a new shoot rises: the attachment that has come to Tirumāl’s feet. First it grasps the feet; through them it takes as favorable objects the entire divine body, the Supreme Self who is the support of that body, His qualities everywhere, and the vastness of His two vibhūtis. Thus devotion spreads everywhere without withering. The commentator allows this older interpretation while preferring the former in which the self itself is the shoot.

Pāsuram 120

The Āḻvār restates to himself and to those who look to him the attainment he has just described. He makes explicit that the Lord’s own effort was the principal cause by which the soul reached Him and declares the resulting experience to be unfailing bliss.

Tamil Mūlam

இயக்கறாத பல்பிறப்பில் என்னைமாற்றி இன்றுவந்து
உயக்கொண்மேக வண்ணன் நண்ணி என்னிலாய தன்னுளே
மயக்கினான்தன் மன்னுசோதி யாதலாலென் னாவிதான்
இயக்கெலாம் அறுத்தறாத இன்பவீடு பெற்றதே.

IAST Transliteration

iyakkaṟāta palpiṟappil eṉṉaimāṟṟi iṉṟuvantu
uyakkoṇ mēkavaṇṇaṉ naṇṇi eṉṉilāya taṉṉuḷē
mayakkiṉāṉ taṉ maṉṉucōti ātalāl eṉ āvitāṉ
iyakkelām aṟuttaṟāta iṉpavīṭu peṟṟatē.

English Translation of the Pāsuram

The cloud-hued Lord who takes the soul in order to raise it had, through an unbroken succession of many births, caused me to change from one condition to another. Now He came joyfully, approached, and within His own place that abides in me He overwhelmed me with His enduring radiance. Because of this act of His, my very soul has cut away every karmic disorder that had lodged in it and has attained the liberation of bliss that can never cease.

English Translation of the Commentary

Māṟṟi recalls the expression “being born, changing again and again through many births.” Since the Lord, as inner ruler and dispenser of karma, is the one who causes the changing sequence, the verb is causative: He has made the soul pass through changing births until now. One can also interpret “change me from birth” purposively, “intending to remove me from birth,” even though the text uses the completed verbal form; Tamil sometimes permits such a construction.

Iṉṟu vantu is also debated. Some interpret iṉṟu adverbially in the sense “without a cause supplied by me,” emphasizing nirhetuka grace. The words can simply be divided “today/now, having come.” Since naṇṇi, “approaching,” follows, another attractive division is iṉṟu uvantu: “now, rejoicing, He approached.” On this reading the phrase gives the loving motive of His approach, perhaps at the point where previously unknown merit or a gracious pretext has become manifest.

The phrase eṉṉil āya taṉṉuḷē is especially subtle. Earlier teachers sometimes transpose it as taṉ nilaiya eṉ uḷḷē: “within me, who stand as His mode/body,” that is, within my heart. Nilaiya is then taken to indicate śarīra-ātma-bhāva. They say that this helps disclose the Viśiṣṭādvaita meaning of statements such as ahaṃ brahmāsmi: the self belongs to Brahman as His body or mode, not by erasing the distinction between dependent self and Supreme Self. Once such unity-through-dependence is accepted, expressions such as “His standing within me” and “my standing in Him” can both be meaningful.

Yet the words can also yield a satisfactory meaning without transposition. Scripture calls the body the “city of Brahman” and describes the heart as His palace: daharaṃ puṇḍarīkaṃ veśma; daharo ‘smin antarākāśaḥ - “the small lotus is the dwelling; within it is the small inner space.” Thus “His own inner place that is in me” can directly mean the heart. Or: “that which is established in me and supported by me” can refer to the body, within which the heart is the Lord’s chosen interior dwelling.

The Lord is present in every body as inner Self through His divine essence. But the special grace shown to the Āḻvār is more than that universal hidden presence. He also manifests the enduring radiance of His auspicious divine form and uses it to “bewilder” or enchant the Āḻvār. The phrase recalls the “inner light” cherished by yogins. The Lord’s radiance abides in human bodies without discriminating in its ontological presence between yogin and non-yogin; what is special here is that He uncovers this hidden treasure to the Āḻvār and overwhelms him with it.

Another interpretation construes the words as: “He, whose enduring radiance is His very form, mingled me within the condition belonging to Himself.” Mayakkal can mean mingling as well as deluding; accordingly “He bewildered me” can signify that He caused His radiance to unite intimately with the Āḻvār’s heart.

“Bliss-liberation” states both halves of salvation. What is unwanted is removed and does not return; what is desired is obtained and does not cease. Pāsuram 119 already said “without withering,” establishing the end of knowledge-contraction and distress. Here the positive fullness of bliss is added. Just as a Vedic sacrificer who has completed the rite may declare with certainty, “We have reached heaven; we have reached heaven,” the Āḻvār speaks with firm assurance of the liberation and endless bliss he has obtained. The prabandha therefore closes not in uncertainty but in realized confidence grounded primarily in the Lord’s own action.

Concluding Retrospect of the Commentary

The commentator now surveys the architecture of the whole prabandha.

In the first three pāsurams the Āḻvār taught the three great matters of tattva, hita, and puruṣārtha. He then described the Lord’s various divine forms, His being the cause of the creation, maintenance, and dissolution of the entire universe, His superiority to Brahmā, Śiva, and the other deities, and the impossibility of any ordinary person adequately expressing such a Lord.

Thereafter he began to relish the magnificence of the incarnations. He showed that even an apparently very small incarnation is filled with immeasurable majesty and extended the account of the Lord’s descent all the way to arcā. He spoke widely of the greatness of the Haṃsa, Kūrma, Narasiṃha, Vāmana, Kṛṣṇa, and other incarnations.

At one stage he lamented that none of those past manifestations seemed available for immediate experience. Finally he realized that the arcā form itself can be enjoyed now. Beginning with Tiruvēṅkaṭam, he took up Śrīraṅgam, Kuḍandai, and several other sacred places, describing the Lord’s standing, reclining, and sitting postures.

Seeing that the Lord had thus become supremely accessible and stood ready to grant the human goals all the way up to mokṣa, the Āḻvār was astonished that beings nevertheless drown in the ocean of the created world. He therefore undertook to instruct them. Keeping in mind the Veda, Purāṇas, and other authoritative sources, he taught at length and with great beauty: “Take refuge in the Lord of Śrī, who is superior to Brahmā and Rudra and is present in the accessible arcā form; be saved through bhakti or prapatti.”

After giving this teaching to others, he turned to submit his own certainty to the Lord. He declared that he would approach no deity apart from Him. He desired uninterrupted service in this very life; but if the obstacles inherent in embodied existence made such service impossible, he wished to attain the supreme abode and perform kainkarya there.

He therefore examined the appropriate means, reflected that he was already possessed of devotion, and asked to attain the Lord through some effective means without another birth. Having made this request, he began to conclude the prabandha. He prayed that the Lord forgive whatever offense had entered the hymn, consoled his own mind, and finally declared how he would cross the ocean of becoming, enter the ocean of supreme bliss, and shine with expanded knowledge, power, and joy.

The ability to reject contrary doctrines and to clarify in detail the truths concerning the Supreme Reality and the saving means shines with special brilliance in this Āḻvār.

Concluding Sanskrit Śloka

Original Text

अनर्घ भक्तिभावित श्रुतिस्मृति प्रकृष्ट धी-
महिष्ठ भक्तिसारसूरि सूक्तयः सुदुर्ग्रहाः ।
अथापि भव्यदेशिक प्रसत्ति दर्शिते पथि
स्थितो विमृश्य साध्विति व्यधत्त वीरराघवः ॥

IAST Transliteration

anargha-bhakti-bhāvita-śruti-smṛti-prakṛṣṭa-dhī-
mahiṣṭha-bhaktisāra-sūri-sūktayaḥ sudurgrahāḥ |
athāpi bhavya-deśika-prasatti-darśite pathi
sthito vimṛśya sādhv iti vyadhatta vīrarāghavaḥ ||

English Translation

The inspired utterances of the eminent Bhaktisāra Sūri, whose exalted intellect was steeped in priceless devotion and in Śruti and Smṛti, are exceedingly difficult to grasp. Nevertheless Vīrarāghava, standing upon the path shown through the favor of worthy teachers, reflected upon them and composed this exposition, judging it to be sound.

Colophon

Original Text

ஸ்ரீரங்கராமாநுஜ மஹாதேசிகன் திருவடியான உத்தமூர் வாத்ஸ்ய வீரராகவாசார்யன் இயற்றிய
திருச்சந்தவிருத்தப்ரபந்தரக்ஷை
முற்றிற்று.
திருமழிசையாழ்வார் திருவடிகளே சரணம்.
ஆழ்வார் எம்பெருமானார் தேசிகன் திருவடிகளே சரணம்.
சுபமஸ்து.

IAST Transliteration

śrīraṅgarāmānuja mahādēśikaṉ tiruvaṭiyāṉa uttamūr vātsya vīrarāghavācāryaṉ iyaṟṟiya
tiruccantavirutta-prabandha-rakṣai
muṟṟiṟṟu.
tirumaḻisaiyāḻvār tiruvaṭikaḷē caraṇam.
āḻvār emperumāṉār tēśikaṉ tiruvaṭikaḷē caraṇam.
śubham astu.

English Translation

Thus ends the Tirucchanda Virutta Prabandha Rakṣā composed by Uttamūr Vātsya Vīrarāghavācārya, servant at the sacred feet of Śrī Raṅgarāmānuja Mahādeśika.

The sacred feet of Tirumaḻisai Āḻvār alone are refuge.
The sacred feet of the Āḻvārs, Emperumānār, and Deśika alone are refuge.
May there be auspiciousness.
